Rigidity of U -Gibbs measures near conservative Anosov diffeomorphisms on 𝕋 3 {\mathbb{T}}^{3}
Sébastien Alvarez, Martin Leguil, Davi Obata, Bruno Santiago

Abstract
. We show that within a C 1 C^{1} -neighbourhood 𝒰 {\mathcal{U}} of the set of volume preserving Anosov diffeomorphisms on the three-torus 𝕋 3 {\mathbb{T}^{3}} which are strongly partially hyperbolic with expanding center, any f ∈ 𝒰 ∩ Diff 2 ⁡ ( 𝕋 3 ) f\in{\mathcal{U}}\cap{\operatorname{Diff}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{3})} satisfies the dichotomy: either the strong stable and unstable bundles E s E^{s} , E u E^{u} of f f are jointly integrable, or any fully supported u u -Gibbs measure of f f is SRB.



1.
Introduction

1.1.
Context

Invariant foliations play a key role in partially and uniformly hyperbolic dynamics. For example, they can be used to obtain ergodicity, topological transitivity and mixing for certain systems. In the path of trying to understand these foliations, one can investigate their topological and ergodic properties. For topological properties, one may ask about minimality, or robust minimality, of the invariant foliations. In this paper we are going to focus on understanding ergodic properties of the invariant foliations for a certain type of dynamical system.

We refer the reader to Section
2
for the definition of the dynamical objects that appear in this introduction.
Let us denote by
{\mathbb{T}^{3}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}{\mathbb{R}}^{3}/{\mathbb{Z}}^{3}
the three-dimensional torus. We let
{\mathcal{A}}^{2}({\mathbb{T}^{3}})\subset{\operatorname{Diff}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{3})}
be the set of Anosov diffeomorphisms which are strongly partially hyperbolic with uniformly expanding center, that is, a diffeomorphism
f
belongs to
{\mathcal{A}}^{2}({\mathbb{T}^{3}})
if
f
is Anosov and admits a splitting

T{\mathbb{T}^{3}}=E^{s}\oplus E^{c}\oplus E^{u},

where
E^{c}
expands uniformly under the action of
Df
. A diffeomorphism in
{\mathcal{A}}^{2}({\mathbb{T}^{3}})
can be seen as an Anosov and as a partially hyperbolic diffeomorphism. In this context the bundles
E^{s},E^{u},E^{u}\oplus E^{c},E^{s}\oplus E^{c}
and
E^{c}
integrate to invariant foliations respectively denoted by
{\mathcal{W}}^{s},{\mathcal{W}}^{u},{\mathcal{W}}^{cu},{\mathcal{W}}^{cs}
and
{\mathcal{W}}^{c}
, and called the stable, unstable, center-unstable, center-stable and center foliations (see
[
4
,
37
]
). If we see
f
as an Anosov diffeomorphism,
{\mathcal{W}}^{cu}
is a (two dimensional) unstable foliation. If we see it as a partially hyperbolic diffeomorphism,
{\mathcal{W}}^{u}
is the (one dimensional) strong-unstable foliation.

There are two types of elements of
{\mathcal{A}}^{2}({\mathbb{T}^{3}})
: the
conservative elements
, which preserve some volume (that must be ergodic by Hopf’s argument) and form a set denoted by
{\mathcal{A}}_{m}^{2}({\mathbb{T}^{3}})
, and the
dissipative ones
, which don’t. In both cases there exists a unique invariant measure which is the “most compatible” with the volume and that is called the
SRB measure
(for Sinai-Ruelle-Bowen):
measures that are absolutely continuous with respect to the Lebesgue measure along center unstable leaves (see §
2.5
). In particular, they capture the “statistical” behavior of Lebesgue-almost every point (see
[
48
]
)
. SRB measures are very important in the theory of smooth dynamics. Palis conjectured that for a typical dynamical system there are finitely many attractors, each attractor supporting a unique SRB measure and these measures capture the behavior of Lebesgue almost every point
[
36
]
. This conjecture remains open.

1.2.
Dynamics of (center)-unstable foliation

Let
f\in{\mathcal{A}}^{2}({\mathbb{T}^{3}})
with a splitting
T{\mathbb{T}^{3}}=E^{s}\oplus E^{c}\oplus E^{u}
. The dynamics of the center-unstable foliation
{\mathcal{W}}^{cu}
is very well understood. It is minimal (i.e., every leaf is dense in
{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
) and there is a unique SRB measure.

On the other hand, recall that properties of the strong-unstable foliation
{\mathcal{W}}^{u}
are especially interesting for dissipative dynamics: the study of topological and ergodic properties of attractors or quasi-attractors (which are
{\mathcal{W}}^{u}
-saturated, i.e., they contain its
{\mathcal{W}}^{u}
-leaves) is closely related to the problem of understanding properties of these foliations (or laminations). Apart from some finiteness results (see
[
10
,
21
,
23
]
), the dynamical properties of strong-unstable foliation are not well understood even in the uniformly hyperbolic setting.

For instance, it was only recently announced by Avila-Crovisier-Eskin-Potrie-Wilkinson-Zhang that
{\mathcal{W}}^{u}
is minimal for any
C^{1+\alpha}
Anosov diffeomorphism of
{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
. In higher dimensions, Avila-Crovisier-Wilkinson recently announced that
C^{1}
-openly and
C^{r}
-densely among the transitive Anosov diffeomorphisms admitting a decomposition
E^{s}\oplus E^{c}\oplus E^{u}
, where
E^{c}
is one dimensional and uniformly expanding, the strong unstable manifold is minimal.

A type of invariant measures that is associated with
{\mathcal{W}}^{u}
are the so-called
u
-Gibbs measures. A measure is
u
-Gibbs if it admits conditional measures along
{\mathcal{W}}^{u}
leaves that are absolutely continuous with respect to the Lebesgue measure of these leaves. In particular the support of such a measure is
{\mathcal{W}}^{u}
-saturated. Let us make a few remarks about
u
-Gibbs and SRB measures in our setting:

(1)

SRB measures are absolutely continuous along two dimensional objects (
{\mathcal{W}}^{cu}
leaves), while
u
-Gibbs are absolutely continuous along one dimensional objects (
{\mathcal{W}}^{u}
leaves).

(2)

SRB measures are also
u
-Gibbs measures.

(3)

In general, we don’t know when a
u
-Gibbs measure is an SRB measure.

1.3.
Main result

The goal of this paper is related to item (
3
) above. We are interested in knowing when the
u
-Gibbs property implies SRB. In other words, given a measure that is absolutely continuous along
{\mathcal{W}}^{u}
, when can we show that this measure is absolutely continuous along
{\mathcal{W}}^{cu}
?

For
{\mathcal{A}}^{2}({\mathbb{T}^{3}})
, we say that
E^{s}
and
E^{u}
are
jointly integrable
if there exists a two dimensional foliation
{\mathcal{W}}^{su}
tangent to
E^{s}\oplus E^{u}
. It is known that
C^{1}
-openly and
C^{2}
-densely in
{\mathcal{A}}^{2}({\mathbb{T}^{3}})
the directions
E^{s}
and
E^{u}
are not jointly integrable (see
[
44
]
).

Our main result is the following:

Theorem A
.

There exists an open neighbourhood
{\mathcal{U}}
of
{\mathcal{A}}^{2}_{m}({\mathbb{T}^{3}})
within
{\operatorname{Diff}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{3})}
so that for every
f\in{\mathcal{U}}
, either

(1)

E^{s}
and
E^{u}
are jointly integrable, or

(2)

any fully supported ergodic
u
-Gibbs measure
\mu
is SRB.

Remark 1.1
.

The hypothesis that the system is near a volume preserving one is used to have
C^{1}
-stable holonomies. The conclusion of Theorem
A
also holds for
f\in{\mathcal{A}}^{2}({\mathbb{T}^{3}})
with
C^{1}
-stable holonomies, see Theorem
5.10
.

Remark 1.2
.

As we mentioned before, Avila et al. announced that for any
C^{1+\alpha}
Anosov diffeomorphism in
{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
the strong unstable foliation is minimal. Since the support of any
u
-Gibbs measure is saturated by
{\mathcal{W}}^{u}
-leaves, their result would imply that the case (
2
) of Theorem
A
could be improved to every
u
-Gibbs measure is SRB. We could also apply the result announced by Avila-Crovisier-Wilkinson, that we mentioned, to obtain that open and densely in
\mathcal{U}
every
u
-Gibbs is SRB, where
\mathcal{U}
is the open set from Theorem
A
.

Let us mention one application of our result. In
[
19
]
, Gogolev, Kolmogorov and Maimon consider the linear Anosov diffeomorphism on
{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
induced by the matrix

A=\begin{bmatrix}2&1&0\\
1&2&1\\
0&1&1\end{bmatrix}.

The eigenvalues of
A
are real and approximately
0.2
,
1.55
and
3.25
. In particular, the diffeomorphism induced by this matrix belong to
{\mathcal{A}}^{2}_{m}({\mathbb{T}^{3}})
. In
[
19
]
, the authors did a numerical study for two explicit families of perturbations of
A
, one conservative and one dissipative. Their numerical study indicates that for these families of perturbations of
A
, there is a unique
u
-Gibbs measure and this measure coincides with the SRB measure. They make the following conjecture.

Conjecture

(
[
19
]
, Conjecture
1.3
)
.

For all analytic diffeomorphisms
f
in a sufficiently small neighbourhood of
A
there exists a unique
u
-Gibbs measure.

Our result gives that for any
C^{2}
-diffeomorphism in a neighbourhood of
A
, either
E^{s}
and
E^{u}
are jointly integrable or any
u
-Gibbs fully supported is SRB. If we assume Avila et al.’s result, then we would obtain that either we have joint integrability or there is only one
u
-Gibbs measure. In Section
12
we introduce Gogolev-Kolmogorov-Maimon’s conservative and dissipative families and prove that for both examples,
E^{s}
and
E^{u}
are not jointly integrable, applying a very convenient criterion of Gan-Shi
[
17
]
. That gives a theoretical explanation for their numerical study. Let us remark that in
[
19
]
the authors also make conjectures about transitivity and minimality of
{\mathcal{W}}^{u}
. In
[
45
]
, Hertz-Ures gave a positive answer to their transitivity conjecture.

1.4.
Related works and further results

One can think of a
u
-Gibbs measure as a measure that is “homogeneous” along strong unstable manifolds and an SRB measure as being “homogeneous” along entire unstable manifolds.

In a series of celebrated works, in the homogeneous setting, Ratner classified measures that are invariant by the action of a unipotent group
[
40
,
39
,
41
,
42
]
. She proved that such measures are homogeneous, i.e., they are the Haar measure of some subgroup. Observe that unipotent flows parameterize unstable manifolds of the geodesic flow on surfaces with constant negative curvature (the horocycle flow). Hence, a consequence of Ratner’s measure rigidity result is measure rigidity of the
u
-Gibbs measures of the geodesic flow on surfaces with constant negative curvature. A key idea in Ratner’s approach is the so-called polynomial drift, which allowed her to obtain extra invariance of the measure from invariance along orbits of the unipotent flow.

In
[
2
]
, Benoist-Quint introduced the idea of exponential drift to prove a measure rigidity result for stationary measures of a Zariski dense random walk on homogeneous spaces.

Outside the homogeneous setting, Eskin-Mirzakhani gave a non trivial modification of the exponential drift strategy, which is called the factorization method, to prove measure rigidity results for the action of
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
on moduli spaces
[
15
]
. Since then, these ideas were pushed to some different settings. In
[
7
]
, Brown-Hertz classified the hyperbolic stationary measures of random products of surface diffeomorphisms. Cantat-Dujardin applied Brown-Hertz’s result to classify random products of automorphisms of real and complex projective spaces
[
8
]
.

In the partially hyperbolic setting, the third author adapted Brown-Hertz’s result to obtain a rigidity result for
u
-Gibbs measures for partially hyperbolic skew products with two dimensional center
[
35
]
.

In
[
30
]
, Katz adapts the Eskin-Mirzakhani strategy for the smooth setting. He proved that for any
C^{\infty}
Anosov diffeomorphism
f
having a splitting
TM=E^{s}\oplus E^{c}\oplus E^{u}
, where
E^{c}
is one dimensional and expanding, any
u
-Gibbs measure that verifies a technical condition called
QNI
(quantified non-integrability) is SRB. Eskin-Potrie-Zhang, in an ongoing work
[
16
]
, obtained equivalent notions to QNI that are easier to work with. Their result will be used by Avila et al., in another ongoing project, to prove that for any Anosov diffeomorphism in
{\mathcal{A}}^{\infty}({\mathbb{T}^{3}})
either
E^{s}
and
E^{u}
are jointly integrable, or every
u
-Gibbs measure is SRB. All of these works are stated for
C^{\infty}
regularity, but they can be obtained for
C^{r}
regularity for
r\ggg 1
. Part of the goal of this paper is to obtain this type of measure rigidity result for
u
-Gibbs measures, but in lower regularity (in our case
C^{2}
).

1.5.
Ingredients of the proof

The first ingredient concerns the transversality condition. We replace Katz’s QNI condition by a zero-one law for angles inspired by Brown-Hertz
[
6
, Lemma 7.1]
(see also
[
2
]
). We use the fact that
stable holonomies

(H_{x,y}^{s})_{x,y}
are
C^{1}
for any diffeomorphism
f\in\mathcal{A}^{2}({\mathbb{T}^{3}})
close to a conservative one (see Lemma
2.6
); thus, for
x,y
in the same stable manifold, we can define an (unoriented) angle (see Figure
1
)

\alpha^{s}(x,y)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}\angle(DH_{x,y}^{s}E^{u}(x),E^{u}(y)).

\hskip 9.24994pt\mathcal{W}^{s}(x)
\hskip 9.24994pt\mathcal{W}^{c}(x)
\hskip 9.24994pt\mathcal{W}^{c}(y)
\alpha^{s}(x,y)
\hskip 18.49988pt\mathcal{W}^{u}(x)
\hskip 18.49988pt\mathcal{W}^{s}(z)
H_{x,y}^{s}(\mathcal{W}^{u}(x))
\mathcal{W}^{u}(y)
\color[rgb]{1,0.25,0}\mathcal{W}^{cu}(x)
\color[rgb]{1,0.25,0}\mathcal{W}^{cu}(y)
x
z
y
H^{s}_{x,y}(z)

Figure 1.
Stable holonomies and the angle function.

Note that conditional measures
\mu_{x}^{s}
on stable manifolds are not well defined. But full- and zero-measure sets for
\mu_{x}^{s}
are well defined, see §
2.4
. We can now state our zero-one law (see Theorem
4.2
for a slightly more general statement).

Theorem B

(A zero-one law for angles)
.

There exists an open neighbourhood
\mathcal{U}
of
\mathcal{A}_{m}^{2}({\mathbb{T}^{3}})
within
\mathrm{Diff}^{2}({\mathbb{T}^{3}})
such that for any
f\in\mathcal{U}
, and for any ergodic
f
-invariant measure
\mu
, the following dichotomy holds:

(1)

for
\mu
-a.e.
x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
and
\mu_{x}^{s}
-a.e.
y\in\mathcal{W}^{s}(x)
,
\alpha^{s}(x,y)=0
;

(2)

for
\mu
-a.e.
x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
and
\mu_{x}^{s}
-a.e.
y\in\mathcal{W}^{s}(x)
,
\alpha^{s}(x,y)>0
.

This theorem is stated here in terms of the angle function
\alpha^{s}
; yet, it seems possible to generalize it to a broader context (see Remark
4.4
).

The other important ingredient in our proof is the existence of
normal forms
for the dynamics along two-dimensional unstable foliations. The dynamics along unstable manifolds are simplified when they are looked at using normal forms. The theory of non-stationary normal forms has been studied quite extensively since the pioneering work of Guysinsky-Katok
[
22
]
, see for instance
[
26
,
27
,
18
]
. Katz uses the result from
[
26
]
where higher regularity is needed depending on the Lyapunov spectrum.

Yet, these results do not apply directly here, due namely to the fact that
f
is only assumed to be
C^{2}
. Using an
ad hoc
construction, based on one-dimensional normal forms along the center/unstable directions, we show:

Theorem C

(Normal forms)
.

Let
f\in\mathcal{A}^{2}({\mathbb{T}^{3}})
. Then, there exists a family
\{\Phi_{x}\}_{x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}}
of
C^{1}
diffeomorphisms
\Phi_{x}\colon{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\to\mathcal{W}^{cu}(x)
such that

(1)

f\circ\Phi_{x}=\Phi_{f(x)}\circ N_{x}
, with
N_{x}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}\begin{bmatrix}\lambda^{u}_{x}&0\\
0&\lambda^{c}_{x}\end{bmatrix}
, letting
\lambda^{*}_{x}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}\|Df(x)|_{E^{*}}\|
;

(2)

\Phi_{x}(0)=x
and
D\Phi_{x}(0)(1,0)=v^{u}(x)
,
D\Phi_{x}(0)(0,1)=v^{c}(x)
,
v^{u}(x)
, resp.
v^{c}(x)
being a unit vector in
E^{u}(x)
, resp.
E^{c}(x)
;

(3)

\Phi_{x}(\cdot)
depends continuously with the choice of
x
in the local
C^{1}
-topology
1
1

1

Uniform convergence of the function and its first derivative on compact sets.
;

(4)

\Phi_{x}
is a foliated chart for
\mathcal{W}^{u}
, i.e., for all
s\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
\Phi_{x}({\mathbb{R}}\times\{s\})=\mathcal{W}^{u}(\Phi_{x}(0,s))
, and
\Phi_{x}(\{0\}\times{\mathbb{R}})=\mathcal{W}^{c}(x)
.

Remark 1.3
.

The normal forms
\{\Phi_{x}\}_{x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}}
do not define an affine structure on the foliation
\mathcal{W}^{cu}
; yet, we will see in Section
6
certain invariance properties of these normal forms under changes of charts.

(0,0)
\Phi_{x}
\mathcal{W}^{c}(x)
\mathcal{W}^{cu}(x)
\mathcal{W}^{u}(x)
\mathbb{R}^{2}
x

Figure 2.
Theorem
C
provides non-stationary
C^{1}
linearisations of the dynamics along center unstable leaves. They send horizontal lines onto unstable manifolds.

Note that Katz also uses the
C^{\infty}
regularity many other times in his adaptation of Eskin-Mirzakhani’s factorization method for Anosov systems. For example, at some moments, he has to approximate stable/unstable manifolds by Taylor polynomials with very high degree. In our setting, Theorems
B
and
C
are the two main reasons why we are able to adapt the Eskin-Mirzakhani’s strategy in lower regularity.

We stress that, differently from previous works, we implement
Y
-configurations and a version of the factorization technique of
[
15
]
without using suspensions nor any reparametrization. We make all estimations directly with the diffeomorphism. This is possible because we can obtain uniform estimates for the drift of leaf-wise (quotient) measures along the center as well as synchronization estimates for stopping times (see §
9.3
), using only basic distortion estimates (see §
2.3
).

1.6.
Organization of the paper

This paper is organized as follows: in Section
2
we introduce the basic definitions and results we need. In Section
3
we give an outline of the proof of Theorem
A
. In Section
4
we establish a zero-one law for transversality between the bundles
E^{s}
and
E^{u}
. In Section
5
we reduce the proof of Theorem
A
to a more technical result, see Theorem
5.10
. In Section
6
we construct a non-stationary family of
C^{1}
linearisations of the dynamics restricted to center unstable manifolds and use them to construct a family of measures
\{\hat{\nu}^{c}_{x}\}_{x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}}
on the real line. We reduce the proof of our main technical result to proving that these measures are Lebesgue almost surely. In Section
8
, we explain an argument from
[
7
,
25
]
that shows that if the measures
\hat{\nu}_{x}^{c}
are “invariant” by certain affine maps for many point
x
, then the measures
\hat{\nu}_{x}^{c}
are actually the Lebesgue measure. We then explain how this is achieved by reducing the proof in proving Proposition
8.2
In Section
9
we introduce
Y
-configurations and other objects crucial for our argument. In Section
10
we introduce matched
Y
-configurations and in Section
11
we complete the proof.

2.
Partially hyperbolic diffeomorphisms with expanding center

In this section we introduce the class of dynamical systems we work with as well as the main objects, taking the opportunity to fix notations and recall important basic facts.

2.1.
Definitions

For any integer
r\geq 1
, we let
{\mathcal{PH}}^{r}({\mathbb{T}^{3}})
be the set of all
C^{r}

(strongly) partially hyperbolic diffeomorphisms
of
{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
with one-dimensional stable/center/unstable bundles, i.e., the diffeomorphisms
f\colon{\mathbb{T}^{3}}\to{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
such that there exist a continuous splitting of
T{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
into
Df
-invariant line bundles,

T{\mathbb{T}^{3}}=E^{s}\oplus E^{c}\oplus E^{u},

as well as a Riemannian metric
\|.\|

adapted to this splitting
such that the functions

x\mapsto\lambda_{x}^{*}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}\|Df(x)|_{E^{*}}\|,\quad*\in\{s,c,u\},

are continuous and satisfy
\lambda_{x}^{s}<1<\lambda_{x}^{u}
and
\lambda_{x}^{s}<\lambda_{x}^{c}<\lambda_{x}^{u}
, for all
x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
. We also let
E^{cs}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}E^{c}\oplus E^{s}
, resp.
E^{cu}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}E^{c}\oplus E^{u}
be the center-stable, resp. center-unstable subbundle, and set
E^{su}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}E^{s}\oplus E^{u}
. We refer to Katok-Hasselblatt’s book
[
28
]
for more details.

2.1.1.
Anosov diffeomorphisms with uniformly expanding center

We denote by
\mathcal{A}^{r}({\mathbb{T}^{3}})\subset{\mathcal{PH}}^{r}({\mathbb{T}^{3}})
the subset consisting of partially hyperbolic diffeomorphisms
f\in{\mathcal{PH}}^{r}({\mathbb{T}^{3}})
with uniformly expanding center, i.e., such that
\lambda_{x}^{c}>1
, for all
x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
; in particular, any such diffeomorphism
f
is
Anosov
, for the hyperbolic splitting
E^{s}\oplus E^{cu}
. We also denote by
\mathcal{A}_{m}^{r}({\mathbb{T}^{3}})\subset\mathcal{A}^{r}({\mathbb{T}^{3}})
the subset made of
conservative
Anosov diffeomorphisms (i.e., that preserve some volume).

To simplify the exposition, we assume that the bundles
E^{*}
are orientable and that
f
preserves their orientation (this can always be achieved by taking an orientable cover and considering powers of
f
). In particular, there are unitary vector fields
x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}\mapsto v^{*}(x)\in E^{*}(x)
such that

Df(x)v^{*}(x)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}\lambda^{*}_{x}v^{*}(f(x)).

2.1.2.
Notations for orbits and derivatives

To give a more friendly aspect of some long calculations we shall make, we introduce the following notation. For a point
x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
we denote

(1)

x_{n}=f^{n}(x)\>\>\>\>\textrm{for}\>\>\>n\in{\mathbb{Z}}.

Also, for
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}
and for
*=s,c,u
we denote the derivative of
f
in restriction to the bundle
E^{*}
by

(2)

\lambda^{*}_{x}(n)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}\|Df^{n}(x)|_{E^{*}}\|.

The following
cocycle property
follows from the chain rule and the fact that the bundles are one-dimensional:

(3)

\lambda^{*}_{x}(n+m)=\lambda^{*}_{f^{n}(x)}(m)\lambda^{*}_{x}(n),\>\>\>*\in\{s,c,u\}.

The following quantities associated to
f
will be useful for crude estimations

\|Df\|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}\max\{\|Df(x)v\|:x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}},v\in T_{x}{\mathbb{T}^{3}},\|v\|=1\}

and

m(Df)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}\min\{\|Df(x)v\|:x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}},v\in T_{x}{\mathbb{T}^{3}},\|v\|=1\}.

2.1.3.
Adapted metric and hyperbolic estimates

The following quantity will play a key role later when we introduce stopping times and
Y
-configurations:

(4)

d^{\ell}_{x}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}\frac{\lambda^{c}_{x_{-\ell}}(\ell)}{\lambda^{u}_{x_{-\ell}}(\ell)}.

Notice that
d^{\ell}_{x}
measures the amount of projective hyperbolicity we have for the dominated splitting
E^{c}\oplus E^{u}
.

We fix a Riemannian metric on
{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
and constants
\chi^{*}_{j}\in{\mathbb{R}}
, for
*\in\{c,s,u,d\}
and
j=1,2
such that the following holds:

(1)

\chi_{1}^{d}<\chi_{2}^{d}<0
and
e^{\chi_{1}^{d}\ell}<d^{\ell}_{x}<e^{\chi^{d}_{2}\ell}
, for every
x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
and every
\ell\in{\mathbb{Z}}
.

(2)

\chi_{1}^{s}<\chi_{2}^{s}<0
and
e^{\chi_{1}^{s}\ell}<\lambda^{s}_{x}(\ell)<e^{\chi^{s}_{2}\ell}
, for every
x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
and every
\ell\in{\mathbb{Z}}
.

(3)

\chi_{1}^{c}>\chi_{2}^{c}>0
and
e^{\chi_{1}^{c}\ell}>\lambda^{c}_{x}(\ell)>e^{\chi^{c}_{2}\ell}
, for every
x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
and every
\ell\in{\mathbb{Z}}
.

(4)

\chi_{1}^{u}>\chi_{2}^{u}>0
and
e^{\chi_{1}^{u}\ell}>\lambda^{u}_{x}(\ell)>e^{\chi^{u}_{2}\ell}
, for every
x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
and every
\ell\in{\mathbb{Z}}
.

2.1.4.
Invariant manifolds

Let
r\geq 1
, and let
f\in\mathcal{A}^{r}({\mathbb{T}^{3}})
.
It is well-known (see
[
24
]
) that the strong bundles
E^{s}
and
E^{u}
are uniquely integrable to
f
-invariant continuous foliations with
C^{r}
-leaves
{\mathcal{W}}^{s}_{f}={\mathcal{W}}^{s}
and
{\mathcal{W}}^{u}_{f}={\mathcal{W}}^{u}
respectively, called the
strong stable
and
strong unstable
foliations. Since the splitting
E^{s}\oplus E^{cu}
is Anosov, the center-unstable bundle
E^{cu}
also integrates uniquely to an
f
-invariant continuous foliation
{\mathcal{W}}^{cu}_{f}={\mathcal{W}}^{cu}
, called the
center-unstable
foliation. For any
x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
and
*=s,u,cu
, we denote by
{\mathcal{W}}^{*}(x)
the leaf of
{\mathcal{W}}^{*}
through
x
; it is an immersed
C^{r}
manifold.

We now define the concept of
joint integrability
which appear in the statement of Theorem
A

Definition 2.1

(Joint integrability)
.

We say that
f\in{\mathcal{A}}^{r}({\mathbb{T}^{3}})
is (or that the bundles
E^{s}
and
E^{u}
are)
jointly integrable
if the bundle
E^{s}\oplus E^{u}
integrates to a continuous foliation with
C^{1}
leaves.

Let
r\geq 1
, and fix a
C^{r}
Anosov diffeomorphism
f\in\mathcal{A}^{r}({\mathbb{T}^{3}})
. By Corollary 1.3 in
[
34
]
the non-wandering set
\Omega(f)
of
f
is equal to
\Omega(f)={\mathbb{T}^{3}}
, and
f
is topologically conjugated to a
hyperbolic toral automorphism
. As a consequence one has that

Lemma 2.2
.

The stable foliation of
f
is minimal, i.e., each leaf of
\mathcal{W}^{s}
is dense in
{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
.

2.1.5.
Dynamical coherence

As remarked before, any diffeomorphism
f\in{\mathcal{A}}^{r}({\mathbb{T}^{3}})
can be seen either as a strongly partially hyperbolic diffeomorphism with respect to the splitting
E^{s}\oplus E^{c}\oplus E^{u}
and an Anosov diffeomorphism with respect to the splitting
E^{s}\oplus E^{cu}
. By Lemma
2.2
we know that
\Omega(f)={\mathbb{T}^{3}}
.

By the results of Potrie
[
37
]
and Brin-Burago-Ivanov
[
4
]
on partially hyperbolic diffeomorphisms of
{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
, we have that
f
is
dynamically coherent
. In particular,
E^{cs}
is also integrable to an
f
-invariant continuous foliation
{\mathcal{W}}^{cs}_{f}={\mathcal{W}}^{cs}
, called the
center-stable foliation
. Moreover,
{\mathcal{W}}^{s}
subfoliates
{\mathcal{W}}^{cs}
, while
{\mathcal{W}}^{u}
subfoliates
{\mathcal{W}}^{cu}
, and the collection of all leaves
{\mathcal{W}}^{c}(x)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}{\mathcal{W}}^{cs}(x)\cap{\mathcal{W}}^{cu}(x)
,
x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
, forms a foliation
{\mathcal{W}}^{c}_{f}={\mathcal{W}}^{c}
, called the
center foliation
, which integrates
E^{c}
, and subfoliates both
{\mathcal{W}}^{cs}
and
{\mathcal{W}}^{cu}
. For
*\in\{u,c,s,cu,cs\}
, let
d_{*}
be the leaf-wise distance, and for
x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
,
\sigma>0
, set
{\mathcal{W}}_{\sigma}^{*}(x)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}\{y\in{\mathcal{W}}^{*}(x)\ |\ d_{*}(x,y)<\sigma\}
.

In our Anosov case we can show rather easily that the foliations
{\mathcal{W}}^{c}
and
{\mathcal{W}}^{u}
are globally transverse inside each
{\mathcal{W}}^{cu}
leaf, as the two lemmas below demonstrate.

Lemma 2.3
.

For any
x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
, it holds
{\mathcal{W}}^{cu}(x)=\cup_{y\in{\mathcal{W}}^{c}(x)}{\mathcal{W}}^{u}(y)
.

Proof.

Let
z\in{\mathcal{W}}^{cu}(x)
be arbitrary. We need to show that
{\mathcal{W}}^{c}(x)\cap{\mathcal{W}}^{u}(z)\neq\emptyset
. Since the bundles
E^{c}
and
E^{u}
are integrable and the local leaves have uniform size due to hyperbolicity, since the splitting
E^{c}\oplus E^{u}
is dominated and backwards iteration under
f
contracts distances uniformly along
{\mathcal{W}}^{cu}
we must have some
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that

{\mathcal{W}}^{c}(f^{-n}(x))\cap{\mathcal{W}}^{u}(f^{-n}(z))\neq\emptyset,

with transverse intersection. By forward iteration and using that integral manifolds are invariant by the dynamics we obtain the conclusion of the lemma.
∎

Lemma 2.4
.

For every
y\in{\mathcal{W}}^{c}(x)
, it holds
{\mathcal{W}}^{c}(x)\cap{\mathcal{W}}^{u}(y)=\{y\}
.

{\mathcal{W}}^{c}(x)
{\mathcal{W}}^{u}(y)
\bullet
\bullet
y
y^{\prime}
\bullet
\bullet
f^{-n}(y)
f^{-n}(y^{\prime})
f^{-n}

Figure 3.
Proof of Lemma
2.4
: iterating the picture of the left we arrive at a small scale where the picture violates the uniformly positive angle between
E^{c}
and
E^{u}
.

Proof.

Assume by contradiction the existence of a point
y^{\prime}\neq y
in
{\mathcal{W}}^{c}(x)\cap{\mathcal{W}}^{u}(y)
. Consider the piece
\gamma^{u}
of unstable manifold joining
y
to
y^{\prime}
. Since the length of
f^{-n}(\gamma^{u})
decreases exponentially there exists some
n
such that the curve
f^{-n}(\gamma^{u})
is entirely contained in a coordinate chart for which the line field
E^{c}
is almost vertical. Since this curve joins the points
f^{-n}(y)
and
f^{-n}(y^{\prime})
which belong to the same local integral curve of
E^{c}
, this proves that the tangent space of
f^{-n}(\gamma^{u})
is almost vertical somewhere. This contradicts the dominated splitting
E^{c}\oplus E^{u}
and completes the proof.
∎

2.1.6.
Holonomies

Let
x_{1},x_{2}\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
be two
stably connected
points, i.e., such that
x_{2}\in\mathcal{W}^{s}(x_{1})
. Set
r\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}d_{s}(x_{1},x_{2})
. By transversality, for any sufficiently small
\varepsilon>0
, there is
\sigma_{1}>0
such that for any point
y_{1}\in\mathcal{W}^{cu}_{\sigma_{1}}(x_{1})
, there exists a unique point
y_{2}\in\mathcal{W}^{s}(y_{1})\cap\mathcal{W}^{cu}_{1}(x_{2})
with
d_{s}(y_{1},y_{2})\in(r-\varepsilon,r+\varepsilon)
.
We denote
H_{x_{1},x_{2}}^{s}(y_{1})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}y_{2}
.
Thus, we get a well defined local homeomorphism

H_{x_{1},x_{2}}^{s}\colon{\mathcal{C}}_{1}\to{\mathcal{C}}_{2},

from a neighbourhood
{\mathcal{C}}_{1}
of
x_{1}
within
\mathcal{W}^{cu}(x_{1})
to a neighbourhood
{\mathcal{C}}_{2}
of
x_{2}
within
\mathcal{W}^{cu}(x_{2})
,
called the (local)
stable holonomy map
between
{\mathcal{C}}_{1}
and
{\mathcal{C}}_{2}
.
Holonomies
H^{u},H^{cs},H^{cu}
along
\mathcal{W}^{u}
,
\mathcal{W}^{cs}
,
\mathcal{W}^{cu}
are defined in a similar way.

2.2.
Regularity of extreme bundles and holonomy maps

It is crucial to our proof that certain holonomy maps are of class
C^{1}
. This is the case when some
bunching
inequalities are satisfied between the rates of contraction/expansion of the system; they actually hold in a neighbourhood of volume preserving Anosov diffeomorphisms of
{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
, which is the main motivation behind this assumption in our result.

In our setting, we will say that
f
is
bunched
if there exists some
n\in\mathbb{N}
such that for any
x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
,

(5)

\lambda^{s}_{f,x}(n)<\frac{\lambda^{c}_{f,x}(n)}{\lambda^{u}_{f,x}(n)}.

In the equation above, we explicited the dependence of the contraction and expansion rates on
f
. We remark that this is clearly a
C^{1}
-open condition. This definition appears in
[
38
]
(see also
[
9
, §4.7]
).

2.2.1.
Regularity of the unstable bundle

Let
f\in{\mathcal{A}}^{2}({\mathbb{T}^{3}})
. Then,
f
is a strongly partially hyperbolic diffeomorphism with one-dimensional center bundle, hence
f
is automatically center bunched. By
[
38
, Theorem B]
, the unstable bundle
E^{u}
is
C^{1}
when restricted to any center unstable leaf
{\mathcal{W}}^{cu}(x)
. In particular, the vector field
v^{u}|_{{\mathcal{W}}^{cu}}
is a
C^{1}
vector field over the immersed submanifold
{\mathcal{W}}^{cu}(x)
and its
C^{1}
norm depends continuously with respect to
x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
.

As a corollary, we obtain that for any two small and nearby center curves
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}\subset{\mathcal{W}}^{cu}(x)
, in the same center unstable leaf, the unstable holonomy map
H^{u}\colon\gamma_{1}\to\gamma_{2}
is
C^{1}
. This regularity will play a role in our argument in Section
10.4
. Moreover, for some estimations in our proof it is important to quantify the Lipschitz constant of these unstable holonomy maps, as in the following result, which is simply a more precise statement of
[
38
, Theorem B]
in our case.

Lemma 2.5
.

Let
f\in{\mathcal{A}}^{2}({\mathbb{T}^{3}})
. There exists
\rho_{0}>0,C^{u}>0
such that for every
x,y\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
, if
x\in{\mathcal{W}}^{u}_{2}(y)
then the unstable holonomy
H^{u}_{x,y}
between local center manifolds is defined over
{\mathcal{W}}^{c}_{\rho_{0}}(x)
and for every
z,z^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{W}}^{c}_{\rho_{0}}(x)
we have

d(H^{u}_{x,y}(z),H^{u}_{x,y}(z^{\prime}))\leq C^{u}d(z,z^{\prime}).

2.2.2.
Regularity of the stable bundle

For the bundle
E^{s}
a stronger statement can be made when
f
is close to a volume preserving map. Indeed, fix arbitrarily
f_{0}\in{\mathcal{A}}_{m}^{1}({\mathbb{T}^{3}})
.

Lemma 2.6
.

There exists a neighbourhood
\mathcal{U}(f_{0})
of
f_{0}
within
\mathrm{Diff}^{2}({\mathbb{T}^{3}})
such that for any diffeomorphism
f\in\mathcal{U}(f_{0})
, we have
f\in\mathcal{A}^{2}({\mathbb{T}^{3}})
and the stable bundle
E^{s}
of
f
is of class
C^{1}
.

Proof.

Since
f_{0}
is uniformly hyperbolic, there exists a continuous function
{\mathbb{T}^{3}}\ni x\mapsto C(x)\in\mathrm{GL}(3,\mathbb{R})
such that for every point
x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
,

Df_{0}(x)=C(f_{0}(x))^{-1}\begin{bmatrix}\lambda^{s}_{f_{0},x}&0&0\\
0&\lambda^{c}_{f_{0},x}&0\\
0&0&\lambda^{u}_{f_{0},x}\end{bmatrix}C(x).

The matrix
C(x)
is a matrix with positive determinant that takes a basis formed by unit vectors in
E^{*}(x)
, and sends it to the orthogonal basis
(1,0,0)
,
(0,1,0)
and
(0,0,1)
. In particular, there exists a uniform constant
C\geq 1
such that

\max\{|\mathrm{det}\ C(x)|,|\mathrm{det}\ C(x)^{-1}|\}\leq C.

Consequently,

|\mathrm{det}\ Df_{0}(x)|=|\mathrm{det}\ C(f_{0}(x))^{-1}|\cdot|\mathrm{det}\ C(x)|\cdot\lambda^{s}_{f_{0},x}\lambda^{c}_{f_{0},x}\lambda^{u}_{f_{0},x}.

Observing that for every
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,

|\mathrm{det}\ Df_{0}(x)|=|\mathrm{det}\ C(f^{n}_{0}(x))^{-1}|\cdot|\mathrm{det}\ C(x)|\cdot\lambda^{s}_{f_{0},x}(n)\lambda^{c}_{f_{0},x}(n)\lambda^{u}_{f_{0},x}(n).

The diffeomorphism
f_{0}
is conservative so its Jacobian is a coboundary (by Liv
\check{\mathrm{s}}
ic’s Theorem
[
33
]
and
[
3
, Theorem 4.14]
). Thus, there exists a continuous function
\phi\colon{\mathbb{T}^{3}}\to(0,\infty)
bounded away from
0
and
\infty
such that for any
x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
, we have

|\mathrm{det}\ C(f_{0}(x))^{-1}|\cdot|\mathrm{det}\ C(x)|\cdot\lambda^{s}_{f_{0},x}\lambda^{c}_{f_{0},x}\lambda^{u}_{f_{0},x}=\frac{\phi\circ f(x)}{\phi(x)}.

Hence,

\displaystyle\lambda_{f_{0},x}^{s}(n)

\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda_{f_{0},x}^{s}(n)\lambda_{f_{0},x}^{c}(n)\lambda_{f_{0},x}^{u}(n)}{\lambda_{f_{0},x}^{c}(n)\lambda_{f_{0},x}^{u}(n)}

\displaystyle=|\mathrm{det}\ C(f_{0}(x))^{-1}|\cdot|\mathrm{det}\ C(x)|\cdot\frac{\phi\circ f^{n}(x)}{\phi(x)}\cdot\frac{1}{(\lambda_{f_{0},x}^{c}(n))^{2}}\frac{\lambda_{f_{0},x}^{c}(n)}{\lambda_{f_{0},x}^{u}(n)}

\displaystyle<\frac{\lambda_{f_{0},x}^{c}(n)}{\lambda_{f_{0},x}^{u}(n)},

as long as
n
is large enough so that

|\mathrm{det}\ C(f_{0}(x))^{-1}|\cdot|\mathrm{det}\ C(x)|\frac{\phi\circ f^{n}(x)}{\phi(x)}<(\lambda_{f_{0},x}^{c}(n))^{2}.

Such an integer
n
may be chosen independently of
x
because
\phi
is uniformly bounded away from
0
and
\infty
,
\max\{|\mathrm{det}\ C(x)^{-1}|,|\mathrm{det}\ C(x)|\}\leq C
and
E^{c}
is uniformly expanding. In particular, if
f\in\mathrm{Diff}^{2}({\mathbb{T}^{3}})
is sufficiently
C^{1}
-close to
f_{0}
, then
f\in\mathcal{A}^{2}({\mathbb{T}^{3}})
, and for the same choice of
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and every
x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
, it holds

\lambda_{f,x}^{s}(n)<\frac{\lambda_{f,x}^{c}(n)}{\lambda_{f,x}^{u}(n)}
and
f
verifies (
5
).
In other words, the hyperbolic splitting
E^{s}\oplus E^{cu}
of
f
is bunched (see (
5
)). Then, according to the results of
[
38
]
(see also
[
9
, Theorem 4.21]
), the stable bundle
E_{f}^{s}
is
C^{1}
, as well as the stable holonomy maps.
∎

Remark 2.7
.

As noted in the above proof, the assumption that
f
is
C^{1}
-close to a conservative diffeomorphism ensures that the following
bunching condition
is automatically satisfied (hence that stable holonomies
H^{s}
are
C^{1}
, by
[
38
]
):

(6)

\lambda_{f,x}^{s}(n)<\frac{\lambda_{f,x}^{c}(n)}{\lambda_{f,x}^{u}(n)},\quad\forall\,x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}.

In other words, (
6
) means that the lack of conformality of
Df
along
E^{cu}
is dominated by the contraction along
E^{s}
. In particular, the conclusion of Theorem
A
holds true for any Anosov diffeomorphism
f\in\mathcal{A}^{2}({\mathbb{T}^{3}})
satisfying (
6
).

2.2.3.
Hölder regularity of
cs
-holonomies

Despite the above regularity results, a substantial source of technical difficulties for our strategy comes from the absence of smoothness for
center stable holonomies
. The quest for overcoming this is the main reason behind our
matching argument for
Y
-configurations
in Sections
10
and
11.3.2
. In our setting, the best that can be said about center stable holonomies comes from Theorem A in
[
38
]
, which we quote below in a convenient way for our purposes.

Lemma 2.8
.

Let
f\in{\mathcal{A}}^{2}({\mathbb{T}^{3}})
. Then there exist
\rho_{0}>0
(which we can assume is the same from Lemma
2.5
),
C^{cs}>0,\theta^{cs}>0
such that for every
x,y\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
if
x\in{\mathcal{W}}^{cs}_{\rho_{0}}(y)
then the center-stable holonomy
H^{cs}_{x,y}
is defined on
{\mathcal{W}}^{u}_{2}(x)
and for every
z,z^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{W}}^{u}_{2}(x)
we have

d(H^{cs}_{x,y}(z),H^{cs}_{x,y}(z^{\prime}))\leq C^{cs}d(z,z^{\prime})^{\theta^{cs}}.

We remark that the roles of
\rho_{0}
and
2
are “exchanged” when compared with the role of these constants in Lemma
2.5
. Here, the transversal we are looking at for the center-stable holonomy has size
2
, while in Lemma
2.5
the transversal has size
\rho_{0}
.

2.3.
Basic distortion estimates

The goal of this subsection is to collect some classical distortion estimates and fix once and for all some constants which are going to be very important all along our constructions.

Lemma 2.9

(Basic distortion lemma)
.

Let
\varphi\colon{\mathbb{T}^{3}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
be a Hölder continuous function. Then, there exists a constant
C=C(\varphi)>0
such that

(a)

If
y\in{\mathcal{W}}^{s}_{1}(x)
and
n>0
or

(b)

if
f^{n}(y)\in{\mathcal{W}}^{cu}_{1}(f^{n}(x))
, for
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, then

\left|\sum_{\ell=0}^{n-1}\varphi(f^{\ell}(x))-\varphi(f^{\ell}(y))\right|\leq C.

We omit the proof as it is quite classical. Applying the lemma to the functions
\varphi=\log\|Df(.)|_{E^{*}}\|
,
*=c,u
we obtain a constant
C_{0}=C_{0}(f)\geq 1
such that if
y\in{\mathcal{W}}^{s}_{1}(x)
and
n>0
or if
f^{\ell}(y)\in{\mathcal{W}}^{cu}_{1}(f^{\ell}(x))
for every
\ell=0,\dots,n
, then

(7)

C_{0}^{-1}\leq\frac{\|Df^{n}(x)|_{E^{*}}\|}{\|Df^{n}(y)|_{E^{*}}\|}\leq C_{0}.

Moreover, up to enlarging
C_{0}
, we also have that if
y\in{\mathcal{W}}^{cu}_{1}(x)
then

(8)

C_{0}^{-1}\leq\frac{\|Df^{-n}(x)|_{E^{*}}\|}{\|Df^{-n}(y)|_{E^{*}}\|}\leq C_{0}.

for every
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
.
Another important application is obtained by considering the function
\psi(x)=\log\frac{\|Df(x)|_{E^{c}}\|}{\|Df(x)|_{E^{u}}\|}
. We can assume that the constant
C_{0}
also satisfies the following

Corollary 2.10
.

Given
\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}
recall from (
4
) that
d^{\ell}_{x}=\frac{\|Df^{\ell}(f^{-\ell}(x))|_{E^{c}}\|}{\|Df^{\ell}(f^{-\ell}(x))|_{E^{u}}\|}
. If
f^{-\ell}(y)\in{\mathcal{W}}^{s}_{1}(f^{-\ell}(x))
, then,

C_{0}^{-1}\leq\frac{d^{\ell}_{x}}{d^{\ell}_{y}}\leq C_{0}.

2.3.1.
Distortion for quadrilaterals

One of the key dynamical configuration for our strategy are the
quadrilaterals
. A
quadrilateral
is a quadruple
(x,x^{u},y,y^{u})\in({\mathbb{T}^{3}})^{4}
such that
x^{u}\in{\mathcal{W}}^{u}_{1}(x)
,
y\in{\mathcal{W}}^{s}_{1}(x)
, and
y^{u}\in{\mathcal{W}}^{u}_{1}(y)\cap{\mathcal{W}}^{cs}(x^{u})
. For such a quadrilateral, we define the point
z^{u}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}H^{s}_{x,y}(x^{u})
, so that
z^{u}\in\mathcal{W}^{s}(x^{u})\cap{\mathcal{W}}^{c}(y^{u})
.

\bullet
x^{u}
{\mathcal{W}}^{s}_{1}(x)
\bullet
x
{\mathcal{W}}^{u}_{1}(x)
z^{u}
\bullet
{\mathcal{W}}^{u}_{1}(y)
\bullet
y
\bullet
y^{u}

Figure 4.
A quadrilateral.

From our previous distortion results, we can take a larger constant
C_{0}=C_{0}(f)
in order to have the following.

Corollary 2.11
.

Now, assume that
y\in{\mathcal{W}}^{s}_{1}(x)
and that
x^{u}\in{\mathcal{W}}^{u}_{1}(x)
and
y^{u}\in{\mathcal{W}}^{u}_{1}(y)
are such that
y^{u}=H^{cs}_{x,y}(x^{u})
. Let
z^{u}=H_{x,y}^{s}(x^{u})
. If
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
satisfies
d(f^{n}(z^{u}),f^{n}(y^{u}))\leq 1
then

C_{0}^{-1}\leq\frac{\|Df^{\ell}(x^{u})|_{E^{c}}\|}{\|Df^{\ell}(y^{u})|_{E^{c}}\|}\leq C_{0},

for every
\ell=0,\dots,n
.

Proof.

The result follows from (
7
) since

\frac{\|Df^{\ell}(x^{u})|_{E^{c}}\|}{\|Df^{\ell}(y^{u})|_{E^{c}}\|}=\frac{\|Df^{\ell}(x^{u})|_{E^{c}}\|}{\|Df^{\ell}(z^{u})|_{E^{c}}\|}\times\frac{\|Df^{\ell}(z^{u})|_{E^{c}}\|}{\|Df^{\ell}(y^{u})|_{E^{c}}\|}.\qed

2.4.
Subordinate partitions and disintegrations

We move now to the
ergodic theory
of Anosov diffeomorphisms of
{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
with expanding center. Before giving the main definitions, we give some preliminaries on measurable partitions, disintegration and invariant measures.

2.4.1.
Measurable partitions

Let
\mu
be a probability measure of some standard Borel set
X
. Let
\xi_{1},\xi_{2}
be two partitions (mod
0
) of
X
into measurable subsets. Say that
\xi_{1}

is finer than
(or
refines
)
\xi_{2}
, denote
\xi_{2}\prec\xi_{1}
, if for
\mu
-a.e.
x\in X
we have
\xi_{1}(x)\subset\xi_{2}(x)
mod
0
.

The
join
of
\xi_{1}
and
\xi_{2}
is the partition defined as
\xi_{1}\vee\xi_{2}=\{\xi_{1}(x)\cap\xi_{2}(x):\,x\in X\}
.

A partition
\xi
of
X
is
measurable
whenever there exists a sequence
(\xi_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
of finite partitions of
X
by Borel subsets such that

\xi=\bigvee_{n=0}^{\infty}\xi_{n}.

Rokhlin proved the following fundamental result in
[
46
]
. The measure
\mu
can be
disintegrated
into atoms of any measurable partition
\xi
. It means that there exists a family of probability measures
\{\mu^{\xi}_{x}\}_{x}
, called a
family of conditional measures of
\mu
relative to
\xi
defined for
\mu
-a.e.
x\in X
and satisfying for
\mu
-a.e.
x\in X
:

(1)

\mu^{\xi}_{x}
is a probability measure on
X
satisfying
\mu^{\xi}_{x}(\xi(x))=1
;

(2)

if
y\in\xi(x)
then
\mu^{\xi}_{y}=\mu^{\xi}_{x}
.

Moreover for every Borel subset
A\mathop{\subset}X
,

(3)

x\mapsto\mu^{\xi}_{x}(A)
is measurable;

(4)

\mu(A)=\int\mu^{\xi}_{x}(A)\,d\mu(x).

Moreover such a family is unique modulo a null set of
\mu
.

2.4.2.
Disintegration of invariant measures

Assume now that
\mu
is invariant by some invertible measurable transformation
f\colon X\to X
. Let
\xi
be a measurable partition and, for
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, let
\xi_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}f^{n}\xi
. Let
\{\mu_{x}\}_{x}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}\{\mu_{x}^{\xi}\}_{x}
and
\{\mu_{n,x}\}_{x}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}\{\mu_{x}^{\xi_{n}}\}_{x}
be families of conditional measures of
\mu
relative to
\xi
and
\xi_{n}
respectively. The following lemma will be useful for our purposes.

Lemma 2.12
.

For every
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
\mu
-almost every
x\in X
, we have

\mu_{n,x}=f^{n}_{\ast}\mu_{f^{-n}(x)}.

Proof.

Let
\varphi\in L^{1}(X,\mu)
. We first disintegrate
\mu
along
\xi
and use twice the
f
-invariance:

\displaystyle\int\varphi(x)\,d\mu(x)

\displaystyle=\int\varphi\circ f^{n}(x)\,d\mu(x)=\int\left(\int_{\xi(x)}\varphi\circ f^{n}(y)\,d\mu_{x}(y)\right)d\mu(x)

\displaystyle=\int\left(\int_{\xi(f^{-n}(x))}\varphi\circ f^{n}(y)\,d\mu_{f^{-n}(x)}(y)\right)d\mu(x)

\displaystyle=\int\left(\int_{\xi_{n}(x)}\varphi(y)\,d\big(f^{n}_{\ast}\mu_{f^{-n}(x)}\big)(y)\right)d\mu(x).

We deduce that
\{f^{n}_{\ast}\mu_{f^{-n}(x)}\}_{x}
is a system of conditional measures of
\mu
with respect to
\xi_{n}
. The lemma follows by uniqueness
\mu
-a.e. of conditional measures.
∎

2.4.3.
Partitions subordinate to expanded and contracted foliations

Let
M
be a closed manifold,
f\colon M\to M
be a diffeomorphism of
M
, and
\mu
be an ergodic invariant measure of
f
. Assume that
{\mathcal{W}}^{+}
is a foliation invariant by
f
. Assume furthermore that it is uniformly expanded, that is
\|Df^{-1}|_{T{\mathcal{W}}^{+}}\|\leq\lambda
for some constant and
0<\lambda<1
.

A measurable partition
\xi
is
subordinate to

{\mathcal{W}}^{+}
if the following properties hold for
\mu
-a.e.
x\in M
:

(1)

\xi(x)
is a subset of
{\mathcal{W}}^{+}(x)
of diameter less than
1
;

(2)

\xi(x)
contains an open (in the internal topology) neighbourhood of
x
in
{\mathcal{W}}^{+}(x)
;

(3)

\xi\prec f^{-1}\xi
(we say that
\xi
is
increasing
);

(4)

\bigvee_{n=0}^{\infty}f^{-n}\xi
is the partition into points.

The existence of subordinate partitions was proven in
[
31
]
in a more general context (see also
[
47
]
and
[
5
, Appendix D]
).

Remark 2.13
.

Assume that
{\mathcal{W}}^{\prime}\subset{\mathcal{W}}^{+}
is an
f
-invariant subfoliation with expansion constant
0<\lambda^{\prime}\leq\lambda
and that
\xi
is subordinate to
{\mathcal{W}}^{+}
. Then it follows from the proofs given in
[
5
,
31
,
47
]
that the partition
\xi^{\prime}=\xi\vee{\mathcal{W}}^{\prime}
is subordinate to
{\mathcal{W}}^{\prime}
.

Similarly, assume
{\mathcal{W}}^{-}
is invariant and
uniformly contracted
by
f
. This means that
\|Df|_{T{\mathcal{W}}^{-}}\|<\lambda
for some
0<\lambda<1
. We say that
\xi
is
subordinate to
{\mathcal{W}}^{-}
if properties
(1)
through
(4)
above holds replacing
f
by
f^{-1}
. We then say that
\xi
is
decreasing
for
f
.

Remark 2.14
.

If
{\mathcal{W}}^{+}
(resp.
{\mathcal{W}}^{-}
) has dimension
1
then atoms of the subordinate partition constructed in
[
5
,
31
,
47
]
are intervals.

2.4.4.
Superposition property of subordinate partitions

Let
M,f,\mathcal{W}^{+}
be as in the above §
2.4.3
.
Let
\xi
be a partition subordinate to
\mathcal{W}^{+}
. Then, for every
n\geq 0
,
\xi_{-n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}f^{-n}\xi
is also a partition subordinate to
\mathcal{W}^{+}
. Since
\xi_{-n}(x)
contains an open neighbourhood of
x
for
\mu
-a.e.
x\in M
, an atom of
\xi
contains at most countably many atoms of
\xi_{-n}
.
In the terminology of
[
12
, Definition 5.15]
, we say that
\xi
and
\xi_{-n}
are
countably equivalent
.

From
[
12
]
, Proposition 5.17 we obtain the following
superposition property
.

Lemma 2.15

(Superposition property)
.

For
\mu
a.e.
x\in M
it holds that

\mu_{x}(\xi_{-n}(x))>0.

Moreover, if
\mu_{-n,x}
is the disintegration of
\mu
with respect to the partition
\xi_{-n}
, that is, for any measurable set
A
,

\mu_{-n,x}(A)=\frac{\mu_{x}(A\cap\xi_{-n}(x))}{\mu_{x}(\xi_{-n}(x))}.

By the definition of a subordinate partition, for
\mu
-a.e.
x\in M
,
\{\xi_{-n}(x)\}_{n\geq 0}
contains a basis of open neighbourhoods of
x
.

Corollary 2.16
.

For
\mu
-a.e.
x\in M
,
\mu_{x}
charges every open neighbourhood of
x
.

Remark 2.17
.

If
\mathcal{W}^{-}
is contracting, and
\xi
is subordinate to
\mathcal{W}^{-}
, then the analogous statements to Lemma
2.15
and Corollary
2.16
hold for
\mathcal{W}^{-}
, replacing
\xi_{-n}
with
\xi_{n}
, for
n\geq 0
.

2.4.5.
Uniform growth property

Another useful property of the sequence of partitions
\xi_{n}
subordinate to a uniformly expanded foliation
{\mathcal{W}}^{+}
is the following
uniform growth property
.

Lemma 2.18
.

Given a real number
R>0
, for
\mu
almost every
x\in M
there exists
n_{0}=n_{0}(x,R)>0
such that if
n>n_{0}
then

{\mathcal{W}}^{+}_{R}(x)\subset\xi_{n}(x)

Proof.

Define
\Lambda(\varepsilon)=\{x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}};{\mathcal{W}}^{+}_{\varepsilon}(x)\subset\xi(x)\}
, and observe that
\mu(\Lambda(\varepsilon))\to 1
as
\varepsilon\to 0
, due to property (2) in the definition of subordinate partitions. The conclusion holds whenever
f^{-n_{k}}(x)\in\Lambda(1/k)
where
n_{k}>\frac{\log(kR)}{-\log\lambda}
(recall that
\|Df^{-1}|_{T{\mathcal{W}}^{+}}\|<\lambda<1
). So the conclusion of the lemma holds in the set
\bigcup_{k}f^{n_{k}}(\Lambda(1/k))
which has measure
1
by
f
-invariance of
\mu
.
∎

Remark 2.19
.

The above lemma has an analogous version for measurable partitions subordinate to uniformly contracted foliations: the integer
n_{0}
is then negative and the conclusion holds for all
n<n_{0}
.

2.5.
SRB and
u
-Gibbs measures

We return now to our partially hyperbolic setting where
f\in{\mathcal{A}}^{2}({\mathbb{T}^{3}})
. Let
\xi^{cu}
be a measurable partition of
{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
subordinate to the center unstable foliation
{\mathcal{W}}^{cu}
. Let
\mu
be an ergodic invariant measure for
f
. We say that
\mu
is an
SRB measure
if its disintegration
\{\mu^{cu}_{x}\}_{x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}}
with respect to the partition
\xi^{cu}
satisfies that
\mu^{cu}_{x}
is absolutely continuous with respect to the inner Lebesgue measure
\operatorname{Leb}^{cu}_{x}
of
{\mathcal{W}}^{cu}(x)
for
\mu
a.e.
x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
.

Consider now
\xi^{u}
a measurable partition subordinate to the unstable foliation
{\mathcal{W}}^{u}
and let
\{\mu^{u}_{x}\}_{x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}}
denote the disintegration. We say that
\mu
is a
u
-Gibbs measure
provided that
\mu^{u}_{x}
is absolutely continuous with respect to the inner Lebesgue (length) measure
\operatorname{Leb}^{u}_{x}
of
{\mathcal{W}}^{u}(x)
for
\mu
a.e.
x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
.

Remark 2.20
.

By Ledrappier-Young
[
32
]
none of the above definitions depend on the respective particular choice of subordinate partition.

2.5.1.
Conditional measures along unstable leaves of an
u
-Gibbs measure

We can (and we shall) consider the particular case in which the atoms of
\xi^{u}
are
\xi^{u}(x)={\mathcal{W}}^{u}(x)\cap\xi^{cu}(x)
. By Remark
2.13
we know that this defines a partition subordinate to the unstable foliation
{\mathcal{W}}^{u}
. This is specially important in our setting, in which we want to prove that a given
u
-Gibbs measure is SRB. In a similar fashion, we consider the partition
\xi^{c}
whose atoms are
\xi^{c}(x)={\mathcal{W}}^{c}(x)\cap\xi^{cu}(x)
.

Denote by
\xi^{*}_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}f^{n}(\xi^{*})
, for
*=u,cu,c
and
n\geq 0
, with
\xi^{*}_{0}=\xi^{*}
. Consider
\mu
a
u
-Gibbs measure and let
\mu^{cu}_{n,x}
denote the conditional measures along the partition
\xi^{cu}_{n}
. Notice that
\{\xi^{u}_{n}(y)\}_{y\in\xi^{cu}_{n}(x)}
is a measurable partition of the probability space
(\xi^{cu}_{n}(x),\mu^{cu}_{n,x},{\mathcal{B}}|_{\xi^{cu}_{n}(x)})
, where
{\mathcal{B}}|_{\xi^{cu}_{n}(x)}
denotes the Borel sigma algebra of
{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
restricted to the atom
\xi^{cu}_{n}(x)
. Rokhlin’s disintegration theorem in this case give us a probability measure
\mu^{c}_{n,x}
defined on
\xi^{c}_{n}(x)
and a family of probability measures
\{\mu^{u}_{n,y}\}_{y\in\xi^{c}_{n}(x)}
such that for every
A\subset\xi^{cu}_{n}(x)
Borel measurable set we have

\mu^{cu}_{n,x}(A)=\int_{A\cap{\mathcal{W}}^{c}(x)}\mu^{u}_{n,y}(A\cap{\mathcal{W}}^{u}(y))d\mu^{c}_{n,x}(y).

Remark 2.21
.

For simplicity of the exposition in many situations, where no confusion may arise, given a set
A\subset{\mathcal{W}}^{*}(x)
, we shall denote
|A|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}\operatorname{Leb}^{*}_{x}(A)
for
*\in\{s,c,u\}
.

An easy consequence of the
u
-Gibbs property is the following.

Lemma 2.22
.

There exists
\beta>1
depending only on the diffeomorphism
f
such that for
\mu
-almost every
x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
,

\frac{1}{\beta\,\mathrm{Leb}^{u}_{x}\left[\xi^{u}(x)\right]}<\frac{d\mu^{u}_{x}}{d\mathrm{Leb}^{u}_{x}}<\frac{\beta}{\mathrm{Leb}^{u}_{x}\left[\xi^{u}(x)\right]}

inside
\xi^{u}(x)
.

Proof.

This follows from the fact that
\mu^{u}_{x}
is a probability measure that has a uniformly log-Hölder continuous density with respect to
\mathrm{Leb}^{u}_{x}
.
∎

3.
Heuristics of the proof

This section can be used to get an overview of the proof of Theorem
A
and also as a guide to read our paper.

From now on let
f\in{\mathcal{A}}^{2}({\mathbb{T}^{3}})
be a diffeomorphism that is close to a conservative one. In this case, stable holonomies
H_{x,y}^{s}
are
C^{1}
, and we can define angles
\alpha^{s}(x,y)
for two points
x,y
in the same stable manifold (see Definition
4.1
). The vanishing of the angle
\alpha^{s}
can be seen as a kind of infinitesimal joint integrability.

Let
\mu
be an ergodic
u
-Gibbs measure for
f
.

Starting point: a zero-one law

We first use Theorem
B
, which yields the following dichotomy.

Case 1:

\alpha^{s}(x,y)=0
, for
\mu
-a.e. every pair
(x,y)
in the same stable manifold;

Case 2:

\alpha^{s}(x,y)>0
, for
\mu
-a.e. every pair
(x,y)
in the same stable manifold.

The proof of this theorem will be given in Section
4
and relies on a martingale argument.
In words, we either have positive angles almost everywhere along the stable manifold of almost every point, or we have zero angles almost everywhere along the stable manifold of almost every point.

The rest of the proof is divided into two parts:

—

[
Case 1 +
\mathrm{supp}(\mu)={\mathbb{T}^{3}}]\implies
joint integrability;

—

Case 2
\implies\mu
is SRB.

Part 1: joint integrability.

Given a point
x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
we define
the
Bad set
as

\mathbf{B}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}\{x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}:\mu_{x}^{s}\{\alpha^{s}_{x}>0\}=0\},

where
\alpha_{x}^{s}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}[y\in\mathcal{W}^{s}(x)\mapsto\alpha^{s}(x,y)]
, and
\mu^{s}_{x}
is the conditional measure of
x
along a stable manifold (see §
5.1
for more details). The
Bad set
is the set of points such that there is an “infinitesimal” joint integrability with almost every other point in its stable manifold.

The first part is an argument by contraposition that goes as follows (see Proposition
5.5
):

—

if there is no joint integrability, then for any
x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
, the set
\{\alpha_{x}^{s}>0\}
is open and dense within the stable manifold
\mathcal{W}^{s}(x)
(see Lemma
5.8
);

—

if, furthermore, the
Bad set

\mathbf{B}
has measure
1
, the continuity of the angles implies that whenever
\alpha^{s}(x_{0},y_{0})>0
, there exists a small foliated chart
\mathcal{V}
around
y_{0}
for
\mathcal{W}^{s}
of measure
0
(see Lemma
5.9
);

—

hence, combining the two previous points, if there is no joint integrability and
\mathbf{B}
has full measure, then the support of
\mu
must have empty interior.

The last point is the only place in the paper where the support condition on
\mu
is used.

Part 2: transversality implies SRB

Most of this paper is dedicated to proving that if
\mu
is a
u
-Gibbs measure such that
\mu(\mathbf{B})=0
then
\mu
is SRB (Theorem
5.10
).

To check that a
u
-Gibbs measure
\mu
is SRB, one can consider the disintegration of this measure along center-unstable manifolds
\{\mu^{cu}_{x}\}
and then quotient it by the strong unstable manifolds
\{\hat{\mu}^{c}_{x}\}
. These are called the transverse measures. Then
\mu
is SRB if and only if the transverse measures are equivalent to Lebesgue. In this approach, it is really convenient to consider certain parameterizations of center-unstable manifolds that simplify the dynamics, the so-called
normal forms
(see Section
6
). These coordinates allow us to identify the quotient measures
\{\hat{\mu}^{c}_{x}\}
with measures
\{\hat{\nu}^{c}_{x}\}
in
\mathbb{R}
. To conclude that the measures
\{\hat{\mu}^{c}_{x}\}
are equivalent to Lebesgue, we will show that the measures
\{\hat{\nu}^{c}_{x}\}
are proportional to the Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{R}
, where we say that two locally finite Borel measures
\nu,\eta
are proportional, and we indicate it by
\nu\propto\eta
, if
\nu=c\eta
for some
c>0
.

To do so, it is enough to prove that for
\mu
-a.e.
x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
,
\hat{\nu}^{c}_{x}
is invariant by translation. Actually, thanks to an argument due originally to Katok-Spatzier
[
29
]
and Kalinin-Katok
[
25
]
(see also
[
7
, Proposition 7.1]
), which is a beautiful mixture of ergodic and Lie theoretic arguments, it is enough to prove something weaker:
for a set
G
of points
x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
with positive measure, there exist affine maps
\psi
with uniformly bounded derivatives and arbitrarily small translational parts such that
\hat{\nu}_{x}^{c}\propto\psi_{\ast}\hat{\nu}_{x}^{c}
. This is Lemma
8.1
(largely inspired by
[
6
, Lemma 6.1]
).

The construction of these affine maps is where our key arguments are located. Our strategy is to use the
Y
-configurations
introduced by Eskin-Mirzakhani
[
15
]
(see also Eskin-Lindenstrauss
[
14
]
). Below we outline this proof, splitting the explanation in two parts, for the sake of clarity. First, we describe
Y
-configurations. Then we explain how to use them in order to get invariance by affine maps in the way we described in last paragraph.

Y
-configurations

See Figure
5
. Fixing
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
we find points
x
and
y
that are typical for
\mu
such that:

(1)

y\in{\mathcal{W}}^{s}_{\mathrm{loc}}(x)
and
d(x,y)\approx\|Df^{\ell}|_{E^{s}}\|
;

(2)

if
x_{-\ell}=f^{-\ell}(x)
and
y_{-\ell}=f^{-\ell}(y)
, then
d(x_{-\ell},y_{-\ell})\approx 1
and the angle
\alpha^{s}(x_{-\ell},y_{-\ell})
is more than some constant
\frac{1}{C}
.

As a consequence of points (1) and (2),
\alpha^{s}(x,y)\geq\frac{1}{C}\frac{\|Df^{\ell}(x_{-\ell})|_{E^{c}}\|}{\|Df^{\ell}(x_{-\ell})|_{E^{u}}\|}
. Normal forms help a lot here. This computation is performed inside the proof of Lemma
9.2
.

We can then find points
x^{u}\in{\mathcal{W}}^{u}_{\mathrm{loc}}(x)
and
y^{u}\in{\mathcal{W}}^{u}_{\mathrm{loc}}(y)
such that:

—

there exists a point
z^{u}
with
\{z^{u}\}={\mathcal{W}}^{s}_{\mathrm{loc}}(x^{u})\cap{\mathcal{W}}^{c}_{\mathrm{loc}}(y^{u})
;

—

d(z^{u},y^{u})\approx\frac{\|Df^{\ell}(x_{-\ell})|_{E^{c}}\|}{\|Df^{\ell}(x_{-\ell})|_{E^{u}}\|}
.

So far we obtained points
x^{u}
and
y^{u}
with some estimate of the displacement in the center direction that we get when projecting
x^{u}
to
{\mathcal{W}}^{u}_{\mathrm{loc}}(y)
by center stable holonomy. Next, fixing
\varepsilon>0
, we define the stopping time

\tau(\ell)=\tau(x,x^{u},\varepsilon,\ell)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}\inf\left\{n\in\mathbb{N}:\frac{\|Df^{\ell}(x_{-\ell})_{E^{c}}\|}{\|Df^{\ell}(x_{-\ell})|_{E^{u}}\|}\|Df^{n}(x^{u})|_{E^{c}}\|\geq\varepsilon\right\}

and consider the points
f^{\tau(\ell)}(x^{u})
and
f^{\tau(\ell)}(y^{u})
. The choice of the stopping time
\tau(\ell)
is such that

(9)

d(f^{\tau(\ell)}(x^{u}),f^{\tau(\ell)}(y^{u}))\approx\varepsilon,

see §
9.3.2
. Essentially, these points are related to the translational part of size
\varepsilon
of the affine maps that we desire to construct. To control the derivative of the affine maps and to actually obtain an “invariance” of the measures
\hat{\nu}^{c}_{x}
, as we mentioned before, we also define another stopping time

t(\ell)=t(x,x^{u},\varepsilon,\ell)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}\inf\left\{n\in\mathbb{N}:\frac{\|Df^{n}(x)|_{E^{c}}\|}{\|Df^{\tau(\ell)}(x^{u})|_{E^{c}}\|}\geq 1\right\}.

In particular, this condition implies that

(10)

\|Df^{t(\ell)}(x)|_{E^{c}}\|\approx\|Df^{\tau(\ell)}(x^{u})|_{E^{c}}\|,

see §
9.1
.

{\mathcal{W}}^{s}_{{\operatorname{loc}}}(x_{-\ell})
{\mathcal{W}}^{u}_{{\operatorname{loc}}}(x_{-\ell})
H^{s}_{x_{-\ell},y_{-\ell}}({\mathcal{W}}^{u}_{{\operatorname{loc}}}(x_{-\ell}))
z^{u}_{-\ell}
{\mathcal{W}}^{u}_{{\operatorname{loc}}}(y_{-\ell})
y^{u}_{-\ell}
f^{\ell}
f^{t(\ell)}
f^{\tau(\ell)}
f^{\tau(\ell)}
f^{\ell}\
f^{t(\ell)}
x_{-\ell}
\alpha^{s}(x_{-\ell},y_{-\ell})
y_{-\ell}
x^{u}
\asymp\varepsilon
f^{\tau(\ell)}(x^{u})
f^{\tau(\ell)}(z^{u})
f^{\tau(\ell)}(y^{u})
x^{u}_{-\ell}
y
x
f^{t(\ell)}(x)
f^{t(\ell)}(y)
z^{u}
y^{u}

Figure 5.
The points
x,x^{u},x_{-\ell},f^{\tau(l)}(x^{u})
and
f^{t(\ell)}(x)
are called a
Y
-configuration (similarly the points involving
y
’s).

How to use
Y
-configurations to get invariance by affine maps

By construction of measures
\{\hat{\nu}^{c}_{p}\}_{p\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}}
we understand how they change under three basic moves.

(1)

Applying the dynamics
. For
\mu
-a.e.
p\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
,

\hat{\nu}^{c}_{f^{n}(p)}\propto\Lambda_{*}\hat{\nu}^{c}_{p}
, for every
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, where
\Lambda(s)=\|Df^{n}(p)|_{E^{c}}\|s
is a linear map of
{\mathbb{R}}
(see Lemma
7.13
).

(2)

Moving along unstable manifolds
. For
q\in{\mathcal{W}}^{u}(p)
then
\hat{\nu}^{c}_{q}\propto L_{\ast}\hat{\nu}^{c}_{p}
where
L(s)=\beta s
is a linear map, for some
C_{u}^{-1}\leq\beta\leq C_{u}
, and
C_{u}=C_{u}(d_{u}(p,q))>1
is a number which is bounded from above and it depends on the unstable distance
d_{u}(p,q)
(see Lemma
7.14
).

(3)

Moving along center manifolds
. For
q\in{\mathcal{W}}^{c}(p)
then
\hat{\nu}^{c}_{q}\propto\Psi_{\ast}\hat{\nu}^{c}_{p}
where
\Psi(s)=as+b
, with
C_{c}^{-1}\leq a\leq C_{c}
for some constant
C_{c}=C_{c}(d_{c}(p,q))>1
bounded from above with
d_{c}(p,q)
, and with
b\approx d_{c}(p,q)
(see Lemma
7.12
).

Now we look at the points on the top part of Figure
5
and deduce two facts.

(a)

\hat{\nu}^{c}_{f^{\tau(\ell)}(x^{u})}\propto(\Lambda_{1})_{*}\hat{\nu}^{c}_{f^{t(\ell)}(x)}
for some
linear
map
\Lambda_{1}\colon{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
with derivative bounded independently of
\ell
.

(b)

\hat{\nu}^{c}_{f^{\tau(\ell)}(y^{u})}\propto(\Lambda_{2})_{*}\hat{\nu}^{c}_{f^{t(\ell)}(y)}
for some
linear
map
\Lambda_{2}\colon{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
with derivative bounded independently of
\ell
.

There is a subtlety here: stopping times
\tau(\ell)
and
t(\ell)
depend on
x,x^{u}
and not on
y,y^{u}
. This is treated by our
synchronization estimates
(Lemma
9.4
). Now note that as
\ell\to\infty
,
\tau(\ell),t(\ell)\to\infty
so
d(f^{\tau(\ell)}(x^{u}),f^{\tau(\ell)}(z^{u}))\to 0
and
d(f^{t(\ell)}(x),f^{t(\ell)}(y))\to 0
. If we knew that the family of measures
\hat{\nu}^{c}_{z}
were continuous with
z
then we could hope to compare
\hat{\nu}^{c}_{f^{\tau(\ell)}(x^{u})}
with
\hat{\nu}^{c}_{f^{\tau(\ell)}(y^{u})}
and take accumulation points to construct the desired set
G
.

But the objects we are working with are only measurable, so we must first fix a large Lusin set for which the map
z\mapsto\hat{\nu}^{c}_{z}
is continuous (among other dynamical objects that appear in the proof). We want to do the constructions of all of the points mentioned above, in a way that all of them belong to this Lusin set. For this, it is essential to obtain
quasi-isometric estimates
for the functions
\tau(\cdot)
and
t(\cdot)
(see Lemma
9.1
; see also Lemma
11.5
where the quasi-isometric estimates for stopping times are used in a crucial way).

By continuity, we have that
\hat{\nu}^{c}_{f^{t(\ell)}(x)}\approx\hat{\nu}^{c}_{f^{t(\ell)}(y)}
and
\hat{\nu}^{c}_{f^{\tau(\ell)}(x^{u})}\approx\hat{\nu}^{c}_{f^{\tau(\ell)}(z^{u})}
. We conclude from this that
\hat{\nu}^{c}_{f^{\tau(\ell)}(x^{u})}
is
almost proportional to

\hat{\nu}^{c}_{f^{\tau(\ell)}(y^{u})}
,
up to some linear map with controlled derivative
.

After considering a subsequence
\ell_{k}
we obtain points
p
and
q
(obtained as the limit of
f^{\tau(\ell_{k})}(x^{u})
and
f^{\tau(\ell_{k})}(y^{u}))
) with the properties that:

—

q\in\mathcal{W}^{c}_{\mathrm{loc}}(p)
;

—

d(p,q)\approx\varepsilon
;

—

p
and
q
belong to the Lusin set;

—

\hat{\nu}^{c}_{q}\propto\hat{\Lambda}_{\ast}\hat{\nu}^{c}_{p}
,
\hat{\Lambda}
being linear with uniformly bounded derivative (see §
10.4
).

Finally we explained that
\hat{\nu}^{c}_{p}\propto\Psi_{\ast}\hat{\nu}^{c}_{q}
for an affine map
\Psi
with uniformly bounded derivative and a translational part of order
\varepsilon
: we find
\hat{\nu}^{c}_{p}\propto(\Psi\hat{\Lambda})_{\ast}\hat{\nu}^{c}_{p}
:
\Psi\hat{\Lambda}
is the desired affine map (with bounded slope and translational part
\approx\varepsilon
).

A technical difficulty

There is one delicate point in the above argument. Since the center stable foliation is not absolutely continuous, in general, we cannot choose the points
x^{u}
and
y^{u}
at the same time in the Lusin set and such that
y^{u}\in{\mathcal{W}}^{cs}_{\mathrm{loc}}(x^{u})
. To overcome this difficulty, we introduce the notion of matched
Y
-configurations in Section
10
(the picture changes just a little bit from the one described above) and use it to prove our theorem (see Section
11
).

Let us also emphasize that the control we obtain on the distance between the points
z^{u}
and
y^{u}
is possible because
H^{s}
is
C^{1}
, so essentially we can control this distance by looking at the angle between
DH^{s}_{x,y}E^{u}(x)
and
E^{u}(y)
. This allows us to explicitly define the stopping time
\tau
in our construction. This is not possible in Katz’s proof since the holonomies are only Hölder. To get around this problem, he defines a much more complicated stopping time built from an operator using ideas from Eskin-Mirzakhani.

4.
A zero-one law for angles

The goal of this section is to prove Theorem
B
. In a slightly more general context, i.e., for some
f\in\mathcal{PH}^{2}({\mathbb{T}^{3}})
with
C^{1}
stable holonomies (for instance, if
f
is close to some
f_{0}\in{\mathcal{A}}^{2}_{m}({\mathbb{T}^{3}})
, as we saw in Lemma
2.6
), we introduce the angle function
\alpha^{s}
, which measures the “twist” of unstable manifolds along stable manifolds.

Definition 4.1

(The angle function)
.

Let
x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
and
y\in\mathcal{W}^{s}(x)
. The
angle function
is the assignment
(x,y)\mapsto\alpha^{s}(x,y)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}\angle(DH^{s}_{x,y}(x)E^{u}(x),E^{u}(y))
.

In this context, we have the following zero-one law, whose proof is inspired by the work of Brown-Hertz
[
6
, Lemma 7.1]
.

Theorem 4.2

(A zero-one law for angles)
.

Let
f\in\mathcal{PH}^{2}({\mathbb{T}^{3}})
be a partially hyperbolic diffeomorphism with a splitting
T{\mathbb{T}^{3}}=E^{s}\oplus E^{c}\oplus E^{u}
, whose stable holonomies
H^{s}
along the strong stable foliation
{\mathcal{W}}^{s}
are
C^{1}
. Fix an ergodic
f
-invariant measure
\mu
. Let
\xi^{s}
be a measurable partition subordinate to
{\mathcal{W}}^{s}
and
\{\mu^{s}_{x}\}_{x}
be a system of conditional measures relative to
\xi^{s}
. Then the following dichotomy holds:

(1)

either for
\mu
-almost every
x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
,

\mu^{s}_{x}\left\{y\in\xi^{s}(x):\alpha^{s}(x,y)=0\right\}=1;

(2)

or for
\mu
-almost every
x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
,

\mu^{s}_{x}\left\{y\in\xi^{s}(x):\alpha^{s}(x,y)=0\right\}=0.

The second alternative will allow us to build “twisted” quadrilaterals (such as the one depicted in Figure
4
) with points
x,y
in some good Lusin set while for
x^{u}
and
y^{u}
we will find points of the Lusin set arbitrarily close to them, so that the twisted quadrilateral will be part of a
matched
Y
-configurations
associated to
x
and
y
.

4.1.
Conditional expectation,
\sigma
-algebras and martingales

The proof of Theorem
4.2
requires some preliminaires about martingales.
If
\xi
is a measurable partition of a measurable space
X
then we let
{\mathcal{F}}_{\xi}
denote the
\sigma
-algebra generated by unions of atoms of
\xi
. Let
\{\mu_{x}\}_{x}
be a system of conditional measures of
\mu
with respect to
\xi
. We define the
conditional expectation
of
\varphi\in L^{1}(X,\mu)
as the following
L^{1}
-function

{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}[\varphi\,|\,{\mathcal{F}}_{\xi}]\colon x\mapsto\int_{\xi(x)}\varphi\,d\mu^{\xi}_{x}.

Note that if
(\xi_{n})_{n}
is an increasing sequence of partitions (in the sense that
\xi_{n}\prec\xi_{n+1}
) then we have
{\mathcal{F}}_{\xi_{n}}\mathop{\subset}{\mathcal{F}}_{\xi_{n+1}}
.

The following result is a consequence of the increasing martingale theorem (for which we refer to
[
13
, Theorem 5.5, p.126]
).

Theorem 4.3

(Increasing martingale theorem)
.

Let
(\xi_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
be an increasing sequence of measurable partitions of
X
such that
\bigvee_{n=0}^{\infty}\xi_{n}
is the partition into points.
Then, for every
\varphi\in L^{1}(X,\mu)
and
\mu
-almost every
x\in X
, we have

\lim_{n\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\big[\varphi\,\big|\,{\mathcal{F}}_{\xi_{n}}\big](x)=\varphi(x).

4.2.
Proof of the zero-one law

We are now ready to prove Theorem
4.2
.
We fix
\xi^{s}
, a measurable partition subordinate to
{\mathcal{W}}^{s}
. For
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, set
\xi^{s}_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}f^{n}\xi^{s}
. Systems of conditional measures relative to
\xi^{s}
and
\xi^{s}_{n}
are denoted respectively by
\{\mu^{s}_{x}\}_{x}
and
\{\mu^{s}_{n,x}\}_{x}
. For
\mu
-almost every
x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
and every
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, set

—

{\mathcal{P}}^{\xi^{s}}(x)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}\left\{y\in\xi^{s}(x):\alpha^{s}(x,y)=0\right\}
,

—

{\mathcal{P}}^{\xi^{s}}_{n}(x)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}\left\{y\in\xi_{n}^{s}(x):\alpha^{s}(x,y)=0\right\}
.

By definition of
\xi^{s}_{n}
and invariance of the unstable bundle and of the stable foliation, we have the following commutation relation for
\mu
-almost every
x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
:

(11)

f^{n}{\mathcal{P}}^{\xi^{s}}\left(f^{-n}(x)\right)={\mathcal{P}}^{\xi^{s}}_{n}(x).

Let
A\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}\{x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}:\mu^{s}_{x}[{\mathcal{P}}^{\xi^{s}}(x)]>0\}
. This is a Borel set. We must prove the following dichotomy:

(1)

either
\mu(A)=1
, and for
\mu
-almost every
x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
,
\mu^{s}_{x}[{\mathcal{P}}^{\xi^{s}}(x)]=1
;

(2)

or
\mu(A)=0
.

So let us suppose
\mu(A)>0
and prove (
1
). With that goal in mind, we claim that
A
is an
f
-invariant set (mod 0). Indeed, take a
\mu
generic point
x\in A
. Notice that
{\mathcal{P}}^{\xi^{s}}_{1}(x)\subset{\mathcal{P}}^{\xi^{s}}(x)
, since
\xi^{s}_{1}(x)\subset\xi^{s}(x)
. Also, recall from Lemma
2.12
that

\mu^{s}_{1,f(x)}=f_{*}\mu^{s}_{x}.

Combining this with (
11
) one obtains that

\mu^{s}_{1,f(x)}({\mathcal{P}}^{\xi^{s}}_{1}(f(x)))=\mu^{s}_{x}(f^{-1}({\mathcal{P}}^{\xi^{s}}_{1}(f(x))))=\mu^{s}_{x}({\mathcal{P}}^{\xi^{s}}(x))>0.

Thus, from the superposition property (Lemma
2.15
and Remark
2.17
) one gets

\mu^{s}_{f(x)}({\mathcal{P}}^{\xi^{s}}(f(x)))\geq\mu^{s}_{f(x)}({\mathcal{P}}^{\xi^{s}}_{1}(f(x)))=\mu^{s}_{1,f(x)}({\mathcal{P}}^{\xi^{s}}_{1}(f(x)))\mu^{s}_{f(x)}(\xi^{s}_{1}(f(x)))>0,

which implies that
f(x)\in A
, proving our claim.

From the ergodicity of
\mu
, we have
\mu(A)=1
. We define functions in
L^{1}({\mathbb{T}^{3}},\mu)
by

—

\phi\colon x\mapsto\mu^{s}_{x}[{\mathcal{P}}^{\xi^{s}}(x)]
;

—

\phi_{n}\colon x\mapsto\mu^{s}_{n,x}[{\mathcal{P}}^{\xi^{s}}_{n}(x)]=\mu^{s}_{n,x}[{\mathcal{P}}^{\xi^{s}}(x)]
, for each
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
.

For
\mu
-almost every
x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
and every
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
we consider

—

{\mathcal{F}}^{s}_{x}={\mathcal{F}}^{s}_{\xi^{s}(x)}=\{\emptyset,\xi^{s}(x)\}
, the trivial
\sigma
-algebra over
\xi^{s}(x)
;

—

{\mathcal{F}}^{s}_{n,x}={\mathcal{F}}^{s}_{n,\xi^{s}(x)}
, the
\sigma
-algebra generated by unions of atoms
\xi_{n}^{s}(y)
,
y\in\xi^{s}(x)
(note that
{\mathcal{F}}^{s}_{n,x}\mathop{\subset}{\mathcal{F}}^{s}_{n+1,x}
);

—

{\mathcal{F}}^{s}_{\infty,x}={\mathcal{F}}^{s}_{\infty,\xi^{s}(x)}
, the smallest
\sigma
-algebra containing
\bigcup_{n=0}^{\infty}{\mathcal{F}}^{s}_{n,x}
. This is the Borel
\sigma
-algebra of
\xi^{s}(x)
.

For
\mu
-almost every
x\in{\mathbb{T}}^{3}
and
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, we define the function
\psi_{n,x}\in L^{1}(\xi^{s}(x),\mu_{x}^{s})
by the following formula, for
\mu^{s}_{x}
-almost every
y\in\xi^{s}(x)
:

\psi_{n,x}(y)={\mathbb{E}}_{\mu_{x}^{s}}\big[\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{P}}^{\xi^{s}}(x)}\,\big|\,{\mathcal{F}}^{s}_{n,x}\big](y)=\mu^{s}_{n,y}\big[{\mathcal{P}}^{\xi^{s}}(x)\big].

Note that
\psi_{n,x}(y)=\phi_{n}(y)
for all
n
and
\mu^{s}_{x}
-almost every
y\in{\mathcal{P}}^{\xi^{s}}(x)
(note that in that case
{\mathcal{P}}^{\xi^{s}}(x)={\mathcal{P}}^{\xi^{s}}(y)
).

On the one hand, for
\mu
-almost every
x\in{\mathbb{T}}^{3}
, by the increasing martingale theorem (we apply Theorem
4.3
to the probability space
(\xi^{s}(x),\mu_{x}^{s})
),
\psi_{n,x}
converges to
\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{P}}^{\xi^{s}}(x)}

\mu^{s}_{x}
-almost surely as
n\to+\infty
. In particular,

(12)

\text{for }\mu^{s}_{x}\text{-a.e. }y\in{\mathcal{P}}^{\xi^{s}}(x),\quad\psi_{n,x}(y)=\phi_{n}(y)\to 1\text{ as }n\to+\infty.

Let us define the set

\mathcal{S}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}\{x\in{\mathbb{T}}^{3}:\phi_{n}(x)\to 1\text{ as }n\to+\infty\}.

For each
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
\phi_{n}
is measurable, hence
\mathcal{S}
is a Borel set. Assume that
\mu[\mathcal{S}]=0
. Since
\mu[\mathcal{S}]=\int\mu_{x}^{s}[\mathcal{S}]d\mu(x)
, we would then have
\mu_{x}^{s}[\mathcal{S}]=0
, for
\mu
-a.e.
x\in{\mathbb{T}}^{3}
. But (
12
) implies that for
\mu
-a.e.
x\in A
,
\mu_{x}^{s}[\mathcal{S}]\geq\mu_{x}^{s}[\mathcal{S}\cap{\mathcal{P}}^{\xi^{s}}(x)]=\mu_{x}^{s}[{\mathcal{P}}^{\xi^{s}}(x)]>0
, and by our assumption that
\mu[A]>0
, we reach a contradiction. Therefore,

\mu[\mathcal{S}]=\mu\{x\in{\mathbb{T}}^{3}:\phi_{n}(x)\to 1\text{ as }n\to+\infty\}>0.

On the other hand, we deduce from (
11
) and from Lemma
2.12
that

\phi_{n}(x)=\mu^{s}_{n,x}[{\mathcal{P}}^{\xi^{s}}_{n}(x)]=f^{n}_{\ast}\mu^{s}_{f^{-n}(x)}[f^{n}{\mathcal{P}}(f^{-n}(x))]=\phi(f^{-n}(x)).

The latter proves that
\phi\circ f^{-k}
converges to
1

\mu
-almost surely on
\mathcal{S}
as
k\to+\infty
. Therefore, by considering Cesàro averages
\frac{1}{n}\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\phi\circ f^{-k}(x)
for a
\mu
-generic point
x\in\mathcal{S}
, and by Birkhoff’s theorem, we conclude that
\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}}\phi\,d\mu=1
. As
\phi
takes values in
[0,1]
, we must have
\phi(x)=1
, for
\mu
-almost every
x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
.
∎

Remark 4.4
.

It is clear that
\alpha^{s}(x,y)=0
is an equivalence relation on stable leaves, so
Theorem
4.2
can be generalized as follows. Let
f
be as in Theorem
4.2
. Let
\mathcal{R}
be a measurable equivalence relation on stable leaves (i.e., such that
x\mathcal{R}y\,\Rightarrow\,y\in{\mathcal{W}}^{s}(x)
) such that
x\mathcal{R}y\,\Rightarrow\,f^{n}(x)\mathcal{R}f^{n}(y)
, for any
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
. Fix a measurable partition
\xi^{s}
subordinate to
{\mathcal{W}}^{s}
, a system
\{\mu^{s}_{x}\}_{x}
of conditional measures relative to
\xi^{s}
, and for
x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
, let
{\mathcal{P}}^{\xi^{s}}(x)
be its
\mathcal{R}
-equivalence class. Then the following dichotomy holds:

(1)

either for
\mu
-almost every
x\in M
,

\mu^{s}_{x}[{\mathcal{P}}^{\xi^{s}}(x)]=1
;

(2)

or for
\mu
-almost every
x\in M
,

\mu^{s}_{x}[{\mathcal{P}}^{\xi^{s}}(x)]=0
.

5.
Joint integrability and the
Bad set

We start in this section the formal proof of Theorem
A
. So we let
f\in{\mathcal{A}}^{2}({\mathbb{T}^{3}})
be an Anosov diffeomorphism, strongly partially hyperbolic with expanding center and
C^{1}
stable bundle. Recall from Lemma
2.6
that this is always satisfied when
f
is close to a conservative map
f_{0}
. Let
\mu
denote an ergodic
u
-Gibbs measure, with full support.

We are going to reduce the proof of Theorem
A
to a more technical version of the result by studying the set of points
x
for which one sees almost no twist of the bundle
E^{u}
by the application of the stable holonomy. Our main technical result says that if the measure of this
Bad set
is zero then
\mu
is SRB.

5.1.
The
Bad set

For any
x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
, we denote

{\mathcal{P}}(x)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}\Big\{y\in{\mathcal{W}}^{s}(x):\alpha^{s}(x,y)=0\Big\},\quad\mathcal{N}(x)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}\Big\{y\in{\mathcal{W}}^{s}(x):\alpha^{s}(x,y)>0\Big\}.

Observe that given any measurable partition
\xi^{s}
subordinate to
{\mathcal{W}}^{s}
we have that for
\mu
-a.e.
x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
,
{\mathcal{P}}^{\xi^{s}}(x)={\mathcal{P}}(x)\cap\xi^{s}(x)
, where
{\mathcal{P}}^{\xi^{s}}(x)
was defined in the proof of Theorem
4.2
, our zero-one law: see §
4.2
.

Remark 5.1
.

Since the unstable bundle
E^{u}
is invariant under
Df
, and holonomy maps are equivariant with the dynamics, i.e.,

(13)

f\circ H^{s}_{x,y}=H^{s}_{f(x),f(y)}\circ f,

we have
\forall\,x,y,

(14)

\alpha^{s}(x,y)=0\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\forall\,n\in{\mathbb{Z}},\ \alpha^{s}(f^{n}(x),f^{n}(y))=0.

For any
x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
, the function
\alpha^{s}(x,\cdot)
on
\mathcal{W}^{s}(x)
is continuous. Therefore, the sets
{\mathcal{P}}(x)
and
\mathcal{N}(x)
are respectively closed and open.
Moreover, by Remark
5.1
, it holds

(15)

f^{n}({\mathcal{P}}(x))={\mathcal{P}}\big(f^{n}(x)\big),\quad f^{n}(\mathcal{N}(x))=\mathcal{N}(f^{n}(x)),\quad\forall\,n\in{\mathbb{Z}}.

Definition 5.2

(Bad set)
.

Let
\xi^{s}
be a measurable partition subordinate to the stable foliation
{\mathcal{W}}^{s}
, and let
\{\mu_{x}^{s}\}_{x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}}
be a system of conditional measures relative to
\xi^{s}
. The
Bad set

\mathbf{B}=\mathbf{B}(\xi^{s},\mu)
is defined as

\mathbf{B}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}\big\{x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}:\mu^{s}_{x}(\mathcal{N}(x))=0\big\}.

Using our zero-one law, and the results established in §
4.2
, we have the following properties.

(1)

\mathbf{B}
is equal to the set
A
introduced in §
4.2
;

(2)

\mathbf{B}
is measurable and
f
-invariant, and it satisfies that for all
\ell\in{\mathbb{Z}}

(16)

\mathbf{B}(\xi^{s},\mu)=\mathbf{B}(\xi^{s}_{\ell},\mu),

where we recall
\xi^{s}_{\ell}=f^{\ell}(\xi^{s})
is still a measurable partition subordinate to
{\mathcal{W}}^{s}
;

(3)

\mathbf{B}
has measure
0
or
1
.

A priori the
Bad set

\mathbf{B}=\mathbf{B}(\xi^{s},\mu)
depends on the particular choice of the measurable partition subordinate to
{\mathcal{W}}^{s}
. Nevertheless, the next lemma shows that the Bad set associated to another subordinate partition is equal to
\mathbf{B}
modulo sets of measure
0
.

Lemma 5.3
.

For every measurable partition
\eta^{s}
subordinate to
{\mathcal{W}}^{s}
we have

\mu\left(\mathbf{B}(\xi^{s},\mu)\right)=\mu\left(\mathbf{B}(\eta^{s},\mu)\right)\in\{0,1\}.

Proof.

For
\ell\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, let us denote
\xi^{s}_{\ell}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}f^{\ell}(\xi^{s})
. Then, all the partitions
\xi^{s}_{\ell}
are measurable partitions subordinate to
{\mathcal{W}}^{s}
and by (
16
) we have that for all
\ell\in{\mathbb{Z}}
,
\mathbf{B}(\xi^{s},\mu)=\mathbf{B}(\xi^{s}_{\ell},\mu)
. These sets have measure
0
or
1
; assume they have measure
1
. Consider a
\mu
-generic point
x
so that

—

\eta^{s}(x)
contains an open neighbourhood of
x
in
{\mathcal{W}}^{s}(x)
so
\mu^{\xi^{s}_{m}}_{x}(\eta^{s}(x))>0
for all
m
(see Corollary
2.16
);

—

\eta^{s}(x)
has diameter less than
1
and;

—

\mu_{x}^{\xi^{s}_{m}}({\mathcal{N}}(x))=0
.

Using Lemma
2.18
(specifically Remark
2.19
) there exists
m<0
such that
\eta^{s}(x)\mathop{\subset}\xi^{s}_{m}(x)
so we can apply the superposition property and

\mu^{\eta^{s}}_{x}({\mathcal{N}}(x))=\frac{\mu^{\xi^{s}_{m}}_{x}({\mathcal{N}}(x)\cap\eta^{s}(x))}{\mu^{\xi^{s}_{m}}_{x}(\eta^{s}(x))}\leq\frac{\mu^{\xi^{s}_{m}}_{x}({\mathcal{N}}(x))}{\mu^{\xi^{s}_{m}}_{x}(\eta^{s}(x))}=0.

As a result,
x\in\mathbf{B}\left(\eta^{s},\mu\right)
. This proves that
\mu\left(\mathbf{B}(\eta^{s},\mu)\right)=1
, confirming the second assertion and concluding.
∎

Although we always need to fix a partition to speak about the bad set, Lemma
5.3
allows us to speak about
the measure of the Bad set
without fixing a particular choice of a partition.

Remark 5.4
.

The conclusion of Lemma
5.3
remains valid even if the measurable partition
\eta^{s}
is only required to have the following properties for
\mu
-a.e.
x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}

(1)

\eta^{s}(x)\mathop{\subset}{\mathcal{W}}^{s}(x)
;

(2)

\eta^{s}(x)
contains an open neighbourhood of
{\mathcal{W}}^{s}(x)
in the internal topology.

(3)

\eta^{s}(x)
has uniformly bounded diameter;

Indeed the proof of Lemma
5.3
does not use that
\eta^{s}
is decreasing.

There are many (non decreasing) measurable partitions satisfying these three properties. For example, consider any finite foliated atlas
{\mathcal{A}}
for
{\mathcal{W}}^{s}
composed of open charts
U_{i}
such that
\mu(\partial U_{i})=0
. Define a finite partition mod
0
of
{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
as

\mathcal{Q}=\bigvee_{i}\left\{U_{i},{\mathbb{T}^{3}}\setminus\overline{U_{i}}\right\},

Each atom
\mathcal{Q}(x)
is an open set included inside a foliated chart for
{\mathcal{W}}^{s}
. As a result it is trivially foliated by stable plaques: this defines a measurable partition of
\mathcal{Q}(x)
. We obtain a refinment of
\mathcal{Q}
, denoted by
\eta^{s}
, that satisfies the desired properties, and is referred to as the
measurable partition associated to

{\mathcal{A}}
.

5.2.
Joint integrability
