When the Bad set has positive measure, by ergodicity we have that for almost every
x
, we see almost no twist of
E^{u}
along
{\mathcal{W}}^{s}(x)
. This can be read as an infinitesimal form of joint integrability between
E^{s}
and
E^{u}
. In this paragraph we shall improve this to actual joint integrability.

Proposition 5.5
.

Let
f\colon\mathbb{T}^{3}\to\mathbb{T}^{3}
be a
C^{2}
Anosov diffeomorphism, strongly partially hyperbolic with expanding center and
C^{1}
stable holonomies. Let
\mu
be a fully supported ergodic
f
-invariant measure. If the
Bad set
has full measure then
E^{s}
and
E^{u}
are jointly integrable.

It is worth to point out that the proof of this proposition is the only place in our argument towards Theorem
A
where the full support assumption of the
u
-Gibbs measure is used. Moreover, in Proposition
5.5
the ergodic invariant measure
\mu
does not need to be
u
-Gibbs.

5.2.1.
Local joint integrability

In the proof of Proposition
5.5
we shall apply a criterion for joint integrability that comes from
[
11
]
. See also
[
43
, §2.3]
.

Definition 5.6
.

Given
x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
we say that the bundles
E^{s}
and
E^{u}
are
jointly integrable at
x
if there exists
\delta,\varepsilon>0
such that for each
z\in{\mathcal{W}}^{s}_{\delta}(x)
and
y\in{\mathcal{W}}^{u}_{\delta}(x)
it holds

{\mathcal{W}}^{u}_{\varepsilon}(z)\cap{\mathcal{W}}^{s}(y)\neq\emptyset.

The result below follows directly from
[
11
, Lemma 5]
(see also
[
43
, Lemma 2.3.7]
).

Lemma 5.7
.

Let
f\in{\mathcal{A}}^{2}({\mathbb{T}^{3}})
. Assume that
E^{s}
and
E^{u}
are jointly integrable at each
x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
, with uniform constants
\delta,\varepsilon
. Then,
f
is jointly integrable (in the sense of Definition
2.1
).

5.2.2.
Proof of Proposition
5.5

This Proposition follows directly from Lemma
5.8
and Lemma
5.9
below.

On the topological level, we have:

Lemma 5.8
.

Let
f\colon\mathbb{T}^{3}\to\mathbb{T}^{3}
be a
C^{2}
Anosov diffeomorphism, strongly partially hyperbolic with expanding center and
C^{1}
stable holonomies.

Then the following dichotomy holds:

—

either
E^{s}
and
E^{u}
are jointly integrable;

—

or for any
x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
, the set
\mathcal{N}(x)
is open and dense in
{\mathcal{W}}^{s}(x)
.

Proof.

Let us assume that there exists
x_{0}\in{\mathbb{T}}^{3}
such that
{\mathcal{P}}(x_{0})
contains a non-trivial open interval, i.e., that for some
x\in\mathcal{W}^{s}(x_{0})
, and
\varepsilon>0
, it holds
\mathcal{W}_{\varepsilon}^{s}(x)\subset{\mathcal{P}}(x_{0})
.
Since
{\mathcal{P}}(x)={\mathcal{P}}(x_{0})
, we also have
{\mathcal{P}}(x)\supset\mathcal{W}_{\varepsilon}^{s}(x)
, hence by (
15
), for any integer
n\geq 1
,

(17)

{\mathcal{P}}\big(f^{-n}(x)\big)\supset f^{-n}\big(\mathcal{W}_{\varepsilon}^{s}(x)\big).

By compactness, we can take a subsequence
(f^{-n_{k}}(x))_{k\geq 0}
such that
f^{-n_{k}}(x)\to y
as
k\to+\infty
, for some point
y\in{\mathbb{T}}^{3}
. Since the restriction of
f^{-1}
to stable leaves is uniformly expanding, we deduce from (
17
) that

(18)

{\mathcal{P}}(y)=\mathcal{W}^{s}(y).

Let us now show that the same holds for any point, i.e., that
{\mathcal{P}}(z)=\mathcal{W}^{s}(z)
, for any
z\in{\mathbb{T}}^{3}
.
Fix
z\in{\mathbb{T}}^{3}
and
z^{\prime}\in\mathcal{W}^{s}(z)
. By minimality of the stable foliation, the leaf
\mathcal{W}^{s}(y)
is dense in
{\mathbb{T}}^{3}
, hence there exists a sequence
(y_{n})_{n\geq 0}\in(\mathcal{W}^{s}(y))^{{\mathbb{N}}}
such that
\lim_{n\to+\infty}y_{n}=z
. For each integer
n\geq 0
, we let
y_{n}^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}H_{z,z^{\prime}}^{s}(y_{n})\in\mathcal{W}^{cu}(z^{\prime})\cap\mathcal{W}^{s}(y_{n})
. In particular, we also have
\lim_{n\to+\infty}y_{n}^{\prime}=H_{z,z^{\prime}}^{s}(\lim_{n}y_{n})=z^{\prime}
. By continuity of the angle function
\alpha^{s}
, we deduce that

\alpha^{s}(z,z^{\prime})=\lim_{n\to+\infty}\alpha^{s}(y_{n},y_{n}^{\prime})=0,

i.e.,
z^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}(z)
. Since
z^{\prime}\in\mathcal{W}^{s}(z)
was chosen arbitrarily within
\mathcal{W}^{s}(z)
, we deduce that
\mathcal{W}^{s}(z)=\mathcal{P}(z)
, for all
z\in{\mathbb{T}}^{3}
.

Now, fix arbitrarily
x\in{\mathbb{T}}^{3}
. Let
0<\delta<\varepsilon
be chosen so that the stable holonomy map
H^{s}_{x,z}\colon{\mathcal{W}}^{cu}_{\delta}(x)\to{\mathcal{W}}^{cu}_{\varepsilon}(z)
is well defined for every
z\in{\mathcal{W}}^{s}_{\delta}(x)
.
Notice that for any
y\in\mathcal{W}_{\delta}^{u}(x)
, for any
z\in\mathcal{W}_{\delta}^{s}(x)
, if we set
y^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}H_{x,z}^{s}(y)\in\mathcal{W}_{\varepsilon}^{s}(y)
, we have

\angle\big(DH_{x,z}^{s}E^{u}(y),E^{u}(y^{\prime})\big)=\alpha^{s}(y,y^{\prime})=0.

In particular,
H_{x,z}^{s}\big(\mathcal{W}_{\delta}^{u}(x)\big)
is a
C^{1}
curve that is everywhere tangent to
E^{u}
and at

y^{\prime}
it is tangent to
E^{u}(y^{\prime})
. By the unique integrability of the
E^{u}
bundle, we conclude that

H_{x,z}^{s}\big(\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{loc}}^{u}(x)\big)\subset\mathcal{W}^{u}(y^{\prime}).

Hence, we obtain that for any
x\in{\mathbb{T}}^{3}
, any
z\in{\mathcal{W}}^{s}_{\delta}(x)
and
y\in{\mathcal{W}}^{u}_{\delta}(x)
we have

{\mathcal{W}}^{u}_{\varepsilon}(z)\cap{\mathcal{W}}^{s}_{\varepsilon}(y)\neq\emptyset.

This proves that
f
fulfils the assumption of Lemma
5.7
. Therefore,
f
is jointly integrable.∎

The lemma below concludes the proof of Proposition
5.5
.

\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{loc}}^{u}(x_{0})
H^{s}_{x_{0},y_{0}}(\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{loc}}^{u}(x_{0}))
\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{loc}}^{u}(y_{0})
x_{0}
\pi(y)
y
\mathcal{W}^{s}(x_{0})
\mathcal{U}
\mathcal{W}^{s}(\pi(y))=\mathcal{W}^{s}(y)
T
\mathcal{V}_{y_{0}}
\mathcal{W}^{cu}_{\mathrm{loc}}(y_{0})
y_{0}
\alpha^{s}(x_{0},y_{0})

Figure 6.
Case where
E^{s}\oplus E^{u}
is not integrable and
\nu(\mathbf{B}_{\nu})=1
.

Lemma 5.9
.

Let
f\colon\mathbb{T}^{3}\to\mathbb{T}^{3}
be a
C^{2}
Anosov diffeomorphism, strongly partially hyperbolic with expanding center and
C^{1}
stable holonomies.
Assume that
E^{s}
and
E^{u}
are not jointly integrable. Then, for any ergodic

f
-invariant Borel probability measure
\nu
on
{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
, we have the dichotomy:

—

either the
Bad set
associated to
\nu
has zero measure;

—

or
\mathrm{supp}(\nu)
has empty interior.

Proof.

Fix
\nu
as in the statement of the lemma, and let
x_{0}\in{\mathbb{T}}^{3}
. Assume the first possibility does not occur. Then, the
Bad set
has full measure. As we assume that
E^{s}
and
E^{u}
are not jointly integrable, by Lemma
5.8
, the set
\mathcal{N}(x_{0})
is open and dense in
{\mathcal{W}}^{s}(x_{0})
. Fix
y_{0}\in\mathcal{N}(x_{0})
, i.e., such that
\alpha^{s}(x_{0},y_{0})\neq 0
. By the continuity of the angle function
\alpha^{s}
, there exist:

—

a foliated chart
\mathcal{U}
for
\mathcal{W}^{s}
containing
x_{0},y_{0}
; stable plaques of
\mathcal{U}
are denoted by
\{\mathcal{U}(x)\}_{x}
;

—

a transversal
T
for
\mathcal{W}^{s}
at
x_{0}
;

—

a neighbourhood
\mathcal{V}_{y_{0}}\subset\mathcal{U}
of
y_{0}
;

—

a projection
\pi\colon\mathcal{V}_{y_{0}}\ni y\to\pi(y)\in T
along stable plaques of
\mathcal{U}
;

such that

\forall\,y\in\mathcal{V}_{y_{0}},\quad\alpha^{s}(\pi(y),y)>0.

The open set
{\mathcal{U}}
is obtained as a sufficiently small neighbourhood of a path inside
{\mathcal{W}}^{s}
from
x_{0}
to
y_{0}
(which must be trivially foliated by
{\mathcal{W}}^{s}
by Reeb’s stability theorem). See Figure
6
.

We claim that
\nu({\mathcal{V}}_{y_{0}})=0
. To see this, we consider a finite foliated atlas
{\mathcal{A}}
for
{\mathcal{W}}^{s}
such that

(1)

\overline{{\mathcal{U}}}
is included inside a foliated chart
{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}
of
{\mathcal{A}}
with
\mu(\partial{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime})=0
;

(2)

every other foliated chart of
{\mathcal{A}}
is disjoint from
\mathcal{{\mathcal{U}}}
, and its boundary has measure
0
.

To obtain such an atlas, first observe that from our construction,
{\mathcal{U}}
can be obtained so that its closure lies inside an open set
{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}
trivially foliated by
{\mathcal{W}}^{s}
. Next, we cover the compact set
{\mathbb{T}^{3}}\setminus{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}
by finitely many open balls
B_{i}
that trivialize
{\mathcal{W}}^{s}
, have
\mu(\partial B_{i})=0
and are disjoint from
{\mathcal{U}}
. Let
\eta
be the measurable partition associated to
{\mathcal{A}}
(see Remark
5.4
). By construction, stable plaques
{\mathcal{U}}(x)
are contained inside atoms of
\eta
and we have that for almost every
x\in T
,

\eta(x)\cap{\mathcal{V}}_{y_{0}}={\mathcal{U}}(x)\cap{\mathcal{V}}_{y_{0}}\mathop{\subset}{\mathcal{N}}(x),

hence
\nu_{x}^{\eta}(\eta(x)\cap{\mathcal{V}}_{y_{0}})\leq\nu_{x}^{\eta}({\mathcal{N}}(x))=0
for
\mu
-a.e.
x
. By the definition of conditional measures, this implies that
\nu({\mathcal{V}}_{y_{0}})=0
.
In other words, any point
y\in\mathcal{N}(x_{0})
has an open neighbourhood
\mathcal{V}_{y}\ni y
such that
{\mathbb{T}}^{3}\setminus\mathcal{V}_{y}
has full measure
therefore,

\mathrm{supp}(\nu)\subset{\mathbb{T}}^{3}\setminus\bigcup_{y\in\mathcal{N}(x_{0})}\mathcal{V}_{y}.

As
\mathcal{N}(x_{0})
is open and dense in
{\mathcal{W}}^{s}(x_{0})
, and
{\mathcal{W}}^{s}(x_{0})
is dense in
{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
, the set
\bigcup_{y\in\mathcal{N}(x_{0})}\mathcal{V}_{y}
is open and dense in
{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
. Thus,
\mathrm{supp}(\nu)
has empty interior.
∎

5.3.
Main technical theorem

We shall now show that our main result, Theorem
A
, can be reduced to a more technical statement involving the
Bad set
.

Theorem 5.10
.

Let
f\colon\mathbb{T}^{3}\to\mathbb{T}^{3}
be a
C^{2}
Anosov diffeomorphism, strongly partially hyperbolic with expanding center and
C^{1}
stable holonomies. Let
\mu
be an ergodic
u
-Gibbs measure. If the
Bad set
of
\mu
has zero measure then
\mu
is an SRB measure.

5.3.1.
Proof of Theorem
A
assuming Theorem
5.10

Recall that by Lemma
2.6
for every
f_{0}\in{\mathcal{A}}^{2}_{m}({\mathbb{T}^{3}})
there exists a small neighbourhood
{\mathcal{U}}(f_{0})
in
{\operatorname{Diff}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{3})}
so that every
f\in{\mathcal{U}}(f_{0})
is an Anosov diffeomorphism, strongly partially hyperbolic with expanding center and
C^{1}
stable holonomies. Let
{\mathcal{U}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}\cup_{f_{0}\in{\mathcal{A}}^{2}_{m}({\mathbb{T}^{3}})}{\mathcal{U}}(f_{0})
. Take
f\in{\mathcal{U}}
. Then
f
fulfils the assumptions of Theorem
5.10
. Assume that
E^{s}
and
E^{u}
are not jointly integrable and let
\mu
be a fully supported ergodic
u
-Gibbs measure. Then, Lemma
5.9
implies that the
Bad set
has zero measure. By Theorem
5.10
it follows that
\mu
is SRB, concluding.∎

It is important to remark that in this main technical result we
do not assume
that our
u
-Gibbs measure is fully supported. The result says that the existence of positive angles
\alpha^{s}(x,y)
for almost every
y\in{\mathcal{W}}^{s}(x)
and almost every
x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
suffices to convert
\mu
into an SRB measure.

6.
Normal forms

In this section, our goal is to prove Theorem
C
. This section is independent of the rest of the paper and we want to emphasize that we do not require any regularity of holonomies. We begin by revisiting the normal forms along one dimensional expanding foliations, as developped by Kalinin-Katok
[
25
]
. Then, using strongly that
E^{u}
is
C^{1}
along the center-unstable manifold, we extend this construction to two dimensions. In the final part of the section, we study the change of coordinates for points on the same leaf of
{\mathcal{W}}^{cu}
.

Let
f\colon{\mathbb{T}}^{3}\to{\mathbb{T}}^{3}
be a
C^{2}
Anosov diffeomorphism with a decomposition
T{\mathbb{T}}^{3}=E^{s}\oplus E^{c}\oplus E^{u}
, where
E^{c}
is uniformly expanded.

Let us recall some notations that will be frequently used in this section.

—

Given a point
x\in\mathbb{T}^{3}
we will write
\lambda^{*}_{x}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}\|Df(x)|_{E^{*}}\|
, for
*=c,u
. Observe that
\|Df^{-1}(x)|_{E^{*}}\|=(\lambda^{*}_{x_{-1}})^{-1}
.

—

Given
\ell\in{\mathbb{Z}}
we write
x_{\ell}=f^{\ell}(x)
.

—

Given
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, and
*=c,u
, we write

(19)

\lambda_{x}^{*}(n)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}\|Df^{n}(x)|_{E^{*}}\|=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\prod_{\ell=0}^{n-1}\lambda_{x_{\ell}}^{*},&\text{ when }n\geq 0;\\
\prod_{\ell=1}^{-n}\frac{1}{\lambda_{x_{-\ell}}^{*}}=\frac{1}{\lambda_{x_{n}}^{*}(-n)},&\text{ when }n<0.\end{array}\right.

6.1.
One dimensional normal forms

Given
x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
and
y\in{\mathcal{W}}^{cu}(x)
, we consider the functions

(20)

\rho_{x}^{*}(y)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}\prod_{\ell=1}^{+\infty}\frac{\lambda^{*}_{x_{-\ell}}}{\lambda^{*}_{y_{-\ell}}}=\lim_{n\to+\infty}\frac{\lambda_{y}^{*}(-n)}{\lambda_{x}^{*}(-n)},\>\>\textrm{for}\>\>*=c,u.

It follows readily from the definition that
\rho_{y}^{*}(x)=(\rho_{x}^{*}(y))^{-1}
. Furthermore, for any
z\in{\mathcal{W}}^{cu}(x)={\mathcal{W}}^{cu}(y)
, we have

(21)

\rho_{x}^{*}(z)=\rho_{y}^{*}(z)\rho_{x}^{*}(y).

The basic distortion result (Lemma
2.9
) implies that
\rho_{x}^{*}\colon{\mathcal{W}}^{cu}(x)\to(0,+\infty)
is a continuous map that depends continuously on the base point (for further details we refer to
[
25
]
).

We then define

(22)

{\mathcal{H}}^{*}_{x}(y)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}\int_{x}^{y}\rho_{x}^{*}(\hat{y})\,d\hat{y},

which gives a
C^{1}
diffeomorphism from
{\mathcal{W}}^{*}(x)
to the real line. Now, consider the inverse map
\Phi^{*}_{x}=({\mathcal{H}}^{*}_{x})^{-1}
. Here’s the proposition.

Proposition 6.1

(Kalinin-Katok – see
[
25
]
, Section 3.1)
.

For
\ast=c,u
, The family
\{\Phi^{*}_{x}\}_{x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}}
of
C^{1}
diffeomorphisms
\Phi^{*}_{x}\colon{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathcal{W}}^{*}(x)
satisfies

(1)

f\circ\Phi^{*}_{x}(s)=\Phi^{*}_{x_{1}}(\lambda^{*}_{x}s)
, for all
s\in{\mathbb{R}}
;

(2)

\Phi^{*}_{x}(0)=x
;

(3)

D\Phi^{*}_{x}(0)e_{1}=v^{*}(x)
, where
e_{1}
denotes the unitary tangent vector field of the real line
{\mathbb{R}}
.

For any
x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
and
y\in\mathcal{W}^{*}(x)
, we define the
change of charts
as

(23)

\mathcal{H}_{x,y}^{*}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}\mathcal{H}^{*}_{y}\circ(\mathcal{H}^{*}_{x})^{-1}=(\Phi^{*}_{y})^{-1}\circ\Phi^{*}_{x}\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}.

Lemma 6.2
.

For any
x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
and
y\in\mathcal{W}^{*}(x)
, and for any
s\in\mathbb{R}
, it holds

(24)

\mathcal{H}_{x,y}^{*}(s)-\mathcal{H}_{x,y}^{*}(0)=\rho_{y}^{*}(x)\cdot s.

In other words, the change of one-dimensional normal form coordinates is affine with derivative
\rho^{*}_{y}(x)
.

Proof.

Fix
s\in\mathbb{R}
, and let
z\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}(\mathcal{H}_{x}^{*})^{-1}(s)=(\mathcal{H}_{y}^{*})^{-1}({\mathcal{H}}_{x,y}^{*}(s))\in{\mathcal{W}}^{*}(x)={\mathcal{W}}^{*}(y)
. By (
22
). By differentiating
\mathcal{H}_{x,y}^{*}=\mathcal{H}_{y}^{*}\circ(\mathcal{H}_{x}^{*})^{-1}
at
s
, we get

\frac{d\mathcal{H}_{x,y}^{*}}{ds}(s)=\frac{(\mathcal{H}_{y}^{*})^{\prime}(z)}{(\mathcal{H}_{x}^{*})^{\prime}(z)}=\frac{\rho_{y}^{*}(z)}{\rho_{x}^{*}(z)}=\rho_{y}^{*}(x),

thus
\mathcal{H}_{x,y}^{*}(s)-\mathcal{H}_{x,y}^{*}(0)=\int_{0}^{s}\rho_{y}^{*}(x)\,dt=\rho_{y}^{*}(x)\cdot s
.
∎

6.2.
Two dimensional normal forms

Recall that there are unitary vector fields
{\mathbb{T}^{3}}\ni x\mapsto v^{*}(x)\in E^{*}(x)
such that

Df(x)v^{*}(x)=\lambda^{*}_{x}v^{*}(x_{1}).

Notice that since
f
is
C^{2}
, the bundle
E^{u}
is
C^{1}
inside
{\mathcal{W}}^{cu}
and since
\|v^{u}(x)\|>0
the map
x\mapsto\lambda^{u}_{x}\in{\mathbb{R}}
is
C^{1}
in restriction to center unstable manifolds.
Moreover, for a fixed
R>0
the
C^{1}
norm

\sup\{\|D(\lambda^{u}|_{{\mathcal{W}}^{cu}(x)})(y)\|:y\in{\mathcal{W}}^{cu}_{R}(x),\>x\in M\}

is bounded by some uniform constant
C=C(R,f)>0
. These results follow from
[
38
]
.

We restate here Theorem
C
, which gives a center-unstable version of Proposition
6.1
.

Theorem 6.3
.

There exists a family of
C^{1}
diffeomorphisms
\Phi_{x}\colon{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\to\mathcal{W}^{cu}(x)
, depending continuously on
x
such that:

(1)

f\circ\Phi_{x}(t,s)=\Phi_{f(x)}(\lambda^{u}_{x}t,\lambda^{c}_{x}s)
, for all
(s,t)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
;

(2)

\Phi_{x}(0,0)=x
;

(3)

D\Phi_{x}(0,0)e_{1}=v^{u}(x)
and
D\Phi_{x}(0,0)e_{2}=v^{c}(x)
;

(4)

\Phi_{x}(\cdot,\cdot)
depends continuously with the choice of
x
in the
C^{1}
-topology;

(5)

for all
s\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
\Phi_{x}({\mathbb{R}}\times\{s\})=\mathcal{W}^{u}(\Phi_{x}(0,s))
, and
\Phi_{x}(\{0\}\times{\mathbb{R}})=\mathcal{W}^{c}(x)
(see Figure
2
).

The drawback of our construction is that the natural generalization of Lemma
6.2
is no longer true, as the remark below demonstrates.

Remark 6.4
.

Note that in general, we cannot expect the change of normal charts
\mathcal{H}_{x,y}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}\Phi_{y}^{-1}\circ\Phi_{x}\colon{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
to be affine for any
x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
and
y\in\mathcal{W}^{cu}(x)
.

Suppose it were the case. We claim that it implies that for any
x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
, the center foliation
\mathcal{W}^{c}
is
C^{1}
within the center-unstable leaf
\mathcal{W}^{cu}(x)
. Indeed, for any
y\in\mathcal{W}^{cu}(x)
, by Theorem
6.3
(
5
), we have

\Phi_{x}^{-1}(\mathcal{W}^{c}(y))=\mathcal{H}_{y,x}\circ\Phi_{y}^{-1}(\mathcal{W}^{c}(y))=\mathcal{H}_{y,x}(\{0\}\times{\mathbb{R}}),

which is a straight line since the map
\mathcal{H}_{y,x}
is affine. Since two distinct center leaves cannot cross, and
\Phi_{x}^{-1}(\mathcal{W}^{c}(x))=\{0\}\times{\mathbb{R}}
is vertical, it follows that
\Phi_{x}^{-1}(\mathcal{W}^{c}(y))
is vertical for any
y\in\mathcal{W}^{cu}(x)
. In particular, since the normal chart
\Phi_{x}
is
C^{1}
, it does imply that
\mathcal{W}^{c}
is
C^{1}
within
\mathcal{W}^{cu}(x)
.

However, regularity of the center foliation
\mathcal{W}^{c}
is a rare phenomenon. For instance, in
[
20
, Lemma 3]
, it is shown that for
r\geq 2
, there exsits a
C^{1}
-open and
C^{r}
-dense set
\mathcal{V}\subset\mathcal{A}_{m}^{r}({\mathbb{T}^{3}})
of the set of
C^{r}
conservative Anosov diffeomorphisms on
{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
considered in the present work such that for
f\in\mathcal{A}_{m}^{r}({\mathbb{T}^{3}})
, the center foliation
\mathcal{W}^{c}
is Lipschitz inside the center-unstable foliation
\mathcal{W}^{cu}
if and only if
f\in\mathcal{A}_{m}^{r}({\mathbb{T}^{3}})\setminus\mathcal{V}
.

We now proceed to the proof of Theorem
6.3
.

6.2.1.
A foliated chart

A natural way of obtaining a parametrization
(t,s)
of
{\mathcal{W}}^{cu}(x)
is to consider the arc-length parameter
t
along the curve
{\mathcal{W}}^{u}(\Phi^{c}_{x}(s))
. As we shall see, it is not hard to prove that this indeed gives a
C^{1}
identification with nice properties (for instance horizontal lines are mapped onto unstable manifolds). The difficulty for proving Theorem
6.3
is that in these coordinates the map
f

does not acts linearly
. For this reason in the forthcoming paragraphs we will perform suitable reparametrizations.

More formally, let
\phi\colon{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathcal{W}}^{cu}(x)\to{\mathcal{W}}^{cu}(x)
denote the flow of the vector field
x\mapsto v^{u}(x)
. Notice that
v^{u}|_{{\mathcal{W}}^{cu}(x)}
is
C^{1}
, with its
C^{1}
norm depending continuously on
x
on compact subsets of
{\mathcal{W}}^{cu}(x)
. With this flow at hand we define a map
\Gamma_{x}\colon{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\to{\mathcal{W}}^{cu}(x)
by

\Gamma_{x}(t,s)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}\phi^{t}(\Phi^{c}_{x}(s)),\quad\forall\,(t,s)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2},

where
\{\Phi^{c}_{x}\}_{x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}}
are Kalinin-Katok’s normal forms in
{\mathcal{W}}^{c}
(see Theorem
6.3
).

Lemma 6.5
.

For each
x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
, the map
\Gamma_{x}
is a
C^{1}
diffeomorphism whose
C^{1}
norm depends continuously with
x
.

Proof.

Let us show that this defines a
C^{1}
diffeomorphism. Indeed,
\Gamma_{x}
is injective and surjective due to Lemmas
2.3
and
2.4
respectively. Therefore it suffices to prove that
\Gamma_{x}
is
C^{1}
with invertible derivative at each point.

Choosing an arbitrary coordinate system locally in
{\mathcal{W}}^{cu}(x)
we can think of
\Gamma_{x}
as a map from
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
to
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
. Now, observe that the Jacobian matrix of the map
(t,s)\mapsto\phi^{t}(\Phi^{c}_{x}(s))
is the
2\times 2
matrix whose columns are the vectors
v^{u}(\phi^{t}(\Phi^{c}_{x}(s)))
and
D\phi^{t}(\Phi^{c}_{x}(s))\frac{d}{ds}\Phi^{c}_{x}(s)
. Since
\phi^{t}
is a
C^{1}
flow and
\Phi^{c}_{x}
is a
C^{1}
curve this proves that
\Gamma_{x}
has continuous derivative. Moreover, since
v^{u}(\Phi^{c}_{x}(s))
and
\frac{d}{ds}\Phi^{c}_{x}(s)
are transverse and since
D\phi^{t}(\Phi^{c}_{x}(s))
is a linear isomorphism, we have that the vectors

v^{u}(\phi^{t}(\Phi^{c}_{x}(s)))=D\phi^{t}(\Phi^{c}_{x}(s))v^{u}(\Phi^{c}_{x}(s))\>\>\>\textrm{and}\>\>\>D\phi^{t}(\Phi^{c}_{x}(s))\frac{d}{ds}\Phi^{c}_{x}(s)

are also transverse.
Thus the Jacobian matrix of
\Gamma_{x}
is invertible, proving the assertion. The continuous dependence of the
C^{1}
norm follows from that of
v^{u}
and
\Phi^{c}
.
∎

6.2.2.
Preliminary construction

We shall start the construction of the map
\Phi
with a slight modification of the construction of the foliated chart
\Gamma_{x}
. Instead of using the arc-length parameter along unstable manifolds we shall use normal forms. This choice will allow us to perform the required reparametrization for linearizing the action of
f|_{{\mathcal{W}}^{cu}(x)}
.

Lemma 6.6
.

The map
\Psi_{x}\colon{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\to{\mathcal{W}}^{cu}(x)
given by
\Psi_{x}(t,s)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}\Phi^{u}_{\Phi^{c}_{x}(s)}(t)
is a
C^{1}
diffeomorphism, with
C^{1}
norm depending continuously from
x
.

Proof.

Notice that
\Psi_{x}
is bijective because we can define directly an inverse map. Indeed, given
y\in{\mathcal{W}}^{cu}(x)
we consider the point
y^{c}\in{\mathcal{W}}^{c}(x)
, whose existence is ensured by Lemmas
2.3
and
2.4
, such that

\{y^{c}\}={\mathcal{W}}^{u}(y)\cap{\mathcal{W}}^{c}(x).

Define then a map
\Xi_{x}\colon{\mathcal{W}}^{cu}(x)\to{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
by

\Xi_{x}(y)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}({\mathcal{H}}^{u}_{y^{c}}(y),{\mathcal{H}}^{c}_{x}(y^{c})).

From the definitions we have

\Psi_{x}\circ\Xi_{x}(y)=\Phi^{u}_{\Phi^{c}_{x}({\mathcal{H}}^{c}_{x}(y^{c}))}({\mathcal{H}}^{u}_{y^{c}}(y))=\Phi^{u}_{y^{c}}({\mathcal{H}}^{u}_{y^{c}}(y))=y.

In a similar way one sees that
\Xi_{x}\circ\Psi_{x}(t,s)=(t,s)
. It suffices then to check that
\Xi_{x}
is
C^{1}
with invertible derivative at each point.

To prove that
\Xi_{x}
is a
C^{1}
diffeomorphism then it suffices to establish that
\Xi_{x}\circ\Gamma_{x}\colon{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
is a
C^{1}
diffeomorphism. To compute the derivative of this map fix a point
y=\Gamma_{x}(t,s)\in{\mathcal{W}}^{cu}(x)
. Then, since
\Gamma_{x}
is a foliated chart, we have
y^{c}=\Gamma_{x}(0,s)
. Thus,

\Xi_{x}\circ\Gamma_{x}(t,s)=({\mathcal{H}}^{u}_{\Gamma_{x}(0,s)}(\Gamma_{x}(t,s)),{\mathcal{H}}^{c}_{x}(\Gamma_{x}(0,s))).

Note that
(t,s)\mapsto{\mathcal{H}}^{c}_{x}(\Gamma_{x}(0,s))=s
is a
C^{1}
function. Moreover, as

{\mathcal{H}}^{u}_{\Gamma_{x}(0,s)}(\Gamma_{x}(t,s))=\int_{\Gamma_{x}(0,s)}^{\Gamma_{x}(t,s)}\rho_{\Gamma_{x}(0,s)}(\Gamma_{x}(r,s))dr,

Leibniz rule will imply that
\Xi_{x}\circ\Gamma_{x}
is a
C^{1}
map as long as we prove that
(t,s)\mapsto\rho_{\Gamma_{x}(0,s)}(\Gamma_{x}(t,s))\in(0,+\infty)
is
C^{1}
. To verify this assertion, recall that

\rho_{\Gamma_{x}(0,s)}^{u}(\Gamma_{x}(t,s))=\prod_{\ell=0}^{+\infty}\frac{\|Df^{-1}(f^{-\ell}(\Gamma_{x}(t,s)))|_{E^{u}}\|}{\|Df^{-1}(f^{-\ell}(\Gamma_{x}(0,s)))|_{E^{u}}\|}.

Consider the auxiliary function
g(t,s)=\log\rho_{\Gamma_{x}(0,s)}^{u}(\Gamma_{x}(t,s))
. Then we can write

g(t,s)=\sum_{\ell=0}^{+\infty}\left(\log\lambda^{u}_{f^{-\ell-1}(\Gamma_{x}(0,s))}-\log\lambda^{u}_{f^{-\ell-1}(\Gamma_{x}(t,s))}\right).

First notice that as
\lambda^{u}_{(.)}\colon{\mathcal{W}}^{cu}\to{\mathbb{R}}
is
C^{1}
and since
d(f^{-\ell}(\Gamma_{x}(0,s)),f^{-\ell}(\Gamma_{x}(t,s)))\to 0
exponentially fast (with uniform rate, depending only on
f
), we deduce that
g
is the uniform limit on compact sets of the sequence of partial sums. Consider the function
g_{\ell}(t,s)=\log\lambda^{u}_{f^{-\ell-1}(\Gamma_{x}(t,s))}
. By the chain rule,

Dg_{\ell}(t,s)=\frac{D\lambda^{u}_{f^{-\ell-1}(\Gamma_{x}(t,s))}Df^{-\ell-1}(\Gamma_{x}(t,s))D\Gamma_{x}(t,s)}{\lambda^{u}_{f^{-\ell-1}(\Gamma_{x}(t,s))}}.

Therefore, for
(t,s)\in B_{R}(0)
there is a uniform constant
C=C(R,f)>0
so that

\|Dg_{\ell}(t,s)\|\leq C\|Df^{-\ell-1}(\Gamma_{x}(t,s))|_{E^{cu}}\|.

The right hand side above has a uniform bound decreasing exponentially as
\ell
increases for
\|Df^{-\ell}(x)|_{E^{cu}}\|\leq e^{-\chi^{c}_{2}\ell}
, for every
x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
, with
\chi_{2}^{c}>0
(recall our notations from §
2.1.3
). Thus,

\|Dg_{\ell}(t,s)\|\leq Ce^{-\chi^{c}_{2}\ell}.

An analogous estimate holds for the function
\tilde{g}_{\ell}(t,s)=\log\lambda^{u}_{\Gamma_{x}(0,s)}
. This proves that the derivative of the truncated series defining
g
also converges uniformly on compact sets. By elementary calculus (see for instance Proposition 1.41 of
[
1
]
) this proves that
g
is
C^{1}
, and therefore we conclude that
(t,s)\mapsto\rho_{\Gamma_{x}(0,s)}^{u}(\Gamma_{x}(t,s))\in(0,+\infty)
is
C^{1}
, as desired. To complete the proof of the lemma, observe that the derivative of
\Xi_{x}\circ\Gamma_{x}
is an upper triangular matrix with non-zero entries in the diagonal. By the inverse function theorem, as
\Xi_{x}\circ\Gamma_{x}
is bijective, this proves that this map is in fact a
C^{1}
diffeomorphism. This ends the proof of the lemma.
∎

6.2.3.
A reparametrization function

Here again, the diffeomorphism
\Psi_{x}
constructed in Lemma
6.6
does not satisfy all the requirements we need. Indeed, to obtain condition (
1
) from Theorem
6.3
it is necessary to perform a suitable reparametrization. The design of such a map is the content of next lemma.

Lemma 6.7
.

For each
x\in{\mathbb{T}}^{3}
, there exists a
C^{1}
function
\beta_{x}\colon{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{+}
such that

(25)

Df(\Phi^{c}_{x}(s))\cdot\beta_{x}(s)v^{u}\left(\Phi^{c}_{x}(s)\right)=\lambda^{u}_{x}\beta_{x_{1}}(\lambda^{c}_{x}s)v^{u}\left(\Phi^{c}_{x_{1}}(\lambda^{c}_{x}s)\right).

Proof.

Let us fix a point
x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
. Consider the sequence of functions
{\mathbb{R}}\ni s\mapsto\psi^{x}_{-\ell}(s)\in{\mathcal{W}}^{c}(x_{-\ell})
where

\psi^{x}_{-\ell}(s)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}\Phi^{c}_{x_{-\ell}}\left((\lambda^{c}_{x_{-\ell}}\cdots\lambda^{c}_{x_{-1}})^{-1}s\right).

For coherence of notation also make the convention that
\psi^{x}_{0}(s)=\Phi^{c}_{x}(s)
.

Remark 6.8
.

Note that by property of Kalinin-Katok’s normal forms, we have that if
y=\psi^{x}_{0}(s)=\Phi^{c}_{x}(s)
for some
s\in{\mathbb{R}}
and
\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}

f^{-\ell}(y)=\psi^{x}_{-\ell}(s).

Using this sequence we define
h_{n,x}\colon{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
,

h_{n,x}(s)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}\left(\log\lambda^{u}_{\psi^{x}_{-\ell}(s)}-\log\lambda^{u}_{x_{-\ell}}\right).

Notice that
y\mapsto\lambda^{u}_{y}
is uniformly bounded from above and from below, and the lower bound is larger than
1
. Thus

|\log\lambda^{u}_{\psi^{x}_{-\ell}(s)}-\log\lambda^{u}_{x_{-\ell}}|\leq|\lambda^{u}_{\psi^{x}_{-\ell}(s)}-\lambda^{u}_{x_{-\ell}}|.

Now, as
y\mapsto\lambda^{c}_{y}
is also uniformly bounded with a lower bound larger than
1
, since the family
\{\Phi^{c}_{x}\}
is continuous in the
C^{1}
topology and
\Phi^{c}_{x_{-\ell}}(0)=x_{-\ell}
it follows that
d(\psi^{x}_{-\ell}(s),x_{-\ell})\to 0
exponentially fast with uniform constants (keep in mind Remark
6.8
). Moreover, as
y\mapsto\lambda^{u}_{y}
is uniformly
C^{1}
inside
{\mathcal{W}}^{cu}
there exists some constant
C>0
such that

|\lambda^{u}_{\psi^{x}_{-\ell}(s)}-\lambda^{u}_{x_{-\ell}}|\leq Cd(\psi^{x}_{-\ell}(s),x_{-\ell}).

This proves that
h_{n,x}
converges uniformly to a continuous function
h_{x}
. We define
\beta_{x}(s)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}e^{h_{x}(s)}
. Observe that with Notation (
20
), we can write

(26)

\beta_{x}(s)=\prod_{\ell=1}^{+\infty}\frac{\lambda^{u}_{\psi^{x}_{-\ell}(s)}}{\lambda^{u}_{x_{-\ell}}}=\rho_{\Phi_{x}^{c}(s)}^{u}(x)=\frac{1}{\rho_{x}^{u}(\Phi_{x}^{c}(s))}.

We claim that
\lambda^{u}_{\Phi^{c}_{x}(s)}\beta_{x}(s)=\lambda^{u}_{x}\beta_{x_{1}}(\lambda^{c}_{x}s)
. This formula immediately gives us (
25
). Indeed note that by property of Kalinin-Katok’s normal forms

\displaystyle Df(\Phi^{c}_{x}(s))\cdot v^{u}(\Phi^{c}_{x}(s))

\displaystyle=\lambda^{u}_{\Phi^{c}_{x}(s)}v^{u}(f(\Phi^{c}_{x}(s)))

\displaystyle=\lambda^{u}_{\Phi^{c}_{x}(s)}v^{u}(\Phi^{c}_{x_{1}}(\lambda^{c}_{x}s)),

and combining this equality with the claim provides (
25
).

To prove the claim we first remark that

\psi^{x_{1}}_{-\ell}(\lambda^{c}_{x}s)=\Phi^{c}_{x_{-\ell+1}}\left((\lambda^{c}_{x_{-\ell+1}}\cdots\lambda^{c}_{x_{-1}}\lambda^{c}_{x})^{-1}\lambda^{c}_{x}s\right)=\psi^{x}_{-\ell+1}(s).

We deduce that

\beta_{x_{1}}(\lambda^{c}_{x}s)=\prod_{\ell=1}^{+\infty}\frac{\lambda^{u}_{\psi^{x_{1}}_{-\ell}(\lambda^{c}_{x}s)}}{\lambda^{u}_{x_{-\ell+1}}}=\prod_{\ell=1}^{\infty}\frac{\lambda^{u}_{\psi^{x}_{-\ell+1}(s)}}{\lambda^{u}_{x_{-\ell+1}}}=\frac{\lambda^{u}_{\Phi^{c}_{x}(s)}}{\lambda^{u}_{x}}\beta_{x}(s),

proving our claim.

To finish the lemma it remains to show that
\beta_{x}
is a
C^{1}
function, depending continuously (with respect to the local
C^{1}
topology) on
x
. With that goal in mind we compute

\frac{d}{ds}\psi^{x}_{-\ell}(s)=(\lambda^{c}_{x_{-\ell}}\cdots\lambda^{c}_{x_{-1}})^{-1}D\Phi^{c}_{x_{-\ell}}\left((\lambda^{c}_{x_{-\ell}}\cdots\lambda^{c}_{x_{-1}})^{-1}s\right)e_{1}.

Applying thus the chain rule we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{d}{ds}\lambda^{u}_{\psi^{x}_{-\ell}(s)}

\displaystyle=D(\lambda^{u}_{\psi^{x}_{-\ell}(s)})\frac{d}{ds}\psi^{x}_{-\ell}(s)

\displaystyle=(\lambda^{c}_{x_{-\ell}}\cdots\lambda^{c}_{x_{-1}})^{-1}D(\lambda^{u}_{\psi^{x}_{-\ell}(s)})D\Phi^{c}_{x_{-\ell}}\left((\lambda^{c}_{x_{-\ell}}\cdots\lambda^{c}_{x_{-1}})^{-1}s\right)e_{1},

where
D(\lambda^{u}_{y})
denotes the derivative of the function
y\mapsto\lambda^{u}_{y}
at
y
. As
(\lambda^{c}_{x_{-\ell}}\cdots\lambda^{c}_{x_{-1}})^{-1}\to 0
exponentially fast with uniform constants, the left hand side above also vanishes exponentially fast because
\lambda^{u}_{y}
is
C^{1}
and
x\mapsto\max_{|s|\leq 1}\|D\Phi^{c}_{x}(s)\|
is uniformly bounded. As a consequence the sequence

h_{n,x}^{\prime}(s)=\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}\frac{\frac{d}{ds}\lambda^{u}_{\psi^{x}_{-\ell}(s)}}{\lambda^{u}_{{}_{\psi^{x}_{-\ell}(s)}}}

is uniformly bounded by a convergent geometric series, because
\lambda^{u}_{y}
is lower bounded by a constant larger than
1
.
Since
h_{n,x}\to h_{x}
uniformly on compact sets, by elementary calculus (see for instance
[
1
]
Proposition 1.41) this proves that
h_{x}
is
C^{1}
with
h_{x}^{\prime}=\lim_{n\to+\infty}h^{\prime}_{n,x}
.
Moreover, the uniform estimates obtained show that the
C^{1}
norm of
f
changes continuously with
x
. This completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

6.2.4.
Defining the normal form

With Lemmas
6.6
and
6.7
at hand we are in position to define our two dimensional parametrization of center-unstable manifolds
{\mathcal{W}}^{cu}(x)
. For each
x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
we consider

\Phi_{x}\colon\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}{\mathbb{R}}^{2}&\to&{\mathcal{W}}^{cu}(x),\\
(t,s)&\mapsto&\Psi_{x}(\beta_{x}(s)t,s)=\Phi^{u}_{\Phi_{x}^{c}(s)}(\beta_{x}(s)t).\end{array}\right.

Notice that, by Lemma
6.7
,
s\mapsto\beta_{x}(s)
is a
C^{1}
positive function. It follows that
(t,s)\mapsto(\beta_{x}(s)t,s)
is a
C^{1}
diffeomorphism of
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
. Therefore, applying Lemma
6.6
we obtain that
\Phi_{x}
is a
C^{1}
diffeomorphism. We denote
{\mathcal{H}}_{x}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}\Phi_{x}^{-1}
.

Proof of Theorem
6.3
.

Properties (
2
)-(
4
) are automatic from the construction of
\Phi_{x}
. To prove (
1
) we apply Proposition
6.1
and obtain

f(\Phi_{x}(t,s))=f\left(\Phi^{u}_{\Phi^{c}_{x}(s)}(\beta_{x}(s)t)\right)=\Phi^{u}_{f\circ\Phi^{c}_{x}(s)}\left(\lambda^{u}_{\Phi^{c}_{x}(s)}\beta_{x}(s)t\right).

Now, equality (
25
) implies that

\beta_{x}(s)\lambda^{u}_{\Phi^{c}_{x}(s)}v^{u}(f\circ\Phi^{c}_{x}(x))=\lambda^{u}_{x}\beta_{x_{1}}(\lambda^{c}_{x}s)v^{u}\left(\Phi^{c}_{x_{1}}(\lambda^{c}_{x}s)\right).

Proposition
6.1
applied with
\Phi^{c}_{x}
shows that
f\circ\Phi^{c}_{x}(s)=\Phi^{c}_{x_{1}}(\lambda^{c}_{x}s)
, where
x_{1}=f(x)
. Combining we get
\beta_{x}(s)\lambda_{\Phi^{c}_{x}(s)}=\lambda^{u}_{x}\beta_{x_{1}}(\lambda^{c}_{x}s)
. One deduces then

f(\Phi_{x}(t,s))=\Phi^{u}_{\Phi^{c}_{x_{1}}(\lambda^{c}_{x}s)}\left(\lambda^{u}_{x}\beta_{x_{1}}(\lambda^{c}_{x}s)t\right).

The very definition of
\Phi_{x}
says that

\Phi^{u}_{\Phi^{c}_{x_{1}}(\lambda^{c}_{x}s)}\left(\lambda^{u}_{x}\beta_{x_{1}}(\lambda^{c}_{x}s)t\right)=\Phi_{x_{1}}(\lambda^{u}_{x}t,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\lambda^{c}_{x}s}).\qed

Remark 6.9
.

It follows readily from our construction that the inverse map
{\mathcal{H}}_{x}\colon{\mathcal{W}}^{cu}(x)\to{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
sends each unstable manifold
{\mathcal{W}}^{u}(y)
, for
y\in{\mathcal{W}}^{c}(x)
onto the horizontal line
\{s={\mathcal{H}}_{x}^{c}(y)\}
.

6.3.
Change of normal form coordinates

As in (
23
) and Remark
6.4
, for any
x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
and
y\in\mathcal{W}^{cu}(x)
, we define the change of charts

(27)

\mathcal{H}_{x,y}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}\mathcal{H}_{y}\circ\mathcal{H}_{x}^{-1}=\Phi_{y}^{-1}\circ\Phi_{x}\colon\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathbb{R}^{2}&\to&\mathbb{R}^{2},\\
(t,s)&\mapsto&(h^{1}_{x,y}(t,s),h^{2}_{x,y}(t,s)).\end{array}\right.

The function
h^{2}_{x,y}
only depends on the second coordinate, as demonstrated by the following lemma.

Lemma 6.10
.

For any
x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
and
y\in\mathcal{W}^{cu}(x)
, the diffeomorphism
\mathcal{H}_{x,y}
preserves the foliation of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
into horizontal lines
\{s=\mathrm{cst}\}
.

Proof.

This is a straightforward consequence of the fact that normal forms sends horizontal lines to unstable manifolds (see Remark
6.9
). More precisely, given
s\in{\mathbb{R}}
, using item (5) of Theorem
6.3
we have

{\mathcal{W}}^{u}(\Phi^{c}_{x}(s))=\Phi_{x}({\mathbb{R}}\times\{s\}).

Now define
\hat{y}={\mathcal{W}}^{c}(y)\cap{\mathcal{W}}^{u}(\Phi^{c}_{x}(s))
(recall Lemma
2.4
). Applying item (5) of Theorem
6.3
once more we obtain

{\mathcal{H}}_{y}({\mathcal{W}}^{u}(\hat{y}))={\mathbb{R}}\times\{{\mathcal{H}}^{c}_{y}(\hat{y})\}

Since
{\mathcal{W}}^{u}(\hat{y})={\mathcal{W}}^{u}(\Phi^{c}_{x}(s))
, this shows that

{\mathcal{H}}_{x,y}({\mathbb{R}}\times\{s\})={\mathbb{R}}\times\{{\mathcal{H}}^{c}_{y}(\hat{y})\},

which concludes the proof.
∎

Lemma 6.11
.

The diffeomorphism
\mathcal{H}_{x,y}(\cdot,\cdot)
depends continuously in the
C^{1}
topology with the pair
(x,y)
,
x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
and
y\in\mathcal{W}^{cu}(x)
. Moreover, for any
\bar{x}\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
, and for
(\bar{t},\bar{s})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
,

(1)

\mathcal{H}_{x,y}(t,s)\to(\bar{t},\bar{s})
as
(x,y)\to(\bar{x},\bar{x})
and
(t,s)\to(\bar{t},\bar{s})
;

(2)

D\mathcal{H}_{x,y}(t,s)\to\mathrm{Id}_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}
as
(x,y)\to(\bar{x},\bar{x})
and
(t,s)\to(\bar{t},\bar{s})
.

Proof.

It follows immediately from Theorem
6.3
(
4
), since
\mathcal{H}_{x,y}=\mathcal{H}_{y}\circ\mathcal{H}_{x}^{-1}
, and

D\mathcal{H}_{x,y}(t,s)=D\mathcal{H}_{y}(\mathcal{H}_{x}^{-1}(t,s))\circ D\mathcal{H}_{x}^{-1}(t,s)
, with

D\mathcal{H}_{x}(t,s)\to D\mathcal{H}_{\bar{x}}(\bar{t},\bar{s}),

as
x\to\bar{x}
and
(t,s)\to(\bar{t},\bar{s})
, and

D\mathcal{H}_{y}(\mathcal{H}_{x}^{-1}(t,s))\to D\mathcal{H}_{\bar{x}}(\mathcal{H}_{\bar{x}}^{-1}(\bar{t},\bar{s}))=(D\mathcal{H}_{\bar{x}}^{-1}(\bar{t},\bar{s}))^{-1},

as
(x,y)\to(\bar{x},\bar{x})
and
(t,s)\to(\bar{t},\bar{s})
.
∎

6.3.1.
Change of normal charts for two points in the same center leaf

We first study the change of normal charts between two points of the same center leaf.

Lemma 6.12
.

Let
x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
and
y\in\mathcal{W}^{c}(x)
. For any
(t,s)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
, it holds

(1)

h^{1}_{x,y}(t,s)=\rho^{u}_{y}(x)t
;

(2)

h^{2}_{x,y}(s)={\mathcal{H}}^{c}_{x,y}(s)=\rho^{c}_{y}(x)s+{\mathcal{H}}^{c}_{y}(x)
.

Proof.

We start by considering the function
h^{1}_{x,y}
. As
x
and
y
belong to the same center leaf, for any
s\in\mathbb{R}
, it holds
h^{1}_{x,y}(0,s)=0
, and

\Phi^{c}_{x}(s)=\Phi_{x}(0,s)=\Phi_{y}(h^{1}_{x,y}(0,s),h^{2}_{x,y}(s))=\Phi_{y}(0,h^{2}_{x,y}(s))=\Phi^{c}_{y}(h^{2}_{x,y}(s)),

hence by (
24
),

h^{2}_{x,y}(s)=(\Phi_{y}^{c})^{-1}\circ\Phi_{x}^{c}(s)=\mathcal{H}_{x,y}^{c}(s).

The second claimed equality follows from Lemma
6.2
.

Now, let us fix
(t,s)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, and denote
t^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}h^{1}_{x,y}(t,s)
and
s^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}h^{2}_{x,y}(s)
for simplicity.
By definition, and as
\Phi^{c}_{y}(h^{2}_{x,y}(s))=\Phi^{c}_{x}(s)
, we obtain

\Phi^{u}_{\Phi^{c}_{x}(s)}(\beta_{x}(s)t)=\Phi_{x}(t,s)=\Phi_{y}(t^{\prime},s^{\prime})=\Phi^{u}_{\Phi^{c}_{y}(s^{\prime})}(\beta_{y}(s^{\prime})t^{\prime}),

hence

(28)

\beta_{x}(s)t=\beta_{y}(s^{\prime})t^{\prime}.

On the other hand, by (
26
) we have

\displaystyle\beta_{y}(s^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\rho^{u}_{\Phi^{c}_{y}(s^{\prime})}(y)=\rho^{u}_{\Phi^{x}_{x}(s)}(y)

(29)

\displaystyle=\rho^{u}_{\Phi^{c}_{x}(s)}(x)\rho^{u}_{x}(y)=\beta_{x}(s)\rho^{u}_{x}(y),

where in the second line we applied (
21
). Combining this with (
28
) we deduce
t^{\prime}=\rho^{u}_{y}(x)t
, concluding.
∎

6.3.2.
Change of normal forms for two points in the same unstable leaf

Let us now study the change of normal charts
\mathcal{H}_{x,x^{\prime}}\colon(t,s)\mapsto(h^{1}_{x,x^{\prime}}(t,s),h^{2}_{x,x^{\prime}}(s))
between two points
x,x^{\prime}
of the same unstable leaf. Unlike the previous case, here we do not get an affine map.

Lemma 6.13
.

Let
x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
and
x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{W}^{u}(x)
. Then, there exists a
C^{1}
function
a_{x,x^{\prime}}:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
such that for any
(t,s)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, it holds

h^{1}_{x,x^{\prime}}(t,s)=\rho^{u}_{x^{\prime}}(x)t+a_{x,x^{\prime}}(s).

Proof.

Consider
(t,s)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, and let us abbreviate
t^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}h^{1}_{x,x^{\prime}}(t,s)
,
s^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}h^{2}_{x,x^{\prime}}(s)
,
y=y(s)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}\Phi_{x}^{c}(s)
,
y^{\prime}=y^{\prime}(s)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}\Phi_{x^{\prime}}^{c}(s^{\prime})
, and
z=z(t,s)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}\Phi_{x}(t,s)=\Phi_{x^{\prime}}(t^{\prime},s^{\prime})
.
Recall that
z=\Phi_{x}(t,s)=\Phi^{u}_{y}(\beta_{x}(s)t)
. By (
22
), we thus obtain

(30)

\beta_{x}(s)t=\int_{y}^{z}\rho_{y}^{u}(\hat{y})\,d\hat{y}.

Similarly,
z=\Phi_{x^{\prime}}(t^{\prime},s^{\prime})=\Phi^{u}_{y^{\prime}}(\beta_{x^{\prime}}(s^{\prime})t^{\prime})
, and

(31)

\beta_{x}(s^{\prime})t^{\prime}=\int_{y^{\prime}}^{z}\rho_{y^{\prime}}^{u}(\hat{y})\,d\hat{y},

Observe that
y^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{W}}^{u}(y)
.
By (
21
), for any point
\hat{y}\in{\mathcal{W}}^{u}(y)={\mathcal{W}}^{u}(y^{\prime})
, we have
\rho_{y^{\prime}}^{u}(\hat{y})=\rho_{y}^{u}(\hat{y})\rho_{y^{\prime}}^{u}(y)
. Hence,

\displaystyle\beta_{x^{\prime}}(s^{\prime})t^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\int_{y^{\prime}}^{z}\rho_{y^{\prime}}^{u}(\hat{y})\,d\hat{y}=\int_{y^{\prime}}^{z}\rho_{y^{\prime}}^{u}(y)\rho_{y}^{u}(\hat{y})\,d\hat{y}

\displaystyle=\rho_{y^{\prime}}^{u}(y)\left(\int_{y^{\prime}}^{z}\rho_{y}^{u}(\hat{y})\,d\hat{y}\right)=\rho_{y^{\prime}}^{u}(y)\left(\int_{y^{\prime}}^{y}\rho_{y}^{u}(\hat{y})\,d\hat{y}+\int_{y}^{z}\rho_{y}^{u}(\hat{y})\,d\hat{y}\right)

\displaystyle=\rho_{y^{\prime}}^{u}(y)\beta_{x}(s)t+\rho_{y^{\prime}}^{u}(y)\int_{y^{\prime}}^{y}\rho_{y}^{u}(\hat{y})\,d\hat{y},

where in the last equality we used (
30
). We conclude that

t^{\prime}=\rho_{y^{\prime}}(y)\frac{\beta_{x}(s)}{\beta_{x^{\prime}}(s^{\prime})}\cdot t+\frac{\rho_{y^{\prime}}^{u}(y)}{\beta_{x^{\prime}}(s^{\prime})}\int_{y^{\prime}}^{y}\rho_{y}^{u}(\hat{y})\,d\hat{y}.

Set
a_{x,x^{\prime}}(s)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}\frac{\rho_{y^{\prime}}^{u}(y)}{\beta_{x^{\prime}}(s^{\prime})}\int_{y^{\prime}}^{y}\rho_{y}^{u}(\hat{y})\,d\hat{y}
. This is the translation part of the change of coordinates. Recall that

\displaystyle\beta_{x}(s)=\rho_{\Phi_{x}^{u}(s)}^{u}(x)=\rho_{y}^{u}(x).

Similarly,

\beta_{x^{\prime}}(s^{\prime})=\rho_{\Phi_{x^{\prime}}^{u}(s^{\prime})}^{u}(x^{\prime})=\rho_{y^{\prime}}^{u}(x^{\prime})=(\rho_{x^{\prime}}^{u}(y^{\prime}))^{-1}.

Therefore, by (
21
), it holds

\displaystyle\rho_{y^{\prime}}^{u}(y)\frac{\beta_{x}(s)}{\beta_{x^{\prime}}(s^{\prime})}=\rho_{y}^{u}(x)\rho_{y^{\prime}}^{u}(y)\rho_{x^{\prime}}^{u}(y^{\prime})=\rho_{y}^{u}(x)\rho_{x^{\prime}}^{u}(y)=\rho_{x^{\prime}}^{u}(x).

We conclude that
t^{\prime}=\rho_{x^{\prime}}^{u}(x)\cdot t+a_{x,x^{\prime}}(s)
. Moreover,

\displaystyle a_{x,x^{\prime}}(s)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}\frac{\rho_{y^{\prime}}^{u}(y)}{\beta_{x^{\prime}}(s^{\prime})}\int_{y^{\prime}}^{y}\rho_{y}^{u}(\hat{y})\,d\hat{y}=\frac{\rho_{x^{\prime}}^{u}(x)}{\beta_{x}(s)}\int_{y^{\prime}}^{y}\rho_{y}^{u}(\hat{y})\,d\hat{y}

\displaystyle=\frac{\rho_{x^{\prime}}^{u}(x)}{\rho_{y}^{u}(x)}\int_{y^{\prime}}^{y}\rho_{y}^{u}(\hat{y})\,d\hat{y}=\rho_{x^{\prime}}^{u}(y)\int_{y^{\prime}}^{y}\rho_{y}^{u}(\hat{y})\,d\hat{y}=\int_{y^{\prime}}^{y}\rho_{y}^{u}(\hat{y})\rho_{x^{\prime}}^{u}(y)\,d\hat{y}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{W}^{u}(y^{\prime},y)}\rho_{x^{\prime}}^{u}(\hat{y})\,d\hat{y},

where in the last integral,
\hat{y}
ranges over the segment
\mathcal{W}^{u}(y^{\prime},y)\subset\mathcal{W}^{u}(y)
of unstable manifold connecting the points
y^{\prime}
and
y
.
∎

Let us now study the map
h^{2}_{x,x^{\prime}}\colon{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
. The first step is to characterize it in terms of unstable holonomies.

Lemma 6.14
.

Fix
x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
and
x^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{W}}^{u}(x)
and let
H^{u}_{x,x^{\prime}}:{\mathcal{W}}^{c}(x)\to{\mathcal{W}}^{c}(x^{\prime})
denote the unstable holonomy map. Define the map
L^{u}_{x,x^{\prime}}:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
by

L^{u}_{x,x^{\prime}}(s)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}{\mathcal{H}}^{c}_{x^{\prime}}\circ H^{u}_{x,x^{\prime}}\circ\Phi^{c}_{x}(s).

Then, we have
h^{2}_{x,x^{\prime}}(s)=L^{u}_{x,x^{\prime}}(s)
.

Proof.

Consider an
s\in{\mathbb{R}}
and define
y=\Phi^{c}_{x}(s)
and
y^{\prime}=H^{u}_{x,x^{\prime}}(y)
. Set
s^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}{\mathcal{H}}^{c}_{x^{\prime}}(y^{\prime})
. Notice that, by definition,
s^{\prime}=L^{u}_{x,x^{\prime}}(s)
. By Remark
6.9
we know that
\Phi_{x}({\mathbb{R}}\times\{s\})={\mathcal{W}}^{u}(y)
, and similarly
\Phi_{x^{\prime}}
sends
{\mathbb{R}}\times\{s^{\prime}\}
onto
{\mathcal{W}}^{u}(y^{\prime})
. Since
{\mathcal{W}}^{u}(y)={\mathcal{W}}^{u}(y^{\prime})
this shows that

{\mathcal{H}}_{x^{\prime}}\circ\Phi_{x}({\mathbb{R}}\times\{s\})={\mathbb{R}}\times\{s^{\prime}\},

and therefore
h^{2}_{x,x^{\prime}}(s)=s^{\prime}
.
∎

We now prove that unstable holonomies, when conjugated by normal forms (as in the previous lemma), become a linear maps of the real line.

Lemma 6.15
.

Fix
x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
and
x^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{W}}^{u}(x)
. Then,
L^{u}_{x,x^{\prime}}(s)=\rho^{c}_{x^{\prime}}(x)s.

Proof.

For ease of notation in this proof, we will denote
L(s)=L^{u}_{x,x^{\prime}}(s)
. By definition, we have that

\Phi^{c}_{x^{\prime}}\circ L(s)=H^{u}_{x,x^{\prime}}\circ\Phi^{c}_{x}(s).

Now, consider an integer
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
and define the functions
g_{k}\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R},s\mapsto\lambda_{x}^{c}(k)\cdot s
(recall Notation (
19
)). Similarly let
g^{\prime}_{k}:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}},s\mapsto\lambda^{c}_{x^{\prime}}(k)\cdot s
. By applying
f^{k}
to both sides of the above equation and using the equivariance properties of normal forms and holonomy maps, we obtain

\Phi^{c}_{x^{\prime}_{k}}(g^{\prime}_{k}\circ L(s))=H^{u}_{x_{k},x^{\prime}_{k}}\circ\Phi^{c}_{x_{k}}(g_{k}(s)).

Taking derivatives with respect to
s\in\mathbb{R}
on both sides we get

(32)

D\Phi_{x_{k}^{\prime}}^{c}(g_{k}^{\prime}(s))\lambda_{x^{\prime}}^{c}(k)\frac{dL}{ds}(s)=DH_{x_{k},x_{k}^{\prime}}^{u}(\Phi_{x_{k}}^{c}(g_{k}(s)))D\Phi_{x_{k}}^{c}(g_{k}(s))\lambda_{x}^{c}(k).

Recall that
\mathrm{det}\ D\Phi_{x_{k}}^{c}(g_{k}(s))=\frac{1}{\rho_{x_{k}}^{c}(\Phi_{x_{k}}^{c}(g_{k}(s)))}
, and
\mathrm{det}\ D\Phi_{x_{k}^{\prime}}^{c}(g_{k}^{\prime}(s))=\frac{1}{\rho_{x_{k}^{\prime}}^{c}(\Phi_{x_{k}^{\prime}}^{c}(g_{k}^{\prime}(s)))}
.
Passing to the determinant in (
32
), we deduce that

(33)

\frac{dL}{ds}(s)=\frac{\rho_{x_{k}^{\prime}}^{c}(\Phi_{x_{k}^{\prime}}^{c}(g_{k}^{\prime}(s)))}{\rho_{x_{k}}^{c}(\Phi_{x_{k}}^{c}(s_{k}(s)))}\cdot\frac{\lambda_{x}^{c}(k)}{\lambda_{x^{\prime}}^{c}(k)}\cdot\mathrm{det}\ DH_{x_{k},x_{k}^{\prime}}^{u}(\Phi_{x_{k}}^{c}(g_{k}(s))).

We now let
k\to-\infty
. By compactness, up to considering a subsequence, we can assume that
x_{k},x_{k}^{\prime}\to p\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
as
k\to-\infty
. Moreover,
g_{k}(s),g_{k}^{\prime}(s)\to 0
as
k\to-\infty
, and by the properties of
\Phi_{(\cdot)}^{c}(\cdot)
stated in Proposition
6.1
, we have
\Phi_{x_{k}}^{c}(g_{k}(s)),\Phi_{x_{k}^{\prime}}^{c}(g_{k}^{\prime}(s))\to\Phi_{q}^{c}(0)=q
as
k\to-\infty
. By continuity of
\rho_{(\cdot)}^{c}(\cdot)
, we deduce that

\lim_{k\to-\infty}\frac{\rho_{x_{k}^{\prime}}^{c}(\Phi_{x_{k}^{\prime}}^{c}(g_{k}^{\prime}(s)))}{\rho_{x_{k}}^{c}(\Phi_{x_{k}}^{c}(g_{k}(s)))}=\frac{\rho_{q}^{c}(q)}{\rho_{q}^{c}(q)}=1.

Since the holonomy maps
H_{x_{k},x_{k}^{\prime}}^{u}
converge uniformly to
H_{q,q}^{u}=\mathrm{Id}|_{\mathcal{W}^{c}(q)}
in the
C^{1}
topology as
k\to-\infty
, we also have
\lim_{k\to-\infty}\mathrm{det}\ DH_{x_{k},x_{k}^{\prime}}^{u}(\Phi_{x_{k}}^{c}(g_{k}(s)))=1
.

By (
33
), (
19
) and (
20
), we conclude that

\frac{dL}{ds}(s)=\lim_{k\to-\infty}\frac{\lambda_{x}^{c}(k)}{\lambda_{x^{\prime}}^{c}(k)}=\prod_{\ell=1}^{+\infty}\frac{\lambda_{x_{-\ell}^{\prime}}^{c}}{\lambda_{x_{-\ell}}^{c}}=\rho_{x^{\prime}}^{c}(x).

This completes the proof.
∎

7.
Leaf-wise quotient measures

Let us denote by
{\mathcal{M}}({\mathbb{R}})
the set of locally finite Borel measures on the real line. Using the families of parametrizations
\{\Phi_{x}\},\{\Phi^{c}_{x}\}_{x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}}
we will construct a family of elements of
{\mathcal{M}}({\mathbb{R}})
which will be the main object of study in this paper. We summarize the result of the construction with the following statement.

Theorem 7.1
.

Let
f\in{\mathcal{A}}^{2}({\mathbb{T}^{3}})
be an Anosov diffeomorphism with expanding center. Let
\mu
be an ergodic
u
-Gibbs measure of
f
. Then, there exists a family
\{\hat{\nu}^{c}_{x}\}_{x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}}\subset{\mathcal{M}}({\mathbb{R}})
of lacally finite Borel measures on the real line with the following property: if, for
\mu
almost every
x
, the measure
\hat{\nu}^{c}_{x}
is proportional to the Lebesgue measure of
{\mathbb{R}}
then
\mu
is an SRB measure.

The family
\{\hat{\nu}^{c}_{x}\}_{x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}}
is referred to as
leaf-wise quotient measures
. Once Theorem
7.1
is established, our main result is reduced to prove that, for
\mu
almost every
x
, each
\hat{\nu}^{c}_{x}
is a translation-invariant measure on the real line. The proposition below summarizes the properties of the family
\{\hat{\nu}^{c}_{x}\}_{x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}}\subset{\mathcal{M}}({\mathbb{R}})
that enable us to achieve this result.

Proposition 7.2

(Basic moves)
.

The family
\{\hat{\nu}^{c}_{x}\}_{x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}}\subset{\mathcal{M}}({\mathbb{R}})
satisfies the following properties on a full measure subset
\Lambda
of
{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
, which is
{\mathcal{W}}^{u}
-saturated. For every
x\in\Lambda

(1)

if
y\in{\mathcal{W}}^{c}(x)\cap\Lambda
then
\hat{\nu}^{c}_{x}\propto({\mathcal{H}}^{c}_{x,y})_{*}\hat{\nu}^{c}_{y}
, where
{\mathcal{H}}^{c}_{x,y}:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
is the change of normal form coordinates on the manifold
{\mathcal{W}}^{c}(x)
, which is an affine map.

(2)

\hat{\nu}^{c}_{f(x)}\propto(\Lambda^{c}_{x})_{*}\hat{\nu}^{c}_{x}
, where
\Lambda^{c}_{x}:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
is the linear map
\Lambda^{c}_{x}(s)=\lambda^{c}_{x}s
.

(3)

if
x^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{W}}^{u}(x)
then
\hat{\nu}^{c}_{x^{\prime}}\propto(L_{x,x^{\prime}})_{*}\hat{\nu}^{c}_{x}
, where
L_{x,x^{\prime}}:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
is the linear map
L_{x,x^{\prime}}(s)=\rho^{c}_{x^{\prime}}(x)s
.

The remainder of this section is dedicated to proving Theorem
7.1
and Proposition
7.2
. Thus, we fix
f\in{\mathcal{A}}^{2}({\mathbb{T}^{3}})
and
\mu
an ergodic
u
-Gibbs measure.

7.1.
Conditional measures of
\mu
in normal forms

We will show that when we push the conditional measures of
\mu
along center unstable manifolds using the normal forms and subsequently disintegrate this measure along the horizontal lines (corresponding to unstable manifolds), we obtain measures
proportional
to the Lebesgue measure. his fact plays a fundamental role in the proofs of Theorem
7.1
and Proposition
7.2
.

7.1.1.
Some preliminary facts about the conditional measures
\mu^{cu}_{n,x}

Let us start by recalling the notation from §
2.5.1
and stating some basic lemmas that will be useful throughout this section. Start with
\xi^{cu}_{0}
, an increasing measurable partition subordinate to the uniformly expanded foliation
\mathcal{W}^{cu}
. When we apply the dynamics, we obtain a sequence
\xi^{cu}_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}f^{n}(\xi^{cu}_{0})
of measurable partitions that remain subordinate to
\mathcal{W}^{cu}
. Moreover, for every
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, we have
\xi^{cu}_{n+1}\prec\xi^{cu}_{n}
. Now consider
\{\mu^{cu}_{n,x}\}_{x\in\mathbb{T}^{3}}
to be the disintegration of
\mu
with respect to the partition
\xi^{cu}_{n}
. Similar to Lemma
2.15
, we find that the following emphsuperposition property holds for
\mu
-a.e.
x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
:

(34)

\textrm{if}\>m>n,\>\textrm{then}\>\mu^{cu}_{n,x}(A)=\frac{\mu^{cu}_{m,x}(A)}{\mu^{cu}_{m,x}(\xi^{cu}_{n}(x))},

for every measurable set
A\subset\xi^{cu}_{n}(x)
.
Furthermore, as
\mu
is
f
-invariant, the distintegrations
\{\mu^{cu}_{n,x}\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}},\,x\in\mathbb{T}^{3}}
satisfy:

Lemma 7.3
.

For every
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and for
\mu
-almost every
x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
, we have

\mu^{cu}_{n+1,f(x)}=f_{*}\mu^{cu}_{n,x}.

Proof.

The proof follows a similar approach to that of Lemma
2.12
.
∎

By intersecting with the center foliation
\mathcal{W}^{c}
, or unstable foliation
\mathcal{W}^{u}
, we can refine each partition
\xi^{cu}_{n}
,
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
. More precisely, for
\star=c,u
, we consider a partition
\xi^{cu}_{n}\prec\xi^{\star}_{n}=\{\xi^{\star}_{n}(y)\}_{y\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}}
, with

\xi^{\star}_{n}(y)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}\mathcal{W}^{\star}(y)\cap\xi^{cu}_{n}(y),\quad\forall\,y\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}.

By Remark
2.13
, we obtain:

Lemma 7.4
.

For
\star=c,u
, the family of sets
\xi^{\star}_{n}
is a measurable partition subordinate to
\mathcal{W}^{\star}
.

For every
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and for
\mu
-almost every
x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
, we define
\zeta^{\star}_{n}(x)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}\mathcal{H}_{x}(\xi^{\star}_{n}(x))\mathop{\subset}{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
, and
\nu_{n,x}^{cu}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}(\mathcal{H}_{x})_{*}\mu^{cu}_{n,x}
. Note that due to item (
1
) of Theorem
6.3
, for each
x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
, it holds

(35)

N_{x}\circ\mathcal{H}_{x}|_{\mathcal{W}^{cu}(x)}=\mathcal{H}_{f(x)}\circ f|_{\mathcal{W}^{cu}(x)},\quad\text{where }N_{x}\colon(t,s)\mapsto(\lambda_{x}^{u}t,\lambda_{x}^{c}s).

As a result of (
35
) and Lemma
7.3
, we conclude:

Lemma 7.5
.

For every
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and for
\mu
-almost every
x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
, it holds

\nu^{cu}_{n+1,f(x)}=(N_{x})_{*}\nu^{cu}_{n,x}.

7.1.2.
Disintegration of the conditional measures in normal forms

As in §
2.5.1
when we disintegrate
\mu^{cu}_{n,x}
along the atoms
\{\xi^{u}_{n}(y)\}_{y\in\xi^{cu}_{n}(x)}
we get a probability measure
\mu^{c}_{n,x}
over
\xi^{c}_{n}(x)
and a family
\{\mu^{u}_{n,y}\}_{y\in\xi^{cu}_{n}(x)}
, each of which is a probability measure on
\xi^{u}_{n}(y)
.

Now, let
{\mathcal{H}}_{x}:{\mathcal{W}}^{cu}(x)\to{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
be the inverse map of the normal form coordinate chart. Define the measures

\nu^{\star}_{n,x}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}({\mathcal{H}}_{x})_{*}\mu^{\star}_{n,x},\>\>\>\textrm{for}\>\>\>\star=c,cu,

and

\nu^{u}_{n,s}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}({\mathcal{H}}_{x})_{*}\mu^{u}_{n,s},\>\>\>\textrm{for each}\>\>\>s={\mathcal{H}}^{c}_{x}(y),\>\>\>\textrm{with}\>\>\>y\in\xi^{c}_{n}(x).

A remarkable property of
u
-Gibbs measures is that their conditional measures along unstable manifolds in normal forms are
proportional
to Lebesgue.

Lemma 7.6
.

For
\mu
-a.e.
x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
and
\nu_{n,x}^{cu}
-a.e.
(t,s)\in\zeta_{n}^{cu}(x)
, there exists a constant
\gamma_{n,x}(s)>0
(independent of
t
) such that for every Borel set
B\subset\zeta^{u}_{n}(\Phi^{c}_{x}(s))
, the following holds

\nu^{u}_{n,s}(B)=\gamma_{n,x}(s)\operatorname{Leb}(B).

Proof.

Given that
\mu
is a
u
-Gibbs measure, for
\mu
-a.e.
x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
, and
\mu_{n,x}^{cu}
-a.e.
y\in\xi_{n}^{cu}(x)
, the measure
\mu_{n,y}^{u}
is absolutely continuous. Its density, up to a constant, is given by (referring to Notation (
20
))

\rho_{y}^{u}\colon\xi_{n}^{u}(y)\ni z\mapsto\rho_{y}^{u}(z)=\prod_{j=0}^{+\infty}\frac{\|Df^{-1}(f^{-j}(z))|_{E^{u}}\|}{\|Df^{-1}(f^{-j}(y))|_{E^{u}}\|}.

Let
(t,s)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}\mathcal{H}_{x}(y)
, so that
\nu_{n,s}^{u}=(\mathcal{H}_{x})_{*}\mu_{n,y}^{u}
. Restricted to
{\mathbb{R}}\times\{s\}
,
\Phi_{x}=\mathcal{H}_{x}^{-1}
is given by the map
\Phi^{u}_{\Phi_{x}^{c}(s)}(\beta_{x}(s)t)
, for some
\beta_{x}(s)\in{\mathbb{R}}
. Therefore, up to a constant that depends only on
s
, the density of
\nu_{n,s}^{u}=(\mathcal{H}_{x})_{*}\mu_{n,y}^{u}
is given by

\tilde{\rho}_{s}^{u}\colon t\mapsto\rho_{y}^{u}(\Phi_{\Phi_{x}^{c}(s)}^{u}(t))D\Phi_{\Phi_{x}^{c}(s)}^{u}(t).

Since
D\Phi_{\Phi_{x}^{c}(s)}^{u}(t)=(D\mathcal{H}_{y}^{u})^{-1}(\Phi_{\Phi_{x}^{c}(s)}^{u}(t))=\rho_{y}^{u}(\Phi_{\Phi_{x}^{c}(s)}^{u}(t))^{-1}
, hence
\tilde{\rho}_{s}^{u}
is constant.
∎

7.2.
Construction of leaf-wise quotient measures

The measures we define here are similar to the quotient measures
\nu^{c}_{n,x}
, but we will use unstable segments of a fixed length together with a normalization that allows for stabilization when
n
is sufficiently large large enough (see Lemma
7.8
below). Let
I\mathop{\subset}{\mathbb{R}}
be any bounded interval centered at
0
. It determines the length of unstable segments used in defining the leaf-wise quotient measures. Given a bounded Borel set
B\mathop{\subset}{\mathbb{R}}
and a
\mu
-typical point
x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
there exists
n_{0}=n_{0}(x,I,B)
such that for every
n\geq n_{0}
,
I\times B\mathop{\subset}\zeta_{n}^{cu}(x)
and
I\times[-1,1]\mathop{\subset}\zeta_{n}^{cu}(x)
: see Lemma
2.18
. We first show that the choice of length of unstable segments, and thus the interval
I\subset{\mathbb{R}}
will not modify the definition.

Lemma 7.7
.

For any other interval
J\subset{\mathbb{R}}
centered at
0
, we have

\frac{\nu_{n,x}^{cu}(I\times B)}{\nu_{n,x}^{cu}(I\times[-1,1])}=\frac{\nu_{n,x}^{cu}(J\times B)}{\nu_{n,x}^{cu}(J\times[-1,1])},

for every sufficiently large
n
.

Proof.

Consider real numbers
\alpha
and
\beta
such that the affine map
\psi(t)=\alpha t+\beta
satisfies
\psi(I)=J
. Then, applying the disintegration in conditional measures and using Lemma
7.6
we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{\nu_{n,x}^{cu}(J\times B)}{\nu_{n,x}^{cu}(J\times[-1,1])}

\displaystyle=\frac{\int_{B}\nu^{u}_{n,s}(J)d\nu^{c}_{n,x}(s)}{\int_{[-1,1]}\nu^{u}_{n,s}(J)d\nu^{c}_{n,x}(s)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\int_{B}\gamma_{n,x}(s)\operatorname{Leb}(\alpha I+\beta)d\nu^{c}_{n,x}(s)}{\int_{[-1,1]}\gamma_{n,x}(s)\operatorname{Leb}(\alpha I+\beta)d\nu^{c}_{n,x}(s)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\int_{B}\gamma_{n,x}(s)\operatorname{Leb}(I)d\nu^{c}_{n,x}(s)}{\int_{[-1,1]}\gamma_{n,x}(s)\operatorname{Leb}(I)d\nu^{c}_{n,x}(s)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\int_{B}\nu^{u}_{n,s}(I)d\nu^{c}_{n,x}(s)}{\int_{[-1,1]}\nu^{u}_{n,s}(I)d\nu^{c}_{n,x}(s)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\nu_{n,x}^{cu}(I\times B)}{\nu_{n,x}^{cu}(I\times[-1,1])}.

This equality holds for sufficiently large
n
, ensuring that
J\times B
and
J\times[-1,1]
are both subsets of
\zeta_{n}^{cu}(x)
. Notice that this can always be achieved due to Lemma
2.18
. This completes the proof.
∎

Given
B\subset{\mathbb{R}}
a bounded Borel measurable subset, and given
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
we define the following number

(36)

\hat{\nu}_{n,x}^{c}(B)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}\frac{\nu_{n,x}^{cu}(I\times B)}{\nu_{n,x}^{cu}(I\times[-1,1])}.

We now use the superposition property to show that this sequence stabilizes for large
n
.

Lemma 7.8
.

For
\mu
-almost every
x\in\mathbb{T}^{3}
, every bounded Borel set
B\subset{\mathbb{R}}
, and every pair of integers
m>n\geq n_{0}(x,I,B)\in{\mathbb{N}}
we have
\hat{\nu}^{c}_{m,x}(B)=\hat{\nu}^{c}_{n,x}(B)
.

Proof.

Firstly, observe that for each pair
m>n
, there exists a
\mu
-full measure set of points
x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
for which
\mu^{cu}_{m,x}(\xi^{cu}_{n,x}(x))>0
. This follows from the superposition property (
34
). By taking the countable intersection of all these sets, and applying (
34
) again, we deduce that for every bounded Borel set
B\subset{\mathbb{R}}
, and
m,n\geq n_{0}(x,I,B)
, so that
I\times B,I\times[-1,1]\mathop{\subset}\zeta_{n}^{cu}(x)\mathop{\subset}\zeta_{m}^{cu}(x)
, the following holds

\displaystyle\hat{\nu}^{c}_{n,x}(B)

\displaystyle=\frac{\mu^{cu}_{n,x}(\Phi_{x}(I\times B))}{\mu^{cu}_{n,x}(\Phi_{x}(I\times[-1,1]))}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mu^{cu}_{m,x}(\Phi_{x}(I\times B))}{\mu^{cu}_{m,x}(\xi^{cu}_{n,x}(x))}\times\frac{\mu^{cu}_{m,x}(\xi^{cu}_{n,x}(x))}{\mu^{cu}_{m,x}(\Phi_{x}(I\times B))}=\hat{\nu}^{c}_{m,x}(B).\qed

Hence, the construction above defines a family of measures on
{\mathbb{R}}
.

Definition 7.9

(Leaf-wise quotient measure)
.

For
\mu
-a.e.
x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
, let
\hat{\nu}^{c}_{x}
be the unique locally finite Borel measure defined in
{\mathbb{R}}
so that, for each bounded Borel
B\subset{\mathbb{R}}
, one has

\hat{\nu}^{c}_{x}(B)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}\hat{\nu}^{c}_{n_{0}(x,B),x}(B).

By Lemma
7.8
the right hand side above is well defined.

Remark 7.10

(Choice of normalization)
.

By construction, we have that
\hat{\nu}^{c}_{x}([-1,1])=1
.

Remark 7.11
.

By construction, for
\mu
-a.e.
x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
,
\hat{\nu}_{x}^{c}
gives positive measure to any open neighbourhood of
0
. This follows from the definition and Corollary
2.16

7.3.
Obtaining the SRB property: Proof of Theorem
7.1

To complete the proof of Theorem
7.1
we assume that for
\mu
almost every
x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
the measure
\hat{\nu}^{c}_{x}
is proportional to Lebesgue on
{\mathbb{R}}
. To conclude that
\mu
is an SRB measure it suffices to show that the conditional measures
\mu^{cu}_{0,x}
are absolutely continuous with respect to
\operatorname{Leb}_{{\mathcal{W}}^{cu}(x)}
for
\mu
almost every
x
. To achieve this goal, consider
a<0<b
with
b-a
large enough so that, denoting
I=[a,b]
, we have

\xi^{cu}_{0}(x)\subset\Phi_{x}(I\times I),\>\textrm{for}\>\mu\>\textrm{almost every}\>x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}.

This can be done because the atoms of
\xi^{cu}_{0}
have uniformly bounded diameter and the
C^{1}
norm of
\Phi_{x}
depends continuously on
x
. Let
R=\max_{x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}}\operatorname{diam}(\Phi_{x}(I\times I))
. Then, applying Lemma
2.18
with this number we get that for
\mu
almost every
x
, there exists
n_{0}(x,I)
such that if
n\geq n_{0}(x,I)
then

(1)

I\subset\zeta^{c}_{n}(x)

(2)

I\times\{s\}\subset\zeta^{u}_{n}(s)
, for every
s\in I
.

Now, given such
x
and
n\geq n_{0}(x,I)
take
s\in I
. We observe

\displaystyle 1=\nu^{u}_{n,s}(\zeta^{u}_{n}(s))

\displaystyle\geq\nu^{u}_{n,s}(I\times\{s\})

\displaystyle=\gamma_{n,x}(s)\operatorname{Leb}(I)

(37)

\displaystyle=\gamma_{n,x}(s)(b-a).

Therefore,

0<\gamma_{n,x}(s)<\frac{1}{b-a}.

We claim that
\nu^{c}_{n,x}|_{I}
is absolutely continuous with respect to the leaf-wise quotient measure
\hat{\nu}^{c}_{x}|_{I}
. Indeed, let us denote for simplicity
\alpha=(\nu^{cu}_{n,x}(I\times[-1,1]))^{-1}
. Then, if
B\subset I
we have

\displaystyle\hat{\nu}^{c}_{x}(B)

\displaystyle=\alpha\nu^{cu}_{n,x}(I\times B)=\alpha\int_{B}\nu^{u}_{n,s}d\nu^{c}_{n,x}(s)

(38)

\displaystyle=\alpha\operatorname{Leb}(I)\int_{B}\gamma_{n,x}(s)d\nu^{c}_{n,x}(s).

Since
\gamma_{n,x}>0
if
\hat{\nu}^{c}_{x}(B)=0
then
\nu^{c}_{n,x}(B)=0
, which proves our claim. Since, by assumption,
\hat{\nu}^{c}_{x}\propto\operatorname{Leb}
we conclude that indeed
\nu^{c}_{n,x}|_{I}
is absolutely continuous with
\operatorname{Leb}|_{I}
. As
d\nu^{cu}_{n,x}(t,s)=d\nu^{u}_{n,s}(t)d\nu^{c}_{n,x}(s)
, it follows immediately from Lemma
7.6
that
\nu^{cu}_{n,x}|_{I\times I}
is absolutely continuous with respect to the two dimensional Lebesgue measure
\operatorname{Leb}|_{I\times I}
. Since
\Phi_{x}
is
C^{1}
, we get that
\mu^{cu}_{n,x}|_{\Phi_{x}(I\times I)}
is absolutely continuous with respect to
\operatorname{Leb}_{{\mathcal{W}}^{cu}(x)}
. By the superposition property we deduce that
\mu^{cu}_{0,x}
is absolutely continuous, as desired. The theorem is proved. ∎

7.4.
Basic moves for leaf-wise measures

We now proceed with the proof of Proposition
7.2
. Our goal is to investigate how the measures
\hat{\nu}^{c}_{x}
change as we move the base point
x
. In this analysis, the understanding of how the normal form coordinates change with the base point on the same center-unstable leaf plays a crucial role.

\Phi_{y}
\Phi_{x}
y
x
\mathcal{W}^{c}(x)
\hat{\nu}_{y}^{c}
\hat{\nu}_{x}^{c}

Figure 7.
Moving the base point within a central leaf changes the leaf-wise measure by an affine map up to renormalization.

7.4.1.
First basic move: moving along center manifolds

In the following lemma we show that for points on the same center leaf there exists an affine map that makes the corresponding leaf-wise quotient measure proportional to each other: see Figure
7
. It is important to note that the derivative of this affine map is determined by normal form coordinates along the center manifold.

Lemma 7.12
.

For
\mu
-a.e.
x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
, and
y\in\mathcal{W}^{c}(x)
, we have

\hat{\nu}_{y}^{c}\propto(h^{2}_{x,y})_{*}\hat{\nu}_{x}^{c},

where
h^{2}_{x,y}\colon{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
is the affine map defined by
s\mapsto\rho_{y}^{c}(x)\cdot s+\mathcal{H}_{y}^{c}(x)
, corresponding to the action of the change of normal forms on the second coordinate given by Lemma
6.12
.

Proof.

Let
I
be a bounded interval centered at
0
and
B
be a bounded Borel subset of
{\mathbb{R}}
. Consider
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, large enough so that
y\in\xi^{cu}_{n}(x)
and

\Phi_{y}(I\times B)\cup\Phi_{y}(I\times[-1,1])\mathop{\subset}\xi^{cu}_{n}(y)=\xi^{cu}_{n}(x).

This is possible after Lemma
2.18
. Recall the normal form coordinate change
{\mathcal{H}}_{x,y}(t,s)={\mathcal{H}}_{y}\circ\Phi_{x}=(h^{1}_{x,y}(t,s),h^{2}_{x,y}(s))
, which is given by (see Lemma
6.12
)

h^{1}_{x,y}(t,s)=\rho^{u}_{y}(x)t\>\>\>\textrm{and}\>\>\>h^{2}_{x,y}(s)=\rho^{c}_{y}(x)s+{\mathcal{H}}^{c}_{y}(x).

Let
\tilde{B}=h^{2}_{x,y}(B)
,
I_{1}=[-1,1]
and
\tilde{I}_{1}=h^{2}_{x,y}(I_{1})
. Then, we can apply Lemma
7.6
as before to argue that

\displaystyle\hat{\nu}^{c}_{x}(B)

\displaystyle=\frac{\mu^{cu}_{n,x}(\Phi_{x}(I\times B))}{\mu^{cu}_{n,x}(\Phi_{x}(I\times I_{1}))}=\frac{\mu^{cu}_{n,y}(\Phi_{y}\circ{\mathcal{H}}_{x,y}(I\times B))}{\mu^{cu}_{n,y}(\Phi\circ{\mathcal{H}}_{x,y}(I\times I_{1}))}

\displaystyle=\frac{\nu^{cu}_{n,y}(\rho^{u}_{y}(x)I\times\tilde{B})}{\nu^{cu}_{n,y}(\rho^{u}_{y}(x)I\times\tilde{I}_{1})}=\frac{\int_{\tilde{B}}\nu^{u}_{n,s}(\rho^{u}_{y}(x)I)d\nu^{c}_{n,x}(s)}{\int_{\tilde{I}_{1}}\nu^{u}_{n,s}(\rho^{u}_{y}(x)I)d\nu^{c}_{n,x}(s)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\int_{\tilde{B}}\nu^{u}_{n,s}(I)d\nu^{c}_{n,x}(s)}{\int_{\tilde{I}_{1}}\nu^{u}_{n,s}(I)d\nu^{c}_{n,x}(s)}=\frac{\nu^{cu}_{n,x}(I\times\tilde{B})}{\nu^{cu}_{n,x}(I\times\tilde{I}_{1})}

\displaystyle=\alpha(x,y)\hat{\nu}^{c}_{y}(\tilde{B}),

where
\alpha(x,y)=\frac{\nu^{cu}_{n,x}(I\times I_{1})}{\nu^{cu}_{n,x}(I\times\tilde{I}_{1})}
. This is exactly the desired conclusion.
∎

\Phi_{x}
\Phi_{f(x)}
x
\mathcal{W}^{c}(x)
f(x)
f
\mathcal{W}^{c}(f(x))
\hat{\nu}_{x}^{c}
\hat{\nu}_{f(x)}^{c}

Figure 8.
Applying the dynamics to the base point changes the leaf-wise measure by a linear map given by the differential of
f
along
E^{c}
up to renormalization.

7.4.2.
Second basic move: applying the dynamics

When we push
x
to
f(x)
the change on the measures is linear and the slope of the linear map is given by the derivative at
x
along the center direction. Recall that for
*=c,u
, we denote by
\Lambda_{x}^{*}
the linear map
t\mapsto\lambda_{x}^{*}\cdot t
(where
\lambda_{x}^{*}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}\|Df(x)|_{E^{*}}\|
).

Lemma 7.13
.

For
\mu
-a.e.
x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
, we have

\hat{\nu}_{f(x)}^{c}\propto(\Lambda_{x}^{c})_{*}\hat{\nu}_{x}^{c}.

Proof.

Let
I
be a bounded interval centered at
0
and
B
be a bounded Borel subset of
{\mathbb{R}}
. Fix
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
large so that
I\times B,I\times[-1,1]\mathop{\subset}\zeta^{cu}_{n+1}(f(x))
(see Lemma
2.18
). By definition, we have

\hat{\nu}_{n+1,f(x)}^{c}(B)=\frac{\nu_{n+1,f(x)}^{cu}(I\times B)}{\nu_{n+1,f(x)}^{cu}(I\times[-1,1])},

and by Lemmas
7.5
and
7.6

\displaystyle\frac{\nu_{n+1,f(x)}^{cu}(I\times B)}{\nu_{n+1,f(x)}^{cu}(I\times[-1,1])}

\displaystyle=\frac{\nu_{n,x}^{cu}(N_{x}^{-1}(I\times B))}{\nu_{n,x}^{cu}(N_{x}^{-1}(I\times[-1,1]))}

\displaystyle=\frac{\int_{(\Lambda^{c}_{x})^{-1}(B)}\nu^{u}_{n,s}((\lambda^{u}_{x})^{-1}I)d\nu^{c}_{n,x}(s)}{\int_{(\Lambda^{c}_{x})^{-1}([-1,1])}\nu^{u}_{n,s}((\lambda^{u}_{x})^{-1}I)d\nu^{c}_{n,x}(s)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\nu^{cu}_{n,x}(I\times(\Lambda^{c}_{x})^{-1}(B)}{\nu^{cu}_{n,x}(I\times(\Lambda^{c}_{x})^{-1}([-1,1]))}

(39)

\displaystyle=\alpha(x)\hat{\nu}^{c}_{x}((\Lambda^{c}_{x})^{-1}(B)),

where
\alpha(x)=\hat{\nu}^{c}_{x}((\Lambda^{c}_{x})^{-1}([-1,1]))^{-1}
. We deduce that

\hat{\nu}_{f(x)}^{c}(B)\propto(\Lambda_{x}^{c})_{\ast}\hat{\nu}_{x}^{c}(B).\qed

\Phi_{x}
\Phi_{x^{\prime}}
\hat{\nu}_{x}^{c}
\hat{\nu}_{x^{\prime}}^{c}
x
x^{\prime}
\mathcal{W}^{u}(x)

Figure 9.
Moving the base point within an unstable leaf changes the leaf-wise measure by a linear map up to renormalization.

7.4.3.
Third basic move: moving along unstable manifolds

For points on the same strong unstable leaf the change is also linear, but the slope is determined by the derivative of the unstable holonomy map, which coincides with the derivative of the linear map giving the action of the change in normal form coordinates on the second variable. The lemma below completes the proof of Proposition
7.2
.

Lemma 7.14
.

For
\mu
-a.e.
x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
, and
x^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{W}}^{u}(x)
, it holds

\hat{\nu}_{x}^{c}\propto(L^{u}_{x,x^{\prime}})_{*}\hat{\nu}_{x^{\prime}}^{c},

for the linear map
L^{u}_{x,x^{\prime}}\colon s\mapsto\rho_{x^{\prime}}^{c}(x)\cdot s
corresponding to the action of the change of normal coordinates on the second variable given by Lemmas
6.14
and
6.15
.

Proof.

For
\mu
-a.e.
x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
, and
x^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{W}}^{u}(x)
, we proceed as follows. Let
I
be any bounded interval centered at
0
and
B
be any bounded Borel subset of
{\mathbb{R}}
. Then, there exists
n
large so that
x^{\prime}\in\xi_{n}^{cu}(x)
and
I\times B,I\times[-1,1]\times B\mathop{\subset}\zeta^{cu}_{n}(x)
. This is possible by Lemma
2.18
.
As before, let
{\mathcal{H}}_{x,x^{\prime}}(t,s)=(h^{1}_{x,x^{\prime}}(t,s),h^{2}_{x,x^{\prime}}(s))
be the change in normal form coordinate. By Lemmas
6.13
,
6.14
and
6.15
we have

h^{1}_{x,x^{\prime}}(t,s)=\rho^{u}_{x^{\prime}}(x)t+a_{x,x^{\prime}}(s),\>\>\>\textrm{and}\>\>\>h^{2}_{x,x^{\prime}}(s)=\rho^{c}_{x^{\prime}}(x)s.

Now, introduce
\tilde{B}=h^{2}_{x,x^{\prime}}(B)
,
\tilde{I}_{1}=h^{2}_{x,x^{\prime}}(I_{1})
, where
I_{1}=[-1,1]
. By Lemma
7.6
:

\displaystyle\hat{\nu}^{c}_{x^{\prime}}(B)

\displaystyle=\frac{\mu^{cu}_{n,x}(\Phi_{x}\circ{\mathcal{H}}_{x,x^{\prime}}(I\times B))}{\mu^{cu}_{n,x}(\Phi_{x}\circ{\mathcal{H}}_{x,x^{\prime}}(I\times I_{1}))}

\displaystyle=\frac{\nu^{cu}_{n,x}(h^{1}_{x,x^{\prime}}(I\times B)\times\tilde{B})}{\nu^{cu}_{n,x}(h^{1}_{x,x^{\prime}}(I\times I_{1})\times\tilde{I}_{1})}

\displaystyle=\frac{\int_{\tilde{B}}\nu^{u}_{n,s}(\rho^{u}_{x^{\prime}}(x)I+a_{x,x^{\prime}}(\rho^{c}_{x}(x^{\prime})s))d\nu^{c}_{n,x}(s)}{\int_{\tilde{I}_{1}}\nu^{u}_{n,s}(\rho^{u}_{x^{\prime}}(x)I+a_{x,x^{\prime}}(\rho^{c}_{x}(x^{\prime})s))d\nu^{c}_{n,x}(s)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\int_{\tilde{B}}\nu^{u}_{n,s}(I)d\nu^{c}_{n,x}(s)}{\int_{\tilde{I}_{1}}\nu^{u}_{n,s}(I)d\nu^{c}_{n,x}(s)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\nu^{cu}_{n,x}(I\times\tilde{B})}{\nu^{cu}_{n,x}(I\times\tilde{I}_{1})}

(40)

\displaystyle=\alpha(x,x^{\prime})\hat{\nu}^{c}_{x}(\tilde{B}),

where
\alpha(x,x^{\prime})=\left(\hat{\nu}^{c}_{x}(\tilde{I}_{1})\right)^{-1}
. This completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

8.
Invariance by affine maps

Once we have constructed the
leaf-wise quotient measures

\{\hat{\nu}^{c}_{x}\}_{x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}}
, Theorem
7.1
tells us that the proof of Theorem
5.10
reduces to show that
\hat{\nu}^{c}_{x}
is a multiple of the Lebesgue measure of the real line. As in
[
7
]
this can be achieved by proving that
\hat{\nu}^{c}_{x}
is, for many points
x
, invariant by affine maps with controlled slope and small translational part. More precisely, the lemma below is the analogue of
[
7
, Proposition 7.1]
in our context.

Lemma 8.1
.

There exist constants
M_{0}>0
and
\delta_{0}\in(0,1)
such that for every
\varepsilon>0
sufficiently small one can find a compact set
G(\varepsilon)\subset\mathbb{T}^{3}
so that
\mu(G(\varepsilon))\geq\delta_{0}
and for every
p\in G(\varepsilon)
there exists an affine map
\psi\colon{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
satisfying

(1)

\frac{1}{M_{0}}<|\psi^{\prime}(0)|<M_{0}
;

(2)

\frac{\varepsilon}{M_{0}}<|\psi(0)|<\varepsilon M_{0}
;

(3)

\psi_{*}\hat{\nu}_{p}^{c}\propto\hat{\nu}_{p}^{c}
.

Furthermore, writing
G_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}\{p\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}:p\in G(\frac{1}{N})\text{ for infinitely many }N\in{\mathbb{N}}\}
, we have
\mu(G_{0})\geq\delta_{0}
.

Exactly as in
[
7
]
, the lemma above implies Theorem
5.10
. The proof is a direct adaptation of
[
25
, Lemma 3.10]
and
[
7
, Lemma 7.3]
; for the convenience of the reader, we provide this beautiful argument below and we refer to
[
7
,
25
]
for other applications of the same argument.

Proof of Theorem
5.10
assuming Lemma
8.1
.

Let
\mathrm{Aff}({\mathbb{R}})
denote the group of invertible affine maps of
{\mathbb{R}}
, and for
p\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
, let
\mathcal{A}(p)\subset\mathrm{Aff}({\mathbb{R}})
be the subgroup of affine maps
\psi\colon{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
such that
\psi_{*}\hat{\nu}_{p}^{c}\propto{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\hat{\nu}_{p}^{c}}
.

We claim that
{\mathcal{A}}(p)
is a closed subgroup of
\mathrm{Aff}({\mathbb{R}})
. Let
\psi_{n}\in{\mathcal{A}}(p)
converge to
\psi\in\mathrm{Aff}({\mathbb{R}})
. Since each element of
\mathrm{Aff}({\mathbb{R}})
is a homeomorphism of the real line and since the convergence in
\mathrm{Aff}({\mathbb{R}})
implies converge in the compact-open topology we have, for each continuous function with compact support
\phi\colon{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
that

\int\phi\circ\psi_{n}\,d\hat{\nu}^{c}_{p}\to\int\phi\circ\psi\,d\hat{\nu}^{c}_{p}.

This implies that
(\psi_{n})_{*}\hat{\nu}^{c}_{p}\to\psi_{*}\hat{\nu}^{c}_{p}
. On the other hand, for each
n
we have

(41)

(\psi_{n})_{*}\hat{\nu}^{c}_{p}=c_{n}\hat{\nu}^{c}_{p},

for some constant
c_{n}
, which can therefore be obtained by

c_{n}=\frac{\hat{\nu}^{c}_{p}(\psi_{n}^{-1}(K))}{\hat{\nu}^{c}_{p}(K)},

for any measurable set
K\subset{\mathbb{R}}
with finite positive measure with respect to both
\hat{\nu}^{c}_{p}
and
(\psi_{n})_{*}\hat{\nu}^{c}_{p}
. Now, as all the measures are locally finite we can require further that such compact set
K
is also a continuity set (of finite measure) for
\psi_{*}\hat{\nu}^{c}_{p}
. In particular, since
(\psi_{n})_{*}\hat{\nu}^{c}_{p}(K)\to\psi_{*}\hat{\nu}^{c}_{p}(K)
, we deduce that
c_{n}
converges to some positive real number
c
. We deduce from (
41
) and uniqueness of limits that
\psi_{*}\hat{\nu}^{c}_{p}=c\hat{\nu}^{c}_{p}
. This proves that
\psi\in{\mathcal{A}}(p)
and establishes our claim. This implies in particular that
{\mathcal{A}}(p)
is a Lie group.

By Lemma
8.1
, for every
p\in G_{0}
,
\mathcal{A}(p)
contains a sequence
(\psi_{j})_{j\in{\mathbb{N}}}
of elements
\psi_{j}\colon t\mapsto\lambda_{j}t+v_{j}
, with
v_{j}\neq 0
,
\lim_{j\to+\infty}v_{j}=0
, and
\lim_{j\to+\infty}\lambda_{j}=\lambda
, for some
\lambda\neq 0
. In particular,
\mathcal{A}(p)
contains the homothety
h_{\lambda}\colon t\mapsto\lambda t
, and
(h_{\lambda}^{-1}\circ\psi_{j})_{j\in{\mathbb{N}}}
converges to the identity within
\mathcal{A}(p)
. Therefore,
\mathcal{A}(p)
is not discrete and must be of dimension
1
or
2
. This implies that the identity component
\mathcal{A}^{0}(p)\subset\mathcal{A}(p)
contains a one-parameter subgroup of
\mathrm{Aff}({\mathbb{R}})
: such a group consists of translations, or is conjugate to homothety.

We now claim that the groups
{\mathcal{A}}^{0}(p)
are isomorphic for a.e
p\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
. Indeed, recall the linear map
\Lambda^{c}_{p}\colon{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
given by
\Lambda^{c}_{p}(t)=\lambda^{c}_{p}t
, where
\lambda^{c}_{p}=\|Df(p)|_{E^{c}}\|
. Then, it follows from Lemma
7.13
that
\psi\in{\mathcal{A}}^{0}(p)
if, and only if,

\Lambda^{c}_{p}\circ\psi\circ(\Lambda^{c}_{p})^{-1}\in{\mathcal{A}}^{0}(f(p))
, proving our claim.

Since isomorphisms classes of closed subgroups of
\mathrm{Aff}({\mathbb{R}})
form a separable space and since
\mu(G_{0})>0
, the claim then follows
by
ergodicity. In particular, for
\mu
-a.e.
p\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
,
\mathcal{A}^{0}(p)
contains a one-parameter subgroup of
\mathrm{Aff}({\mathbb{R}})
.

Assume by contradiction that
\mathcal{A}^{0}(p)
were conjugate to homothety for a positive measure set of
p\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
. Then, by ergodicity, for
\mu
-a.e.
p\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
, the action of
\mathcal{A}^{0}(p)
on
{\mathbb{R}}
would have a unique fixed point
t(p)\in{\mathbb{R}}
. Since
{\mathcal{A}}^{0}(p)
contains affine maps with arbitrarily small (and non-zero) translational part, we have
t(p)\neq 0
for a set of positive measure.

Besides that, as we observed above

{\mathcal{A}}^{0}(f^{n}(p))=\{\Lambda^{c}_{p,n}\circ\psi\circ(\Lambda^{c}_{p,n})^{-1}:\psi\in{\mathcal{A}}^{0}(p)\},
where
\Lambda^{c}_{p,n}(t)=\lambda^{c}_{p}(n)t
(recall (
2
)). As a consequence we deduce

(42)

|t(f^{n}(p))|=\lambda^{c}_{p}(n)\cdot|t(p)|.

Consider a positive measure compact subset
K
of
G_{0}
where the measurable function
p\mapsto t(p)
is continuous and bounded. By Poincaré recurrence for some
p\in K
we have
p_{n_{k}}=f^{n_{k}}(p)\in K
for infinitely many iterates
n_{k}\in{\mathbb{N}}
. By compactness, we can assume
p_{n_{k}}\to q\in K
. However, (
42
) is incompatible with the boundedness of
t|_{K}
. We have thus reached a contradiction.

Therefore,
\mathcal{A}^{0}(p)
contains the group of translations, for
\mu
-a.e.
p\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
. For
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
p\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
, we let
g=g_{t}\colon s\mapsto s+t
and
c(p,t)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}\hat{\nu}_{p}^{c}([-t-1,-t+1])
, so that

(43)

\frac{dg_{*}\hat{\nu}_{p}^{c}}{d\hat{\nu}_{p}^{c}}=c(p,t).

Let us see some properties of the function
(p,t)\mapsto c(p,t)
.
Firstly, for
\mu
-a.e.
p\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
,
\mathcal{A}^{0}(p)
contains all translations. This implies that
\hat{\nu}_{p}^{c}
has no atom, which implies that
c(p,\cdot)
is continuous. We claim that

(44)

c(p,t)=c(p_{n},\lambda^{c}_{p}(n)t),

where
p_{n}=f^{n}(p)
. To prove the claim, consider
\psi=\Lambda^{c}_{p,n}\circ g\circ(\Lambda^{c}_{p,n})^{-1}
and observe that
\psi(s)=s+\lambda^{c}_{p}(n)t
. From the definition it follows that

c(p_{n},\lambda^{c}_{p}(n))=\hat{\nu}^{c}_{p_{n}}(\psi^{-1}([-1,1]))=\frac{\hat{\nu}^{c}_{p_{n}}(\psi^{-1}([-1,1]))}{\hat{\nu}^{c}_{p_{n}}([-1,1])}.

In the last equality we have used our normalization choice
\hat{\nu}^{c}_{p_{n}}([-1,1])=1
for leaf-wise quotient measures. Applying now Lemma
7.12
we deduce

\displaystyle\frac{\hat{\nu}^{c}_{p_{n}}(\psi^{-1}([-1,1]))}{\hat{\nu}^{c}_{p_{n}}([-1,1])}

\displaystyle=\frac{\hat{\nu}^{c}_{p}((\lambda^{c}_{p}(n))^{-1}\times\psi^{-1}([-1,1]))}{\hat{\nu}^{c}_{p}((\lambda^{c}_{p}(n))^{-1}\times[-1,1])}

\displaystyle=\frac{\hat{\nu}^{c}_{p}(g^{-1}(\lambda^{c}_{p}(n))^{-1}\times([-1,1])))}{\hat{\nu}^{c}_{p}((\lambda^{c}_{p}(n))^{-1}\times[-1,1])}

\displaystyle=\frac{g_{*}\hat{\nu}^{c}_{p}(\lambda^{c}_{p}(n))^{-1}\times([-1,1]))}{\hat{\nu}^{c}_{p}((\lambda^{c}_{p}(n))^{-1}\times[-1,1])}

(45)

\displaystyle=c(p,t),

where on the second equality we applied the definition of
\psi
and in the last equality we have used the fact that the measures
g_{*}\hat{\nu}^{c}_{p}
and
\hat{\nu}^{c}_{p}
are proportional by a factor precisely equal to
c(p,t)
. This establishes (
44
).

We now give the final argument for completing the proof. For each
\varepsilon>0
, let
r>0
be chosen such that the set

B_{r,\varepsilon}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}\big\{p\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}:|c(q,t)-1|<\varepsilon,\quad\forall\,|t|<r\big\}

satisfies
\mu(B_{r,\varepsilon})>0
. By ergodicity,
\mu
-a.e. point
p\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
visits
B_{r,\varepsilon}
infinitely many times both in future and past; but (
44
) implies that
|c(p,t)-1|<\varepsilon
for all
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
and
\mu
-a.e.
p\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
. Letting
\varepsilon\to 0
, we conclude that
c(p,t)=1
for all
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
and
\mu
-a.e.
p\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
, hence, by (
43
),
\hat{\nu}_{p}^{c}
is invariant under the group of translations, for
\mu
-a.e.
p\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
, hence it is proportional to Lebesgue measure.
∎

8.1.
Drift along the center

Recall that the normal forms
\{\Phi^{c}_{x}\colon{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathcal{W}}^{c}(x)\}_{x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}}
give us parametrizations of the center manifolds whose change of coordinates are affine maps. Using these changes of coordinates one can build the maps
\psi\colon{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
promised in Lemma
8.1
. Indeed, we claim that it suffices to prove the result below.

Proposition 8.2
.

There exist constants
M>0
and
\delta_{0}\in(0,1)
such that for every
\varepsilon>0
sufficiently small one can find a compact set
G=G(\varepsilon)\subset\mathbb{T}^{3}
so that
\mu(G)\geq\delta_{0}
and for every
p\in G
there exists a point
q\in{\mathcal{W}}_{1}^{c}(p)
, so that

(46)

M^{-1}\varepsilon\leq|{\mathcal{H}}^{c}_{p}(q)|\leq M\varepsilon\>\>\textrm{and}\>\>\>\hat{\nu}^{c}_{q}\propto B_{*}\hat{\nu}^{c}_{p},

for some linear map
B\colon{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
of the form
s\mapsto\beta\cdot s
so that
M^{-1}<|\beta|<M
.

8.1.1.
Proof of Lemma
8.1
assuming Proposition
8.2

We only need to show that the set
G
of Proposition
8.2
satisfies the claims in Lemma
8.1
for a suitably chosen constant
M
(which will be perhaps a bit larger than the one already given by the proposition). To see this, take
q\in{\mathcal{W}}_{1}^{c}(p)
for some
