p\in\mathbb{T}^{3}
and let us, for the sake of this proof, denote by
\xi={\mathcal{H}}^{c}_{p,q}={\mathcal{H}}^{c}_{p}\circ\Phi^{c}_{q}\colon{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
the affine change of normal form coordinates along the center direction.
Because normal forms depends continuously with respect to the base point, we
may enlarge
M
if necessary so that
|\xi^{\prime}(0)|\in(M^{-1},M)
.

Now, take
G
the set given by Proposition
8.2
and let
p\in G
. We shall construct the affine map
\psi\colon{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
claimed by the lemma. For this take
q\in{\mathcal{W}}^{c}_{1}(p)
given by Proposition
8.2
and notice that, at one hand, Lemma
7.12
gives us that
\hat{\nu}^{c}_{q}\propto\xi_{*}\hat{\nu}^{c}_{p}
. On the other hand, Proposition
8.2
says that for some linear map
B\colon{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
with derivative
\beta
bounded in between
M^{-1}
and
M
we have
\hat{\nu}^{c}_{q}\propto B_{*}\hat{\nu}^{c}_{p}
. These two properties give us that
\hat{\nu}^{c}_{p}\propto\psi_{*}\hat{\nu}^{c}_{p}
, where
\psi=B^{-1}\xi
is an affine map. The bounds we have on
\beta
and on
|\xi^{\prime}(0)|
give

|\psi^{\prime}(0)|\in(M^{-2},M^{2}).

Moreover, by Proposition
8.2

|\psi(0)|=|B^{-1}\xi(0)|=|B^{-1}{\mathcal{H}}^{c}_{p}(q)|\in(M^{-2}\varepsilon,M^{2}\varepsilon).

This shows that
\psi
satisfies all the requirements in Lemma
8.1
with
M_{0}=M^{2}
, thus completing the proof.∎

8.1.2.
The Lusin set

We now move on to the proof of Proposition
8.2
, where our key arguments are concentrated. Our approach here draws inspiration from Eskin-Lindenstrauss’ work
[
14
]
. To offer some insight of our reasonning, recall from Lemma
7.13
that the measures
\hat{\nu}^{c}_{x}
change linearly when we shift the base point from
x
to
f^{n}(x)
, with the slope of this linear map being
\|Df^{n}(x)|_{E^{c}}\|
. Our strategy involves identifying specific dynamical configurations in which points,
almost
on the same center leaf, drift apart by a center distance proportional to
\varepsilon
. In these configurations, their corresponding leaf-wise quotient measures are
almost
proportional to each other, modulo a linear map whose slope can be controlled using the dynamics. To pinpoint the precise set where “almost” turns into “equality”, we take limits. To achieve this we must consider points belonging to compact sets restricted to which the assignment
x\mapsto\hat{\nu}^{c}_{x}
has good properties.

With that goal in mind, we denote by
C^{0}_{c}({\mathbb{R}})
the space of continuous functions with compact support of the real line.

Lemma 8.3
.

For every
\delta>0
there exists a compact set
A\subset{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
with
\mu(A)>1-\delta
such that for all
\{x_{n}\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\mathop{\subset}A
converging to
x\in A
the following holds

\int\varphi d\hat{\nu}^{c}_{x_{n}}\to\int\varphi d\hat{\nu}^{c}_{x},\>\>\>\>\textrm{for every}\>\>\varphi\in C^{0}_{c}({\mathbb{R}}).

Proof.

Let us denote
K\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}\operatorname{supp}(\varphi)
. We first claim that, given
\varphi\in C^{0}_{c}({\mathbb{R}})
, the function
x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}\mapsto\int\varphi d\hat{\nu}^{c}_{x}\in{\mathbb{R}}
is measurable. Indeed, consider the set

\Lambda(\varepsilon)=\{x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}};{\mathcal{W}}^{c}_{\varepsilon}(x)\subset\xi^{c}_{0}(x)\}.

Since
\mu(\Lambda(\varepsilon))\to 1
as
\varepsilon\to 0
it suffices to check that, for a given
\varepsilon>0
, the restriction of the function to the corresponding set
\Lambda(\varepsilon)
is measurable (notice that the set
\Lambda(\varepsilon)
itself is measurable). By compactness of
K
and uniform continuity of the
C^{1}
-norm of our normal forms in compact sets, Lemma
2.18
ensures the existence of an
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that

(47)

K\subset\zeta^{c}_{n}(x),\>\>\>\textrm{for every}\>\>\>x\in\Lambda(\varepsilon).

Define
\hat{\varphi}:{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\to{\mathbb{R}}
as
\hat{\varphi}(t,s)=\varphi(s)
. Then, denoting
\alpha(x)=1/\nu^{cu}_{n,x}(I\times[-1,1])
, it follows from (
47
) that

\int\varphi d\hat{\nu}^{c}_{x}=\alpha(x)\int\hat{\varphi}\circ{\mathcal{H}}_{x}(z)d\mu^{cu}_{n,x}(z),

for every
x\in\Lambda(\varepsilon)
. Since the right-hand side above depends measurably on
x\in\Lambda(\varepsilon)
our claim is proved.

As a second step in our proof, we claim the existence of a countable dense subset
\{\varphi_{\ell}\}_{\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}}\subset C^{0}_{c}({\mathbb{R}})
with the following property: for a sequence
\{x_{n}\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\mathop{\subset}{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
converging to a point
x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
, if

\int\varphi_{\ell}d\hat{\nu}^{c}_{x_{n}}\to\int\varphi_{\ell}d\hat{\nu}^{c}_{x},

for all
\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}
, then we can conclude that

\int\varphi d\hat{\nu}^{c}_{x_{n}}\to\int\varphi d\hat{\nu}^{c}_{x},

for every
\varphi\in C^{0}_{c}({\mathbb{R}})
.

To construct such a dense subset, start with a countable dense subset
{\mathcal{F}}
of
C^{0}_{c}({\mathbb{R}})
with the following property: if
\varphi
is in
C^{0}_{c}({\mathbb{R}})
and
K=\operatorname{supp}(\varphi)
then define
\hat{K}=[\inf K-1,\sup K+1]
: for every
\varepsilon>0
small enough there exists
\psi\in{\mathcal{F}}
such that
\|\varphi-\psi\|_{\infty}<\varepsilon
and
\operatorname{supp}(\psi)\subset\hat{K}
. The existence of
{\mathcal{F}}
follows by standard arguments.

Now, augment
{\mathcal{F}}
by adding, for each positive integer
n
, a continuous bump function that equals
1
inside the interval
[-n,n]
and
0
outside
(-n-1,n+1)
. The resulting set, still denoted as
{\mathcal{F}}
, remains countable and dense, but now has the property that for any compact set
K\subset{\mathbb{R}}
, there exists some function
\psi\in{\mathcal{F}}
such that
\psi(x)\geq 1
for every
x\in K
. To show that
{\mathcal{F}}
meets the claim’s requirements, consider
\varphi
in
C^{0}_{c}({\mathbb{R}})
and let
K=\operatorname{supp}(\varphi)
. Choose
\psi\in{\mathcal{F}}
such that
\psi|_{\hat{K}}\geq 1
, where
\hat{K}=[\inf K-1,\sup K+1]
. Then,

\hat{\nu}^{c}_{x_{n}}(\hat{K})\leq\int\psi d\hat{\nu}^{c}_{x_{n}}.

By assumption, the right-hand side above converges to
\int\psi d\hat{\nu}^{c}_{x}
. Thus,
\hat{\nu}^{c}_{x_{n}}(\hat{K})
is bounded by some number
\beta(K)>0
, independent of
n
. Now, given
\varepsilon>0
, choose
\hat{\psi}
in the countable dense family such that

\|\hat{\psi}-\varphi\|_{\infty}<\frac{\varepsilon}{3\beta(K)},

and with
\operatorname{supp}(\hat{\psi})\subset\hat{K}
.
Since
\hat{\psi}
is in
{\mathcal{F}}
there exists
n_{0}\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that if
n\geq n_{0}

\left|\int\hat{\psi}d\hat{\nu}^{c}_{x_{n}}-\int\hat{\psi}d\hat{\nu}^{c}_{x}\right|<\frac{\varepsilon}{3}.

Thus, if
n\geq n_{0}
we have

\displaystyle\left|\int\varphi d\hat{\nu}^{c}_{x_{n}}-\int\varphi d\hat{\nu}^{c}_{x}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left|\int\varphi d\hat{\nu}^{c}_{x_{n}}-\int\hat{\psi}d\hat{\nu}^{c}_{x_{n}}\right|+\left|\int\hat{\psi}d\hat{\nu}^{c}_{x_{n}}-\int\hat{\psi}d\hat{\nu}^{c}_{x}\right|

\displaystyle+\left|\int\hat{\psi}d\hat{\nu}^{c}_{x}-\int\varphi d\hat{\nu}^{c}_{x}\right|

\displaystyle<\frac{\varepsilon\hat{\nu}^{c}_{x_{n}}(\hat{K})}{3\beta(K)}+\frac{\varepsilon}{3}+\frac{\varepsilon\hat{\nu}^{c}_{x}(\hat{K})}{3\beta(K)}

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon.

This proves our second claim. To conclude the proof of the lemma, take
{\mathcal{F}}=\{\varphi_{\ell}\}_{\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}}
as the countable dense family of our previous claim. For each
\ell
, according to our first claim, the function
g_{\ell}:x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}\mapsto\int\varphi_{\ell}d\hat{\nu}^{c}_{x}
is measurable. By Lusin’s theorem there exists a compact set
A_{\ell}\subset{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
with measure
\mu(A_{\ell})>1-\delta/2^{\ell+1}
and such that
g_{\ell}|_{A_{\ell}}
is continuous. Define the compact set
A=\bigcap_{\ell=0}^{\infty}A_{\ell}
. Then,
\mu(A)>1-\delta
and if a sequence
\{x_{n}\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\subset A
converges to
x
, we have
g_{\ell}(x_{n})\to g_{\ell}(x)
for every
\ell
, and the lemma follows from the above claims.
∎

Let us take
{\mathcal{L}}\subset{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
a compact set with large measure (we shall give precise estimates below) such that the conclusion of Lemma
8.3
holds for
A={\mathcal{L}}
. We shall require further the following properties:

(1)

For
\sigma=s,c,u,cu
given
\xi^{\sigma}
, a measurable partition subordinate to
{\mathcal{W}}^{\sigma}
, there exists
r_{0}>0
so that for every
x\in{\mathcal{L}}
,

{\mathcal{W}}^{\sigma}_{r_{0}}(x)\mathop{\subset}\xi^{\sigma}(x).

(2)

For every
x\in{\mathcal{L}}
,
\xi^{u}(x)
is an interval whose size
r^{u}(x)

varies
continuously with
x\in{\mathcal{L}}
(see Remark
2.14
).

The continuity of the function
{\mathcal{L}}\ni x\mapsto\hat{\nu}^{c}_{x}
together with Remark
7.11
imply the following.

Corollary 8.4
.

For each
M>1
there exists
c=c(M)>0
such that if
B:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
is a linear map with derivative in
[M^{-1},M]
then
\hat{\nu}^{c}_{x}(B^{-1}[-1,1])\in[c^{-1},c]
for each
x\in{\mathcal{L}}
.

Proof.

Assume by contradiction that a lower bound does not hold. Denote
J_{0}=[-M^{-1},M^{-1}]
. Then, for each
n
there must exist
x_{n}\mathop{\subset}{\mathcal{L}}
such that
\hat{\nu}^{c}_{x_{n}}(J_{0})\leq 1/n
. We can assume, by compactness of
{\mathcal{L}}
, that
x_{n}\to x\in{\mathcal{L}}
. Since
y\in{\mathcal{L}}\mapsto\hat{\nu}^{c}_{y}
is continuous, we have that

\hat{\nu}^{c}_{x}(J_{0})\leq\liminf_{n\to\infty}\hat{\nu}^{c}_{x_{n}}(J_{0})=0,

which violates Remark
7.11
. By a similar reasoning, considering
J_{1}=[-M,M]
and assuming that the upper bound does not hold we would obtain a point
x\in{\mathcal{L}}
for which
\hat{\nu}^{c}_{x}(J_{1})=\infty
, which is also impossible by local finiteness.
∎

8.1.3.
The main proposition

Let us fix two numbers
\delta
and
\delta_{0}
such that

0<\delta_{0}<\frac{1}{10},\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\text{and}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\delta=3\delta_{0}.

We will require
\mu({\mathcal{L}})>1-\delta_{0}
. The constant
\delta_{0}
is the constant appearing in Lemma
8.1
and Proposition
8.2
. Further assumption will be given on
\delta
and
\delta_{0}
in Section
11
, and more precisely in §
11.3.6
.

Given
M,\varepsilon>0
set

(48)

G(\varepsilon,M)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}\left\{p\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}:\begin{array}[]{l}\exists\,q\in{\mathcal{W}}^{c}_{1}(p),\,\exists\,B(s)=\beta\cdot s\text{ linear map such that}\\
\hat{\nu}^{c}_{q}\propto B_{*}\hat{\nu}^{c}_{p}\\
M^{-1}\varepsilon\leq|{\mathcal{H}}^{c}_{p}(q)|\leq M\varepsilon\\
M^{-1}\leq|\beta|\leq M\end{array}\right\}.

Notice that
G(\varepsilon,M)
is nothing but the set of points
p\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
for which the conclusion of Proposition
8.2
holds with constants
\varepsilon,M
. Recall that in order to prove Proposition
8.2
, and thus Lemma
8.1
, we must show that
\mu(G(\varepsilon,M))\geq\delta_{0}
.

Given this choice of parameters, Proposition
8.2
follows from the statement below.

Proposition 8.5
.

There exists
M>0
such that for
\varepsilon>0
small enough and every compact set
K_{00}
with
\mu(K_{00})>1-2\delta_{0}
,

K_{00}\cap{\mathcal{L}}\cap G(\varepsilon,M)\neq\emptyset.

Proof that Proposition
8.5

\Longrightarrow
Proposition
8.2
.

Let
M>0
be the constant given in the statement above. Assume by contradiction that Proposition
8.2
does not hold with the constants
M
and
\delta_{0}
. Then, for some small
\varepsilon>0
we must have
\mu(G(\varepsilon,M))<\delta_{0}
. By regularity of
\mu
there exists an open neighbourhood
U
of
G(\varepsilon,M)
such that
\mu(U)<\delta_{0}
. Let
K_{00}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}{\mathbb{T}^{3}}\setminus U
. This set is compact and satisfies
\mu(K_{00})\geq 1-\delta_{0}>1-2\delta_{0}
. Proposition
8.5
then implies that
K_{00}\cap G(\varepsilon,M)\neq\emptyset
, which is absurd.
∎

In order to start the proof of Proposition
8.5
, fix
\varepsilon>0
(independently of
\delta
: notice that while
\delta
is fixed we need to take
\varepsilon\to 0
). More assumptions on
\varepsilon
will be given later on. This is the expected size of the drift we want to see along the center direction. Given a compact set
K_{00}
whose measure is larger than
1-2\delta_{0}
, notice that the compact set

K_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}K_{00}\cap{\mathcal{L}}.

has measure
\mu(K_{0})>1-3\delta_{0}=1-\delta
. We shall prove that
K_{0}\cap G(\varepsilon,M)\neq\emptyset
. This will occupy the rest of the paper.

9.
Stopping times,
Y
-configurations, quadrilaterals and synchronization

The goal of this section is to introduce the key dynamical ingredients involved in the proof of Proposition
8.5
, which is the main part of our implementation of an exponential drift argument. We also use this section to derive some estimates relating these ingredients.

9.1.
Stopping times

We introduce below the stopping time functions. They are devised to measure the appropriate time length of the top part of Figure
5
so that we get the precise drift we want along the center direction.

9.1.1.
Definition of stopping times

Recall our concise notations for derivatives

\lambda^{\star}_{x}(n)=\|Df^{n}(x)|_{E^{\star}}\|\>\>\>\>\star=c,s,u,

and

d^{\ell}_{x}=\frac{\lambda^{c}_{x_{-\ell}}(\ell)}{\lambda^{u}_{x_{-\ell}}(\ell)}

introduced in (
2
) of §
2.1.2
and (
4
) of §
2.1.3
, respectively, where
x_{n}=f^{n}(x)
is our concise notation for orbit points introduced in (
1
) of §
2.1.2
.
Given
x\in{\mathbb{T}}^{3}
,
x^{u}\in{\mathcal{W}}^{u}(x)
,
\varepsilon>0
, and
\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}
, we define

\tau(\ell)=\tau(x,x^{u},\varepsilon,\ell)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}\inf\Big\{n\in{\mathbb{N}}:d^{\ell}_{x}\times\lambda^{c}_{x^{u}}(n)\geq\varepsilon\Big\}.

We also define

t(\ell)=t(x,x^{u},\varepsilon,\ell)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}\inf\Big\{n\in{\mathbb{N}}:\frac{\lambda^{c}_{x}(n)}{\lambda^{c}_{x^{u}}(\tau(\ell))}\geq 1\Big\}.

These functions are called the
stopping times
.

9.1.2.
Quasi-isometric estimates

In the following, we fix
\varepsilon>0
. Recall that for
x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
,
x^{u}\in{\mathcal{W}}^{u}(x)
, we abbreviate
\tau(\ell)=\tau(x,x^{u},\varepsilon,\ell)
and
t(\ell)=t(x,x^{u},\varepsilon,\ell)
.

Lemma 9.1

(Quasi-isometric estimates)
.

There exists
\Theta=\Theta(f)>1
and
A=A(f)>0
so that given
\ell,m\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
and
x^{u}\in{\mathcal{W}}^{u}(x)
the following holds

(1)

\Theta^{-1}m-A<\tau(\ell+m)-\tau(\ell)<\Theta m+A
and

(2)

\Theta^{-1}m-A<t(\ell+m)-t(\ell)<\Theta m+A
.

Proof.

In this proof we shall make use of the constants of hyperbolicity of
f
introduced in §
2.1.3
. With these constants at hand, we can now develop the argument for the
quasi-isometric estimates
. For this, fix
x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
and
x^{u}\in{\mathcal{W}}^{u}(x)
. Given some
\varepsilon>0
, let us consider
\tau(\ell)=\tau(x,x^{u},\varepsilon,\ell)
and
t(\ell)=t(x,x^{u},\varepsilon,\ell)
. From the definition of
\tau
, we have that

d^{\ell}_{x}\lambda^{c}_{x^{u}}\left(\tau(\ell)\right)\geq\varepsilon\>\>\>\textrm{and}\>\>\>d^{\ell}_{x}\lambda^{c}_{x^{u}}\left(\tau(\ell)-1\right)<\varepsilon,\>\>\>\textrm{for each}\>\>\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}.

Thus,

(49)

\varepsilon\leq d^{\ell}_{x}\lambda^{c}_{x^{u}}\left(\tau(\ell)\right)<e^{\chi^{c}_{1}}\varepsilon,\>\>\>\textrm{for each}\>\>\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}.

Fix
\ell,m\in{\mathbb{N}}
. On the one hand, by the cocycle property (
3
) we have that
d^{\ell+m}_{x}=d^{\ell}_{x}d^{m}_{x_{\ell}}
and therefore we can use (
49
) to obtain

\varepsilon e^{m\chi^{d}_{1}}<d^{\ell+m}_{x}\lambda^{c}_{x^{u}}\left(\tau(\ell)\right)<\varepsilon e^{\chi^{c}_{1}+m\chi^{d}_{2}}.

On the other hand, we can use the cocycle property once more to write
\lambda^{c}_{f^{\tau(\ell)}(x^{u})}\left(\tau(\ell+m)-\tau(\ell)\right)=\frac{\lambda^{c}_{x^{u}}\left(\tau(\ell+m)\right)}{\lambda^{c}_{x^{u}}\left(\tau(\ell)\right)}
and thus
obtain

e^{\chi^{c}_{2}\left(\tau(\ell+m)-\tau(\ell)\right)}<\frac{\lambda^{c}_{x^{u}}\left(\tau(\ell+m)\right)}{\lambda^{c}_{x^{u}}\left(\tau(\ell)\right)}<e^{\chi^{c}_{1}\left(\tau(\ell+m)-\tau(\ell)\right)}.

Therefore, since
d^{\ell+m}_{x}\lambda^{c}_{x^{u}}\left(\tau(\ell+m)\right)=d^{\ell+m}_{x}\lambda^{c}_{x^{u}}\left(\tau(\ell)\right)\frac{\lambda^{c}_{x^{u}}\left(\tau(\ell+m)\right)}{\lambda^{c}_{x^{u}}\left(\tau(\ell)\right)}
we can combine the two above inequalities and obtain

\varepsilon e^{m\chi_{1}^{d}+\chi_{2}^{c}\left(\tau(\ell+m)-\tau(\ell)\right)}<d^{\ell+m}_{x}\lambda^{c}_{x^{u}}\left(\tau(\ell+m)\right)<\varepsilon e^{\chi_{1}^{c}+m\chi_{2}^{d}+\chi_{1}^{c}\left(\tau(\ell+m)-\tau(\ell)\right)}.

Putting
\ell+m
instead of
\ell
in (
49
) and combining with this we get the inequalities

\varepsilon e^{\chi_{1}^{c}+m\chi_{2}^{d}+\chi_{1}^{c}\left(\tau(\ell+m)-\tau(\ell)\right)}>\varepsilon\>\>\>\textrm{and}\>\>\>\varepsilon e^{-\chi_{1}^{c}+m\chi_{1}^{d}+\chi_{2}^{c}\left(\tau(\ell+m)-\tau(\ell)\right)}<\varepsilon.

Dividing by
\varepsilon
and taking logarithms we deduce that

(50)

m\left[\frac{-\chi_{2}^{d}}{\chi_{1}^{c}}\right]-1<\tau(m+\ell)-\tau(\ell)<m\left[\frac{-\chi_{1}^{d}}{\chi_{2}^{c}}\right]+\frac{\chi_{1}^{c}}{\chi_{2}^{c}}.

Let us deal with the function
\ell\mapsto t(\ell)
. From its very definition we have an inequality analogous to (
49
):

(51)

1\leq\frac{\lambda^{c}_{x}\left(t(\ell)\right)}{\lambda^{c}_{x^{u}}\left(\tau(\ell)\right)}<e^{\chi_{1}^{c}},\>\>\>\textrm{for every}\>\>x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}},\>\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}.

To simplify the remainder of the exposition, we shall denote

\tau_{\ell,m}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}\tau(\ell+m)-\tau(\ell)
and
t_{\ell,m}=t(\ell+m)-t(\ell)
.
Notice that

e^{\chi_{2}^{c}t_{\ell,m}-\chi_{1}^{c}\tau_{\ell,m}}<\frac{\lambda^{c}_{f^{t(\ell)}(x)}\left(t(\ell+m)-t(\ell)\right)}{\lambda^{c}_{f^{\tau(\ell)}(x^{u})}\left(\tau(\ell+m)-\tau(\ell)\right)}<e^{\chi_{1}^{c}t_{\ell,m}-\chi_{2}^{c}\tau_{\ell,m}}.

Combining this with (
51
), we can use the decomposition

\frac{\lambda^{c}_{x}\left(t(\ell+m)\right)}{\lambda^{c}_{x^{u}}\left(\tau(\ell+m)\right)}=\frac{\lambda^{c}_{x}\left(t(\ell)\right)\lambda^{c}_{f^{t(\ell)}(x)}\left(t(\ell+m)-t(\ell)\right)}{\lambda^{c}_{x^{u}}\left(\tau(\ell)\right)\lambda^{c}_{f^{\tau(\ell)}(x^{u})}\left(\tau(\ell+m)-\tau(\ell)\right)}

and conclude that

e^{\chi_{2}^{c}t_{\ell,m}-\chi_{1}^{c}\tau_{\ell,m}}<\frac{\lambda^{c}_{x}\left(t(\ell+m)\right)}{\lambda^{c}_{x^{u}}\left(\tau(\ell+m)\right)}<e^{\chi_{1}^{c}t_{\ell,m}-\chi_{2}^{c}\tau_{\ell,m}+\chi_{1}^{c}}.

Now, using (
51
) with
\ell+m
instead of
\ell
, the above inequality implies that

e^{\chi_{2}^{c}t_{\ell,m}-\chi_{1}^{c}\tau_{\ell,m}}<e^{\chi_{1}^{c}}\>\>\>\textrm{and}\>\>\>1<e^{\chi_{1}^{c}t_{\ell,m}-\chi_{2}^{c}\tau_{\ell,m}+\chi_{1}^{c}},

and thus

e^{\chi_{2}^{c}t_{\ell,m}-\chi_{1}^{c}\tau_{\ell,m}-\chi_{1}^{c}}<1<e^{\chi_{1}^{c}t_{\ell,m}-\chi_{2}^{c}\tau_{\ell,m}+\chi_{1}^{c}}.

Taking logarithms leads us to

\left[\frac{\chi_{2}^{c}}{\chi_{1}^{c}}\right]\tau_{\ell,m}-1<t_{\ell,m}<\left[\frac{\chi_{1}^{c}}{\chi_{2}^{c}}\right]\tau_{\ell,m}+\frac{\chi_{1}^{c}}{\chi_{2}^{c}},

which combined with (
50
) ends the proof.
∎

9.2.
Y
-configurations

We introduce below a dynamical ingredient inspired by
[
14
,
15
]
. They allow us to “decompose” Figure
5
in the “
x
-side” and in the “
y
-side”. Notice that each side has a kind of
Y
-shape. The idea from
[
14
]
for getting the points inside the Lusin set is to try to prove the existence of a large amount of these
Y
-shaped dynamical configurations and then try to find some of them which are linked through stable manifolds, as it appears in Figure
5
. These measure theoretical arguments will be developed in Section
11
. In this section, we introduce these configurations and establish
synchronization and (center) drift estimates for them
.

9.2.1.
Definition

Given
\varepsilon>0
and
\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}
, a
Y
-configuration

Y=Y(x,x^{u},\ell)
is a quintuple of points
(x,x^{u},x_{-\ell},x^{u}_{\tau},x_{t})
, that depends on parameters
x,x^{u}\in\mathbb{T}^{3}
and
\ell
(the dependence on
\varepsilon
is implicit throughout the text), and such that

(1)

x^{u}\in{\mathcal{W}}^{u}(x)
;

(2)

x_{-\ell}=f^{-\ell}(x)
;

(3)

x^{u}_{\tau}=f^{\tau}(x^{u})
, where
\tau=\tau(x,x^{u},\varepsilon,\ell)
;

(4)

x_{t}=f^{t}(x^{u})
, where
t=t(x,x^{u},\varepsilon,\ell)
.

where
\tau(x,x^{u},\varepsilon,\ell)
and
t(x,x^{u},\varepsilon,\ell)
are the stopping times defined above.

We call
\ell
the
length
of the
Y
-configuration. Moreover, given a set
\Lambda\subset{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
, we say that a
Y
-configuration
(x,x^{u},x_{-\ell},x^{u}_{\tau},x_{t})
is
\Lambda
-good
if
x,x^{u},x^{u}_{\tau},x_{t}\in\Lambda
.

Time
0
-\ell
\tau(\ell)
t(\ell)
\mathcal{W}^{u}(x)
\tau(\ell)=\tau(x,x^{u},\varepsilon,\ell)
t(\ell)=t(x,x^{u},\varepsilon,\ell)
\ell
x^{u}
x_{-\ell}
x
f^{t(\ell)}(x)
f^{\tau(\ell)}(x^{u})

Figure 10.
A
Y
-configuration.

In
[
14
]
, the authors defined a notion of
pairs of coupled

Y
-configurations. For technical reasons we shall replace this notion by that of pairs of
matched

Y
-configurations that will appear in the next section.

9.3.
Quadrilaterals and synchronization

Another important aspect of Figure
5
is located at its middle, where we have a kind of a quadrilateral, twisted along the unstable direction. This, indeed, is the third dynamical ingredient in our implementation of an exponential drift argument. It will allow us to use the angle condition and to define the aforementioned notion of matching of
Y
-configurations.

9.3.1.
Quadrilaterals

A
quadrilateral
is a quadruple
(x,x^{u},y,y^{u})\in({\mathbb{T}^{3}})^{4}
such that

(1)

y\in{\mathcal{W}}^{s}(x)
;

(2)

x^{u}\in{\mathcal{W}}^{u}(x)
belongs to the domain of a center-holonomy map
H^{cs}_{x,y}
;

(3)

y^{u}=H^{cs}_{x,y}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(x^{u})}\in{\mathcal{W}}^{u}(y)\cap{\mathcal{W}}^{cs}(x^{u})
.

For such a quadrilateral, we define the point
z^{u}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}H^{s}_{x,y}(x^{u})
, so that
z^{u}\in\mathcal{W}^{s}(x^{u})\cap{\mathcal{W}}^{c}(y^{u})
. Moreover, given
C>1
,
\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
we say that
(x,x^{u},y,y^{u})
is a
(C,\ell)
-quadrilateral
if, besides,

(4)

C^{-1}<d(x_{-\ell},y_{-\ell})<1
,

(5)

C^{-1}<\alpha^{s}(x_{-\ell},y_{-\ell})<C
and

(6)

C^{-1}<d(x,x^{u})<C
,

where, as before,
x_{-\ell}=f^{-\ell}(x)
,
y_{-\ell}=f^{-\ell}(y)
.

{\mathcal{W}}^{s}_{{\operatorname{loc}}}(x_{-\ell})
{\mathcal{W}}^{u}_{{\operatorname{loc}}}(x_{-\ell})
H^{s}_{x_{-\ell},y_{-\ell}}({\mathcal{W}}^{u}_{{\operatorname{loc}}}(x_{-\ell}))
z^{u}_{-\ell}
{\mathcal{W}}^{u}_{{\operatorname{loc}}}(y_{-\ell})
y^{u}_{-\ell}
\gamma^{u}
f^{\ell}
\alpha^{s}(x_{-\ell},y_{-\ell})
f^{\ell}\
x_{-\ell}
y_{-\ell}
x^{u}
x^{u}_{-\ell}
y
x

Figure 11.
A quadrilateral and its pre-image by
f^{\ell}
.

9.3.2.
Drift estimates

In Figure
5
we claim that the displacement along the center at the left top of the configuration is proportional to the parameter
\varepsilon
. We shall now make this assertion precise in terms of normal form coordinates. We first introduce a useful notation that will be used throughout the text, and in particular in the next proof.

Given a set of real parameters
c_{1},\ldots,c_{n}
and two quantities
a
and
b
(that may or may not depend on other variables) we denote
a\asymp_{c_{1},\ldots,c_{n}}b
if there exists a real valued function
\rho=\rho(c_{1},\ldots,c_{n})\geq 1
such that

\rho^{-1}a\leq b\leq\rho a.

Lemma 9.2
.

Fix
\varepsilon>0
. Given
C>1
and
T>0
, there exists a constant
\kappa=\kappa(C,T)>1
such that for every
\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}
large enough, every

(C,\ell)
-quadrilateral
(x,x^{u},y,y^{u})
, and every
\tau\geq 0
such that
|\tau-\tau(y,y^{u},\varepsilon,\ell)|\leq T
we have

(52)

\frac{\varepsilon}{\kappa}<\left|{\mathcal{H}}^{c}_{f^{\tau}(z^{u})}(f^{\tau}(y^{u}))\right|<\kappa\varepsilon.

Proof.

Let
\gamma^{u}=[x,x^{u}]\subset{\mathcal{W}}^{u}(x)
be the segment of strong unstable manifold connecting the points
x
and
x^{u}
. See Figure
11
. Then,
f^{-\ell}\circ H^{s}_{x,y}(\gamma^{u})
is a
C^{1}
curve joining
y_{-\ell}
and
z^{u}_{-\ell}
.
2
2

2

Recall our notation convention for orbit points:
p_{n}=f^{n}(p)
for
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}
.
Since,
f^{-\ell}\circ H^{s}_{x,y}(\gamma^{u})=H^{s}_{x_{-\ell},y_{-\ell}}\circ f^{-\ell}(\gamma^{u})
the assumption
C^{-1}<\alpha^{s}(x_{-\ell},y_{-\ell})<C
implies that the vector tangent to the curve
f^{-\ell}\circ H^{s}_{x,y}(\gamma^{u})
at
y_{-\ell}
and the strong unstable direction
E^{u}(y_{-\ell})
are transverse with an angle between
C^{-1}
and
C
. Recall that
{\mathcal{H}}_{y_{-\ell}}\colon{\mathcal{W}}^{cu}(y_{-\ell})\to{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
sends
{\mathcal{W}}^{u}(y_{-\ell})
onto the horizontal axis (see Theorem
6.3
). As the length of
f^{-\ell}\circ H^{s}_{x,y}(\gamma^{u})
is exponentially small with
\ell
and the bundle
E^{u}
is continuous, a standard compactness argument then ensures that for some constant
c_{1}=c_{1}(C)>0
and for
\ell
large enough, the curve
{\mathcal{H}}_{y_{-\ell}}\circ f^{-\ell}\circ H^{s}_{x,y}(\gamma^{u})
on
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
is contained in the cone

{\mathcal{C}}_{1}=\left\{(v_{1},v_{2})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}:c_{1}^{-1}|v_{1}|\leq|v_{2}|\leq c_{1}|v_{1}|\right\}.

Now, observe that
{\mathcal{H}}_{y}\circ H^{s}_{x,y}(\gamma^{u})={\mathcal{H}}_{y}\circ f^{\ell}\circ\Phi_{y_{-\ell}}\circ{\mathcal{H}}_{y_{-\ell}}\circ f^{-\ell}\circ H^{s}_{x,y}(\gamma^{u})
and that
{\mathcal{H}}_{y}\circ f^{\ell}\circ\Phi_{y_{-\ell}}
is the linear map
(t,s)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\mapsto(\lambda_{y_{-\ell}}^{u}(\ell)t,\lambda_{y_{-\ell}}^{c}(\ell)s)
. These two observations put together imply that the curve
{\mathcal{H}}_{y}\circ H^{s}_{x,y}(\gamma^{u})
is contained in the cone

{\mathcal{C}}_{2}=\left\{(v_{1},v_{2})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}:c_{1}^{-1}\frac{\lambda_{y_{-\ell}}^{c}(\ell)}{\lambda_{y_{-\ell}}^{u}(\ell)}|v_{1}|\leq|v_{2}|\leq c_{1}\frac{\lambda_{y_{-\ell}}^{c}(\ell)}{\lambda_{y_{-\ell}}^{u}(\ell)}|v_{1}|\right\}.

Now let
\gamma^{c}
denote the piece of center manifold connecting
z^{u}
to
y^{u}
. Then
{\mathcal{H}}_{y}\circ\gamma^{c}
connects the point
{\mathcal{H}}_{y}(z^{u})
, whose distance to the origin only depends on the constant
C
and is bounded from above and below, to the point
{\mathcal{H}}_{y}(y^{u})
, which lies on the horizontal axis. Since
{\mathcal{H}}_{y}(z^{u})\in{\mathcal{C}}_{2}
, we deduce that (see Figure
12
)

\operatorname{length}({\mathcal{H}}_{y}\circ\gamma^{c})\asymp_{C}\frac{\lambda_{y_{-\ell}}^{c}(\ell)}{\lambda_{y_{-\ell}}^{u}(\ell)}.

{\mathcal{H}}_{y_{-\ell}}(y_{-\ell})
{\mathcal{H}}_{y_{-\ell}}(y^{u}_{-\ell})
{\mathcal{H}}_{y_{-\ell}}(z^{u}_{-\ell})
{\mathcal{H}}_{y}(y)
{\mathcal{H}}_{y}(y^{u})
{\mathcal{H}}_{y}(z^{u})
{\mathcal{H}}_{y}\circ f^{\ell}\circ\Phi_{y_{-\ell}}

Figure 12.
On normal form coordinates the dynamics on
{\mathcal{W}}^{cu}
acts as a diagonal matrix, with stronger expansion on the horizontal.

Since
C^{-1}<d^{u}(x,x^{u})<C
and we have
C^{1}
holonomies, we also have that
C^{-1}<d(z^{u},y)<C
(upon enlarging the constant
C
if necessary in order to take into account the action of holonomy maps). Recall that normal forms change continuously in the
C^{1}
topology, so another compactness argument ensures that the
C^{1}
norm of
{\mathcal{H}}_{z^{u}}\circ\Phi_{y}|_{W^{cu}_{2C}(y)}
is bounded by some constant depending on
C
. Thus

\operatorname{length}({\mathcal{H}}_{z^{u}}\circ\gamma^{c})\asymp_{C}\frac{\lambda_{y_{-\ell}}^{c}(\ell)}{\lambda_{y_{-\ell}}^{u}(\ell)}.

On the other hand, by the construction of normal forms,
{\mathcal{H}}_{z^{u}}\circ\gamma^{c}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
is a vertical segment with length
|{\mathcal{H}}^{c}_{z^{u}}(y^{u})|
. This proves that

\left|{\mathcal{H}}^{c}_{z^{u}}(y^{u})\right|\asymp_{C}\frac{\lambda_{y_{-\ell}}^{c}(\ell)}{\lambda_{y_{-\ell}}^{u}(\ell)}.

As we did before it is possible to bound uniformly from above the
C^{1}
-norms of all
{\mathcal{H}}^{c}_{\ast}
in restriction to center segments of uniform radius so (upon enlarging
\ell
if necessary) we also have

d_{c}(y^{u},z^{u})\asymp_{C}\frac{\lambda_{y_{-\ell}}^{c}(\ell)}{\lambda_{y_{-\ell}}^{u}(\ell)},\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\text{and}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\left|{\mathcal{H}}^{c}_{y^{u}}(z^{u})\right|\asymp_{C}\frac{\lambda_{y_{-\ell}}^{c}(\ell)}{\lambda_{y_{-\ell}}^{u}(\ell)}.

Since the dynamics on normal forms acts linearly, this implies

\left|{\mathcal{H}}^{c}_{f^{\tau(\ell)}(y^{u})}{(f^{\tau(\ell)}(z^{u}))}\right|\asymp_{C}\frac{\lambda_{y_{-\ell}}^{c}(\ell)}{\lambda_{y_{-\ell}}^{u}(\ell)}\times\lambda^{c}_{y^{u}}(\tau(\ell))

where
\tau(\ell)=\tau(y,y^{u},\varepsilon,\ell)
is the stopping time. By definition this implies that

(53)

\left|{\mathcal{H}}^{c}_{f^{\tau(\ell)}(y^{u})}{(f^{\tau(\ell)}(z^{u}))}\right|\asymp_{C}\varepsilon.

Since
|\tau-\tau(\ell)|\leq T
we obtain

\left|{\mathcal{H}}^{c}_{f^{\tau}(y^{u})}(f^{\tau}(z^{u}))\right|\asymp_{C,T}\varepsilon.\qed

Corollary 9.3
.

There exists a constant
\kappa=\kappa(C,T)
such that for every
\tau
satisfying
|\tau-\tau(y,y^{u},\varepsilon,\ell)|\leq T
it holds

d_{c}(f^{\tau}(y^{u}),f^{\tau}(z^{u}))\leq\kappa\varepsilon.

Proof.

This follows from the proof of Lemma
9.2
and the fact that normal forms
\{{\mathcal{H}}^{c}_{x}\}_{x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}}
are
C^{1}
maps with continuously varying
C^{1}
norm on compact sets.
∎

9.3.3.
Synchronization estimates

The last set of estimates we need concerns the oscillation of the stopping times from one side to the other when we have a
(C,\ell)
-quadrilateral.

Lemma 9.4

(Synchronization for quadrilaterals)
.

For any
C>1
, there exists a constant
T_{0}>0
depending only on
f,C
such that for any
\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}
, and any
(C,\ell)
-quadrilateral
(x,x^{u},y,y^{u})
, it holds

|\tau(y,y^{u},\varepsilon,\ell)-\tau(x,x^{u},\varepsilon,\ell)|<T_{0},\quad|t(y,y^{u},\varepsilon,\ell)-t(x,x^{u},\varepsilon,\ell)|<T_{0}.

Proof.

Recall the constants
\chi_{2}^{c},\chi_{1}^{c}>0
introduced in §
2.1.3
. They satisfy
e^{k\chi_{2}^{c}}\leq\lambda^{c}_{x}(k)\leq e^{k\chi_{1}^{c}}
for every
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
and every
x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
where
\lambda^{c}_{x}(k)=\|Df^{k}(x)|_{E^{c}}\|
is our concise notation for derivatives from (
2
). For the sake of simplicity, denote
\tau(\ell)=\tau(x,x^{u},\varepsilon,\ell)
and
\tau^{\prime}(\ell)=\tau(y,y^{u},\ell,\varepsilon)
. Assume that
\tau(\ell)\geq\tau^{\prime}(\ell)
. By definition

(54)

d^{\ell}_{y}\lambda^{c}_{y^{u}}(\tau^{\prime}(\ell))\geq\varepsilon.

Let us write for
k\in{\mathbb{N}}

(55)

\frac{d^{\ell}_{x}\lambda^{c}_{x^{u}}(\tau^{\prime}(\ell)+k)}{d^{\ell}_{y}\lambda^{c}_{y^{u}}(\tau^{\prime}(\ell))}=\frac{d^{\ell}_{x}}{d^{\ell}_{y}}\times\frac{\lambda^{c}_{x^{u}}(\tau^{\prime}(\ell))}{\lambda^{c}_{y^{u}}(\tau^{\prime}(\ell))}\times\lambda^{c}_{x^{u}_{\tau^{\prime}(\ell)}}(k).

We now bound from below each factor appearing in (
55
). We treat the third factor by observing that
\lambda^{c}_{x^{u}_{\tau^{\prime}(\ell)}}(k)\geq e^{k\chi_{2}^{c}}
. To treat the first factor, we use that
y_{-\ell}\in{\mathcal{W}}^{s}_{1}(x_{-\ell})
so the distortion control given by Corollary
2.10
gives
\frac{d^{\ell}_{y}}{d^{\ell}_{x}}\geq C_{0}^{-1}
Finally, in order to treat the second factor, we notice that Corollary
9.3
provides that

d_{c}(f^{j}(z^{u}),f^{j}(y^{u}))\leq C\varepsilon,

for some constant
C=C(f)>0
and for every
j=0,\dots,\tau^{\prime}(\ell)
. With no loss of generality we can assume that
C\varepsilon<1
. Applying the distortion control of Corollary
2.11
we obtain
\frac{\lambda^{c}_{x^{u}}(\tau^{\prime}(\ell))}{\lambda^{c}_{y^{u}}(\tau^{\prime}(\ell))}\geq C_{0}^{-1}
. These lower bounds together with (
55
) provide the following estimate

d^{\ell}_{x}\lambda^{c}_{x^{u}}(\tau^{\prime}(\ell)+k)\geq\frac{e^{k\chi_{2}^{c}}}{C_{0}^{2}}\varepsilon\geq\varepsilon

as soon as we choose
k=k(f)
such that

(56)

e^{k\chi_{2}^{c}}\geq C_{0}^{2}.

Hence by definition of the stopping time, we obtain
\tau^{\prime}(\ell)+k\geq\tau(\ell)
so
\tau(\ell)-\tau^{\prime}(\ell)\leq k
. A symmetric argument shows that
\tau^{\prime}(\ell)-\tau(\ell)\leq k
if
\tau^{\prime}(\ell)\geq\tau(\ell)
.

We consider now the stopping time
t
. As above, we denote
t(\ell)=t(x,x^{u},\varepsilon,\ell)
and
t^{\prime}(\ell)=t^{\prime}(y,y^{u},\varepsilon,\ell)
. By definition

(57)

\frac{\lambda^{c}_{y}(t^{\prime}(\ell)}{\lambda^{c}_{y^{u}}(\tau^{\prime}(\ell))}\geq 1.

Let us write for
k^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{N}}
:

\displaystyle\frac{\lambda^{c}_{x}(t^{\prime}(\ell)+k^{\prime})}{\lambda^{c}_{x^{u}}(\tau(\ell))}{\frac{\lambda^{c}_{y^{u}}(\tau^{\prime}(\ell))}{\lambda^{c}_{y}(t^{\prime}(\ell)}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda^{c}_{y^{u}}(\tau^{\prime}(\ell))}{\lambda^{c}_{x^{u}}(\tau^{\prime}(\ell))}\times\frac{\lambda^{c}_{x}(t^{\prime}(\ell)}{\lambda^{c}_{y}(t^{\prime}(\ell)}

\displaystyle\quad\times\lambda^{c}_{x_{t^{\prime}(\ell)}}(k^{\prime})\times\frac{1}{\lambda^{c}_{x^{u}_{\tau^{\prime}(\ell)}}(\tau(\ell)-\tau^{\prime}(\ell))}.

We now bound from below each factor of the product above. We have already seen how to treat the first factor
\frac{\lambda^{c}_{x^{u}}(\tau^{\prime}(\ell))}{\lambda^{c}_{y^{u}}(\tau^{\prime}(\ell))}\geq C_{0}^{-1}
. To treat the second factor, we use that
y\in{\mathcal{W}}^{s}_{1}(x)
and apply Corollary
2.11
to get
\frac{\lambda^{c}_{x}(t^{\prime}(\ell))}{\lambda^{c}_{y}(t^{\prime}(\ell))}\geq C_{0}^{-1}
. We treat the third factor by observing that
\lambda^{c}_{x_{t^{\prime}(\ell)}}(k^{\prime})\geq e^{k^{\prime}\chi_{2}^{c}}
. Note that
|\tau(\ell)-\tau^{\prime}(\ell)|\leq k
so the last bound follows from
\frac{1}{\lambda^{c}_{x^{u}_{\tau^{\prime}(\ell)}}(\tau(\ell)-\tau^{\prime}(\ell))}\geq e^{-k\chi_{1}^{c}}
. Finally we find

(58)

\frac{\lambda^{c}_{x}(t^{\prime}(\ell)+k^{\prime})}{\lambda^{c}_{x^{u}}(\tau(\ell))}\geq\frac{e^{k^{\prime}\chi_{2}^{c}}}{C_{0}^{2}e^{k\chi_{1}^{c}}}\times\frac{\lambda^{c}_{y}(t^{\prime}(\ell)}{\lambda^{c}_{y^{u}}(\tau^{\prime}(\ell))}\geq 1,

as soon as we choose
k^{\prime}=k^{\prime}(f)
such that

(59)

e^{k^{\prime}\chi_{2}^{c}}\geq C_{0}^{2}e^{k\chi_{1}^{c}}.

Hence by definition we must have
t(\ell)\leq t^{\prime}(\ell)+k^{\prime}
so
t(\ell)-t^{\prime}(\ell)\leq k^{\prime}
. Here again a symmetric argument yields
t^{\prime}(\ell)-t(\ell)\leq k^{\prime}
. Hence

(60)

T_{0}=\max(k,k^{\prime}),

is the desired constant. This ends the proof.
∎

10.
Matching of
Y
-configurations

In this section we develop a concept devised specifically to address the technical difficulty to implement the exponential drift idea we want to employ, a difficulty which was described at the end of Section
3
. Namely, due to lack of absolute continuity of center stable holonomies we cannot ensure that the points
x^{u}
and
y^{u}
of Figure
5
belong both to the Lusin set. What we can actually prove is that small perturbations of these two points can indeed be put inside the Lusin set. This will lead us to the notion of matched configurations.

10.1.
Matching of dynamical balls

We start the formal definition of matching in our scenario by introducing intervals along the unstable manifold which measure the amount of perturbation of the points
x^{u}
and
y^{u}
which are allowed, without breaking the estimates we performed for quadrilaterals in the previous section.

10.1.1.
Unstable dynamical balls

Although the notion of dynamical balls is quite standard in ergodic theory, we use this name in this paper for a more specific object, adapted to our needs.

Definition 10.1
.

Let
\varepsilon>0
,
\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
x\in\mathbb{T}^{3}
and
x^{u}\in{\mathcal{W}}^{u}(x)
. Let
\tau(\ell)=\tau(x,x^{u},\varepsilon,\ell)
. The
(\varepsilon,\ell)
-unstable dynamical ball
at
x^{u}
is defined as

J(x^{u})=J(x^{u},\varepsilon,\ell)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}f^{-\tau(\ell)}\left({\mathcal{W}}^{u}_{1}(f^{\tau(\ell)}(x^{u}))\right).

Remark 10.2
.

By definition, for every
a\in J(x^{u})
and
j\in\{0,\ldots,\tau(x,x^{u},\varepsilon,\ell)\}

d_{u}(f^{j}(x^{u}),f^{j}(a))<1.

10.1.2.
Synchronization inside a dynamical ball

Before we define the notion of matching of
Y
-configuration it is useful to study the oscillation of stopping times inside an unstable dynamical ball.

Let
T_{0}>0
be the constant obtained in Lemma
9.4
.

Lemma 10.3
.

For every
a\in J(x^{u})
it holds
|\tau(x,a,\varepsilon,\ell)-\tau(x,x^{u},\varepsilon,\ell)|<T_{0}
and
|t(x,a,\varepsilon,\ell)-t(x,x^{u},\varepsilon,\ell)|<T_{0}
.

Proof.

The proof is almost identical to that of Lemma
9.4
. In particular constants
0<\chi_{2}^{c}<\chi_{1}^{c}
are those defined in §
2.1.3
and
k
and
k^{\prime}
, those defined by (
56
) and (
59
). Let us denote
\tau_{a}(l)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}\tau(x,a,\varepsilon,\ell)
. Suppose first that
\tau_{a}(\ell)\geq\tau(\ell).

(61)

\frac{d^{\ell}_{x}\lambda^{c}_{a}(\tau(\ell)+k)}{d^{\ell}_{x}\lambda^{c}_{x^{u}}(\tau(\ell))}=\frac{\lambda^{c}_{a}(\tau(\ell))}{\lambda^{c}_{x^{u}}(\tau(\ell))}\times\lambda^{c}_{a_{\tau(\ell)}}(k).

We have
\lambda^{c}_{a_{\tau(\ell)}}(k)\geq e^{k\chi_{2}^{c}}
. Finally we have by definition
f^{\tau(\ell)}(a)\in{\mathcal{W}}^{u}_{1}(f^{\tau(\ell)}(x^{u}))
so by the distortion control (
7
) we have
\frac{\lambda^{c}_{a}(\tau(\ell))}{\lambda^{c}_{x^{u}}(\tau(\ell))}\geq C_{0}^{-1}
and, by choice of
k
,

d^{\ell}_{x}\lambda^{c}_{a}(\tau(\ell)+k)\geq\frac{e^{k\chi_{2}^{c}}}{C_{0}}d^{\ell}_{x}\lambda^{c}_{x^{u}}(\tau(\ell))\geq\varepsilon.

Hence
\tau_{a}(\ell)-\tau(\ell)\leq k
. A symmetric argument yields
\tau(\ell)-\tau_{a}(\ell)\leq k.

We consider now the stopping time
t
. As above, we denote
t_{a}(\ell)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}\tau(x,a,\varepsilon,\ell)
and
t(\ell)=t(x,a,\varepsilon,\ell)
. Let us first suppose
t_{a}(\ell)\geq t(\ell)
. We have

\displaystyle\frac{\lambda^{c}_{x}(t(\ell)+k^{\prime})}{\lambda^{c}_{a}(\tau_{a}(\ell))}\times{\frac{\lambda^{c}_{x^{u}}(\tau(\ell))}{\lambda^{c}_{x}(t(\ell))}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda^{c}_{x^{u}}(\tau(\ell))}{\lambda^{c}_{a}(\tau(\ell))}\times\lambda^{c}_{x_{t^{\prime}(\ell)}}(k^{\prime})

\displaystyle\times\frac{1}{\lambda^{c}_{x^{u}_{\tau(\ell)}}(\tau(\ell)-\tau^{\prime}(\ell))}.

The first factor is
\geq C_{0}^{-1}
. The second one is
\geq e^{k^{\prime}\chi_{2}^{c}}
. The third one is
\geq e^{-k{\chi_{1}^{c}}}
. Hence by our choice of
k^{\prime}
,

\frac{\lambda^{c}_{x}(t(\ell)+k^{\prime})}{\lambda^{c}_{a}(\tau_{a}(\ell))}\geq\frac{e^{k^{\prime}\chi_{2}^{c}}}{C_{0}e^{k\chi_{1}^{c}}}\times\frac{\lambda^{c}_{x^{u}}(\tau(\ell))}{\lambda^{c}_{x}(t(\ell))}\geq 1.

This proves that
t_{a}(\ell)-t(\ell)\leq k^{\prime}
. Again, a symmetric argument gives
t_{a}(\ell)-t(\ell)\leq k^{\prime}
if
t(\ell)\geq t_{a}(\ell)
. This ends the proof of the lemma because recall that
T_{0}=\max(k,k^{\prime})
.
∎

10.1.3.
Matching of dynamical balls and distortion control

We first define the notion of
matched unstable dynamical balls
and study their geometric properties.

Definition 10.4

(Matched unstable dynamical balls)
.

When
(x,x^{u},y,y^{u})
is a
(C,\ell)
-quadrilateral, then we say that
J(x^{u})=J(x^{u},\varepsilon,\ell)
and
J(y^{u})=J(y^{u},\varepsilon,\ell)
are
(C,\ell)
-
matched
.

We will need the following distortion control for matched unstable dynamical balls.

Proposition 10.5
.

For
\varepsilon
small enough, there exists
\kappa_{3}=\kappa_{3}(f,\varepsilon)
such that if
\ell
is sufficiently large, for every
(C,\ell)
-matched dynamical balls
J(x^{u})
and
J(y^{u})
, and every
a\in J(x^{u})
,
b\in J(y^{u})
, we have

\kappa_{3}^{-1}\leq\frac{\lambda^{\ast}_{b}(j)}{\lambda^{\ast}_{a}(j)}\leq\kappa_{3},

for every integer
0\leq j\leq\max(\tau(x,x^{u},\varepsilon,\ell),\tau(y,y^{u},\varepsilon,\ell))
and
\ast=c,u
.

Proof.

Let us assume that
\tau(\ell)\leq\tau^{\prime}(\ell)
, where
\tau(\ell)=\tau(x,x^{u},\varepsilon,\ell)
and
\tau^{\prime}(\ell)=\tau(y,y^{u},\varepsilon,\ell)
. Let us first notice that by Lemma
9.4
, we have
\tau^{\prime}\leq\tau+T_{0}
.

Let
j\leq\tau(\ell)
so in particular

|f^{j}(J(x^{u}))|,|f^{j}(J(y^{u}))|<1.

Let
z^{u}=H^{s}_{x,y}(x^{u})
so
d(x^{u},z^{u})<1
, for
\ell
large enough, and, by Corollary
9.3
,
d(f^{j}(y^{u}),f^{j}(z^{u}))\leq d(f^{\tau^{\prime}}(y^{u}),f^{\tau^{\prime}}(z^{u}))\asymp_{C,T_{0}}\varepsilon<1
.

Let
\phi_{\ast}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}\log\|Df|_{E^{\ast}}\|
and
c_{\ast},\theta_{\ast}
be the Hölder constant and exponent of
\phi_{\ast}
, for
\ast=c,u
. We have

\left|\log\frac{\lambda^{\ast}_{b}(j)}{\lambda^{\ast}_{a}(j)}\right|\leq\sum_{i=0}^{j-1}|\phi_{\ast}(f^{i}(a))-\phi_{\ast}(f^{i}(b))|.

Hence,

\displaystyle|\phi_{\ast}(f^{i}(a))-\phi_{\ast}(f^{i}(b))|

\displaystyle\leq|\phi_{\ast}(f^{i}(a))-\phi_{\ast}(f^{i}(x^{u}))|+|\phi_{\ast}(f^{i}(x^{u}))-\phi_{\ast}(f^{i}(z^{u}))|

\displaystyle+|\phi_{\ast}(f^{i}(z^{u}))-\phi_{\ast}(f^{i}(y^{u}))|+|\phi_{\ast}(f^{i}(y^{u}))-\phi_{\ast}(f^{i}(b))|,

Note that for
i<j
,
d(f^{i}(a),f^{i}(x^{u})),d(f^{i}(b),f^{i}(y^{u}))\leq e^{\chi^{u}_{1}(i-j)}
. On the other hand, we have
d(f^{i}(x^{u}),f^{i}(z^{u}))\leq e^{i\chi^{s}_{2}}
. Finally
d(f^{i}(z^{u}),f^{i}(y^{u}))\leq e^{\chi^{c}_{1}(i-j)}
. It follows that

|\phi_{\ast}(f^{i}(a))-\phi_{\ast}(f^{i}(b))|\leq c_{\ast}\left(2e^{\theta_{\ast}\chi^{u}_{1}(i-j)}+e^{i\theta_{\ast}\chi^{s}_{2}}+e^{\theta_{\ast}\chi^{c}_{1}(i-j)}\right).

By summing over
i
we deduced that
\big|\log\frac{\lambda^{\ast}_{b}(j)}{\lambda^{\ast}_{a}(j)}\big|
is uniformly bounded from above by a constant depending only on
f
and
\rho
. Of course we can now bound these quotients for
j
up to
\tau^{\prime}(\ell)
by using that

d_{0}^{-T_{0}}\frac{\lambda^{\ast}_{b}(j)}{\lambda^{\ast}_{a}(j)}\leq\frac{\lambda^{\ast}_{b}(j+T_{0})}{\lambda^{\ast}_{a}(j+T_{0})}\leq d_{0}^{T_{0}}\frac{\lambda^{\ast}_{b}(j)}{\lambda^{\ast}_{a}(j)},

where
d_{0}=\|Df\|/m(Df)
(recall §
2.1.2
).
∎

Corollary 10.6
.

There exists
\kappa_{4}=\kappa_{4}(f,\varepsilon)
such that if
\ell
is sufficiently large, for every
(C,\ell)
matched dynamical balls
J(x^{u})
and
J(y^{u})
,

\kappa_{4}^{-1}\leq\frac{|f^{j}(J(y^{u}))|}{|f^{j}(J(x^{u}))|}\leq\kappa_{4},

for every integer
0\leq j\leq\max(\tau(x,x^{u},\varepsilon,\ell),\tau(y,y^{u},\varepsilon,\ell))
.

Proof.

As before we denote
\tau(\ell)=\tau(x,x^{u},\varepsilon,\ell)
and
\tau^{\prime}(\ell)=\tau(y,y^{u},\varepsilon,\ell)
.
Suppose
\tau(\ell)\leq\tau^{\prime}(\ell)\leq\tau(\ell)+T_{0}
(where
T_{0}
is defined by Lemma
9.4
. In particular
m(Df)^{T_{0}}\leq|f^{\tau(\ell)}(J(y^{u}))|\leq 1
and
|f^{\tau(\ell)}(J(x^{u}))|=1
.

For
j\in\{0,\ldots,\tau(\ell)\}
, we notice that

\frac{|f^{j}(J(x^{u}))|}{|f^{j}(J(y^{u}))|}=\frac{\int_{f^{\tau(\ell)}(J(x^{u}))}\lambda^{u}_{q}(k)dq}{\int_{f^{\tau(\ell)}(J(y^{u}))}\lambda^{u}_{q}(k)dq},

where
k=j-\tau(\ell)\leq 0
. By the bounded distortion along the strong unstable manifolds (
7
) we find

\displaystyle C_{0}^{-2}\frac{\lambda^{u}_{f^{\tau(\ell)}(x^{u})}(k)}{\lambda^{u}_{f^{\tau(\ell)}(y^{u})}(k)}\frac{|f^{\tau(\ell)}(J(x^{u}))|}{|f^{\tau(\ell)}(J(y^{u}))|}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{|f^{j}(J(x^{u}))|}{|f^{j}(J(y^{u}))|}

\displaystyle\leq C_{0}^{2}\frac{\lambda^{u}_{f^{\tau(\ell)}(x^{u})}(k)}{\lambda^{u}_{f^{\tau(\ell)}(y^{u})}(k)}\times\frac{|f^{\tau(\ell)}(J(x^{u}))|}{|f^{\tau(\ell)}(J(y^{u}))|}.

Notice that

\lambda^{u}_{f^{\tau(\ell)}(x^{u})}(k)=\frac{\lambda^{u}_{x^{u}}(-k)}{\lambda^{u}_{x^{u}}(\tau(\ell))},

and a similar equation holds with
y^{u}
instead of
x^{u}
. Therefore, we can apply Proposition
10.5
to obtain

C_{0}^{-2}\kappa_{3}^{-2}\leq\frac{|f^{j}(J(x^{u}))|}{|f^{j}(J(y^{u}))|}\leq C_{0}^{2}\|Df\|^{T_{0}}(\kappa_{3})^{2}.

We conclude the proof of the corollary by noting that

m(Df)^{2T_{0}}\frac{|f^{j}(J(x^{u}))|}{|f^{j}(J(y^{u}))|}\leq\frac{|f^{j+T_{0}}(J(x^{u}))|}{|f^{j+T_{0}}(J(y^{u}))|}\leq\|Df\|^{2T_{0}}\frac{|f^{j}(J(x^{u}))|}{|f^{j}(J(y^{u}))|}.\qed

10.2.
Matching of
Y
-configurations

We now define the notion of matched
Y
-configurations and show that our drift argument (Proposition
8.5
) boils down to constructing arbitrarily long pairs of matched
Y
-configurations.

10.2.1.
Matching

We first give the main definition.

Definition 10.7

(Matched
Y
-configurations)
.

Let
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathcal{L}}}\mathop{\subset}{\mathbb{T}}^{3}
,
C>0
,

\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}
. Let
X=X(x,x^{u},\ell)
and
Y=Y(y,y^{u},\ell)
be two
Y
configurations of length
\ell
. We say that
X
and
Y
are
({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathcal{L}}},C,T)
-matched
if there exist
a,b\in{\mathbb{T}}^{3}
, and
\tau,t\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that

(1)

(x,x^{u},y,y^{u})
is a
(C,\ell)
-quadrilateral;

(2)

a\in J(x^{u},\varepsilon,\ell)
and
b\in J(y^{u},\varepsilon,\ell)
;

(3)

|\tau-\tau(x,x^{u},\varepsilon,\ell)|\leq T
and
|t-t(x,x^{u},\varepsilon,\ell)|\leq T
;

(4)

a,b,x,y,f^{\tau}(a),f^{\tau}(b),f^{t}(x),f^{t}(y)\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathcal{L}}}
.

{\mathcal{W}}^{u}_{1}(y)
{\mathcal{W}}^{u}_{1}(x)
J(y^{u})
\bullet
b
J(x^{u})
s
\bullet
x^{u}
\bullet
z^{u}
\bullet
y^{u}
\bullet
a
s
\bullet
x
\bullet
y
{\mathcal{W}}^{c}(y^{u})

Figure 13.
For matched
Y
-configurations we can only put inside the Lusin set “small perturbations”
a
and
b
of the endpoints
x^{y}
and
y^{u}
(respectively) of the quadrilateral.

Remark 10.8
.

At first sight this definition is not symmetric (see item (
3
)). But the synchronization estimate (Lemma
9.4
) implies that,

|\tau-\tau(y,y^{u},\varepsilon,\ell)|\leq T+T_{0},\text{ and }|t-t(y,y^{u},\varepsilon,\ell)|\leq T+T_{0}.

10.2.2.
Finding pairs of long and matched
Y
-configurations

We are now ready to state our main technical lemma which, as we shall see, implies Proposition
8.5
.

Let
{\mathcal{L}}
be the Lusin set defined in §
8.1.2
and
\delta=3\delta_{0}>0
be the constant fixed in §
8.1.3
: we shall give further assumption on
\delta
later on.

Lemma 10.9
.

Let
K_{0}\subset{\mathcal{L}}
be a compact set of measure
\mu(K_{0})>1-\delta
. There exist constants
C=C(\delta)
and
T=T(\delta)
and an infinite subset
{\mathcal{D}}\subset{\mathbb{N}}
such that for every
\ell\in{\mathcal{D}}
there exists a pair
(X,Y)
of
(K_{0},C,T)
-matched
Y
-configurations of length
\ell
.

The end of the section is devoted to proving that Lemma
10.9
implies Proposition
8.5
. The proof of Lemma
10.9
will be the object of Section
11
.

10.3.
Asymptotic control of leaf-wise measures for matched configurations

The goal of this paragraph is to study how leaf-wise quotient measures change along a pair of matched
Y
-configurations, and what happens when we have sequences of longer and longer pairs. To simplify the exposition, we shall break this explanation in two parts. First we deal with a single
Y
-configuration, and then we treat the full situation.

10.3.1.
Control along a
Y
-configuration

The lemma below is essentially an easy corollary of Lemmas
7.13
and
7.14
.

Lemma 10.10
.

For
x\in{\mathbb{T}}^{3}
,
x^{u}\in\mathcal{W}_{r}^{u}(x)
, for some
r>0
,
\varepsilon>0
, and
\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}
as above, it holds

\hat{\nu}_{f^{\tau(\ell)}(x^{u})}^{c}\propto(A_{x,x^{u},\ell})_{*}\hat{\nu}_{f^{t(\ell)}(x)}^{c},

for the linear map
A_{x,x^{u},\ell}\colon s\mapsto a_{x,x^{u},\ell}\cdot s
, with

a_{x,x^{u},\ell}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}\rho_{x^{u}}^{c}(x)\frac{\lambda^{c}_{x}(t(\ell))}{\lambda^{c}_{x^{u}}(\tau(\ell))}.

Moreover,
|a_{x,x^{u},\ell}|\in(a_{0}^{-1},a_{0})
for some constant
a_{0}=a_{0}(r)>1
depending only on the upper bound
r>0
on the distance along
\mathcal{W}^{u}
between
x
and
x^{u}
; in particular, the linear map
A_{x,x^{u},\ell}
is uniformly bounded away from
0
and
\infty
, independently of
\ell
.

Proof.

Applying Lemma
7.13
and Lemma
7.14
we successively obtain:

\displaystyle\hat{\nu}_{f^{\tau(\ell)}(x^{u})}^{c}

\displaystyle\propto(\Lambda_{\tau(\ell),x^{u}}^{c})_{*}\hat{\nu}_{x^{u}}^{c}

\displaystyle\propto(L_{x,x^{u}}\circ\Lambda_{\tau(\ell),x^{u}}^{c})_{*}\hat{\nu}_{x}^{c}

\displaystyle\propto(L_{x,x^{u}}\circ\Lambda_{\tau(\ell),x^{u}}^{c}\circ(\Lambda_{t(\ell),x}^{c})^{-1})_{*}\hat{\nu}_{f^{t(\ell)}(x)}^{c},

where
\Lambda^{c}_{n,x}
denotes the linear map

s\mapsto\lambda^{c}_{x}(n)s
, and
L_{x,x^{u}}
denotes the linear map
s\mapsto\rho_{x^{u}}^{c}(x)\cdot s
. Thus,
A_{x,x^{u},\ell}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}\Lambda_{\tau(\ell),x^{u}}^{c}\circ L_{x,x^{u}}\circ(\Lambda_{t(\ell),x}^{c})^{-1}
is equal to

s\mapsto\rho_{x^{u}}^{c}(x)\frac{\lambda^{c}_{x}(t(\ell))}{\lambda^{c}_{x^{u}}(\tau(\ell))}\cdot s.

By the fact that
d_{u}(x,x^{u})<r
, and by the definition of
t(\ell)
,
|A_{x,x^{u},\ell}^{\prime}(0)|
is uniformly bounded, depending only on
r
and
f
, but not on
\ell
, which concludes the proof.
∎

10.3.2.
Control for matched configurations

We now deal with the full picture of sequences of pairs of good and matched configurations.
For the next result, we refer to §
8.1.2
for the definition of the Lusin set
\mathcal{L}
, to §
9.3
and to §
10.2
for that of quadrilaterals and
(C,\ell)
-matched
Y
-configurations respectively.

The result of this subsection is the core of the proof that Lemma
10.9
implies Proposition
8.5
.
Until the end of this section, the notation
x_{n}
will NOT stand for
f^{n}(x)
but for the usual notation of sequences
.

Lemma 10.11
.

Let
\varepsilon>0

(\ell_{n})
be an increasing sequence of integers. Suppose there exist constants
T,C>0
(independent of
\varepsilon
), and two sequences
X_{n}=(x_{n},x^{u}_{n},\ell_{n})
and
Y_{n}=(y_{n},y^{u}_{n},\ell_{n})
of
Y
-configurations of length
\ell_{n}
that are
({\mathcal{L}},C,T)
-matched. Let
\tau_{n},t_{n},a_{n},b_{n}
be the objects given by the condition of matching and consider the sequences

f^{\tau_{n}}(a_{n}),f^{\tau_{n}}(b_{n})\in\mathcal{L},\text{ and }f^{\tau_{n}}(x^{u}_{n}),f^{\tau_{n}}(y^{u}_{n})\in{\mathbb{T}}^{3}.

Assume all these sequences converge to points
a_{\infty}
,
b_{\infty}
,
p
and
q
respectively. Then
q\in{\mathcal{W}}^{c}(p)
and there exists
\gamma=\gamma(C,T)>1
and
M=M(C,T)
such that

(62)

\frac{\varepsilon}{\gamma}\leq|\mathcal{H}_{p}^{c}(q)|\leq\gamma\varepsilon,

and

(63)

\hat{\nu}_{a_{\infty}}^{c}\propto B_{*}\hat{\nu}_{b_{\infty}}^{c}.

where
B(s)=\beta\cdot s
, is a linear map that satisfies
\frac{1}{M}<|\beta|<M
.

Proof.

Note first that
\tau_{n}
and
t_{n}
tend to infinity: indeed, this is a consequence of the matching condition and of the fact that
\ell_{n}\to\infty
so the stopping times also tend to infinity (by Lemma
9.1
).

Now suppose the sequences
f^{\tau_{n}}(a_{n}),f^{\tau_{n}}(b_{n}),f^{\tau_{n}}(x_{n}),f^{\tau_{n}}(y_{n})
converge to
a_{\infty},b_{\infty},p,q
respectively.
Let
z_{n}^{u}=H^{s}_{x_{n},y_{n}}(x_{n}^{u})\in{\mathcal{W}}^{s}(x_{n})
. The condition of matching implies that
d(z_{n}^{u},x^{u}_{n})
is uniformly bounded so
d(f^{\tau_{n}}(z_{n}),f^{\tau_{n}}(x_{n}))\to 0
as
n\to+\infty
and
f^{\tau_{n}}(z_{n}^{u})\to p
. Since normal forms changes continuously, we have that

{\mathcal{H}}^{c}_{f^{\tau_{n}}(z^{u}_{n})}(f^{\tau_{n}}(y^{u}_{n}))\to{\mathcal{H}}^{c}_{p}(q),\quad\text{as}\quad n\to+\infty.

Thus (
62
) follows directly from Lemma
9.2
and Remark
10.8
. Let us show (
63
). For this we use the matching condition: for all
n
, the points
f^{\tau_{n}}(a_{n}),f^{\tau_{n}}(b_{n}),f^{t_{n}}(x_{n}),f^{t_{n}}(y_{n})
all belong to the Lusin set
{\mathcal{L}}
.

Combining Lemma
10.10
with Lemma
7.14
and Remark
10.8
we deduce that there exist linear maps
B_{n}\colon{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
and
\tilde{B}_{n}\colon{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
and constants
C_{n},\tilde{C}_{n}>0
such that the derivatives
B_{n}^{\prime}
and
\tilde{B}_{n}^{\prime}
lie inside
[M^{-1},M]
for some constant
M=M(T,C)
, independent of
n
and

\hat{\nu}^{c}_{f^{\tau_{n}}(a_{n})}=C_{n}(B_{n})_{*}\hat{\nu}^{c}_{f^{t_{n}}(x_{n})}\>\>\>\textrm{and}\>\>\>\>\>\hat{\nu}^{c}_{f^{\tau_{n}}(b_{n})}=\tilde{C}_{n}(\tilde{B}_{n})_{*}\hat{\nu}^{c}_{f^{t_{n}}(y_{n})}.

We claim that
C_{n}
and
\tilde{C}_{n}
are uniformly bounded away from
0
and
\infty
. Indeed, let us consider
C_{n}
. Let
c=c(M)>0
be the constant given by Corollary
8.4
. Then, denoting
I=[-1,1]
we have that, by our choice of normalization (see Remark
7.10
)
\hat{\nu}^{c}_{f^{\tau_{n}}(a_{n})}(I)=1
and therefore

C_{n}=\frac{1}{\hat{\nu}^{c}_{f^{t_{n}}(x_{n})}(B_{n}^{-1}(I))}\in[c^{-1},c],

for
f^{t_{n}}(x_{n})\in{\mathcal{L}}
for each
n
. A similar argument treats the constants
\tilde{C}_{n}
. Therefore up to enlarging
M
, we can assume that
C_{n},\tilde{C}_{n}\in[M^{-1},M]
.

The condition of matching implies that
y_{n}\in{\mathcal{W}}^{s}(x_{n})
for every
n
and that
d(x_{n},y_{n})
is uniformly bounded. Now recall that
f^{t_{n}}(x_{n}),f^{t_{n}}(y_{n})
belong to the Lusin set
{\mathcal{L}}
, which is compact. We can assume without loss of generality that there exists a point
x\in{\mathcal{L}}
such that
f^{t_{n}}(x_{n})\to x
and
f^{t_{n}}(y_{n})\to x
. For simplicity, let us denote
\hat{a}_{n}=f^{\tau_{n}}(a_{n})
,
\hat{b}_{n}=f^{\tau_{n}}(b_{n})
,
\hat{x}_{n}=f^{t_{n}}(x_{n})
and
\hat{y}_{n}=f^{t_{n}}(y_{n})
. We can also assume that
C_{n}\to C
and
\tilde{C}_{n}\to\tilde{C}
and that there exist linear maps
B,\tilde{B}:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
such that
B_{n}\to B
and
\tilde{B}_{n}\to\tilde{B}
in the
C^{1}
topology, i.e. the slopes of
B_{n}
converge to the slope of
B
and similarly for
\tilde{B}_{n}
and
\tilde{B}
.

Now, we claim that
\hat{\nu}^{c}_{a_{\infty}}=CB_{*}\hat{\nu}^{c}_{x}
and
\hat{\nu}^{c}_{b_{\infty}}=\tilde{C}\tilde{B}_{*}\hat{\nu}^{c}_{x}
. Once we prove this claim, the lemma will be established. To prove the claim, we first observe that

\int C_{n}(\varphi\circ B_{n})d\hat{\nu}^{c}_{\hat{x}_{n}}\to\int C(\varphi\circ B)d\hat{\nu}^{c}_{x},

for every
\varphi\in C^{0}_{c}({\mathbb{R}})
. Indeed, we can write

\displaystyle\left|\int C_{n}(\varphi\circ B_{n})d\hat{\nu}^{c}_{\hat{x}_{n}}-\int C(\varphi\circ B)d\hat{\nu}^{c}_{x}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left|\int C_{n}(\varphi\circ B_{n})d\hat{\nu}^{c}_{\hat{x}_{n}}-\int C(\varphi\circ B)d\hat{\nu}^{c}_{\hat{x}_{n}}\right|

\displaystyle+\left|\int C(\varphi\circ B)d\hat{\nu}^{c}_{\hat{x}_{n}}-\int C(\varphi\circ B)d\hat{\nu}^{c}_{x}\right|.

The second term on the right-hand side above converges to zero as
n\to\infty
because
{\mathcal{L}}
satisfies the conclusion of Lemma
8.3
and
\hat{x}_{n}\in{\mathcal{L}}
converges to
x
. The first term also converges to zero because
C_{n}(\varphi\circ B_{n})
converges uniformly to
C(\varphi\circ B)
and
\hat{\nu}^{c}_{\hat{x}_{n}}(\operatorname{supp}(\varphi))
is bounded independently of
n
. We use this observation to conclude the proof of the claim as follows. Given any
\varphi\in C^{0}_{c}({\mathbb{R}})
we have that

\displaystyle\int\varphi d\hat{\nu}^{c}_{a_{\infty}}

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\to\infty}\int\varphi d\hat{\nu}^{c}_{\hat{a}_{n}}=\lim_{n\to\infty}\int C_{n}(\varphi\circ B_{n})d\hat{\nu}^{c}_{\hat{x}_{n}}

\displaystyle=\int C(\varphi\circ B)d\hat{\nu}^{c}_{x},

proving that
\hat{\nu}^{c}_{a_{\infty}}=CB_{*}\hat{\nu}^{c}_{x}
. The proof that
\hat{\nu}^{c}_{b_{\infty}}=\tilde{C}\tilde{B}_{*}\hat{\nu}^{c}_{x}
is similar so we omit it.
The proof of the lemma is complete.
∎

With Lemma
10.11
at hand we are now in position to reduce the proof of Theorem
5.10
to the proof of Lemma
10.9
.

.

.

How to get invariance by an affine map using the exponential drift: we can move the leaf-wise measures from

Notice that by construction the four points

and

. By (

) we have that

\text{length}(\gamma^{c}_{p,q})=\text{length}(\Phi^{c}_{p}[0,{\mathcal{H}}^{c}_{p}(q)])\asymp_{C,\gamma}\varepsilon.

. Since
