we deduce that

\text{length}(\gamma^{c}_{a_{\infty},\hat{a}})\asymp_{C,\gamma}\varepsilon\>\>\>\>\>\textrm{and}\>\>\>\>\>\text{length}(\gamma^{c}_{b_{\infty},\hat{b}})\asymp_{C,\gamma}\varepsilon

Therefore, as
\varepsilon
is small we deduce that
\hat{b}\in{\mathcal{W}}^{c}_{1}(b_{\infty})
. Using the uniform bound for the
C^{1}
norm of the normal forms in segments of bounded length we have that

(67)

{\mathcal{H}}^{c}_{b_{\infty}}(\hat{b})\asymp_{C,\gamma}\varepsilon.

We apply Lemma
7.14
to get a linear map
L\colon{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
whose derivative satisfies
L^{\prime}(0)\in[C^{-1},C]
, for a constant
C=C(f)
such that
\hat{\nu}_{\hat{b}}\propto L_{*}\hat{\nu}_{a_{\infty}}
. Therefore,

\hat{\nu}_{\hat{b}}\propto(LB)_{*}\hat{\nu}_{b_{\infty}}

and the linear map
L\circ B
has a derivative bounded by
[C^{-1}M^{-1},CM]
. This together with (
67
) implies that
b_{\infty}\in G(\varepsilon,\tilde{M})
for some constant
\tilde{M}=\tilde{M}(\gamma,C,M)
(see (
48
) for the definition of the set
G(\varepsilon,\tilde{M})
), concluding the proof of Proposition
8.5
.∎

11.
Construction of matched
Y
-configurations: end of the proof

This section is devoted to the proof of Lemma
10.9
. Recall that we reduced our main Theorem
A
to Lemma
10.9
. Let us recall what we want to do. We assume that
\mu(\mathbf{B})=0
, where
\mathbf{B}=\mathbf{B}
is the Bad set introduced in Definition
5.2
. In particular, it follows from the zero-one law (Theorem
4.2
) that for
\mu
-a.e.
x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
,

(68)

\mu^{s}_{x}\left\{y\in\xi^{s}(x):\alpha^{s}(x,y)=0\right\}=0.

where we recall that
\alpha^{s}(x,y)=\angle(DH^{s}_{x,y}(x)E^{u}(x),E^{u}(y))
when
y\in{\mathcal{W}}^{s}(x)
. Given a large compact set
K_{0}
we want to find arbitrarily long pairs of
matched

Y
-configurations which are
K_{0}
-good (meaning that their points belong to
K_{0}
).

11.1.
Angle condition and absolute continuity

We start this section by showing how to use the condition
\mu(\mathbf{B})=0
to obtain abundance of pairs of points
y\in{\mathcal{W}}^{s}_{1}(x)
so that
\alpha^{s}(x,y)
is uniformly bounded from below.

Before carrying on the proof recall that for
\ast=s,u,c,cu
, we have fixed measurable partitions
\xi^{\ast}
subordinate to
{\mathcal{W}}^{\ast}
as well as disintegrations
\{\mu^{\ast}_{x}\}_{x}
relative to
\xi^{\ast}
. We fixed a Lusin set
{\mathcal{L}}
of measure
\mu({\mathcal{L}})>1-\delta
(further assumptions on
\delta
will be given later on) as given in §
8.1.2
. In particular there exists
r_{0}>0
such that for every
x\in{\mathcal{L}}
,

(69)

{\mathcal{W}}^{\ast}_{r_{0}}(x)\mathop{\subset}\xi^{\ast}(x).

11.1.1.
A Markov type inequality

Our first ingredient will be a simple inequality à la Markov that will be used several times throughout the section.

Lemma 11.1
.

Let
(X,{\mathcal{B}},\mu)
be a probability space, and
\eta\in(0,1)
. Let
\psi\colon X\to[0,1]
be a measurable function with
\int\psi d\mu>1-\eta
. Let
B\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}\{x\in X:\psi(x)>1-\sqrt{\eta}\}
. Then,
\mu(B)>1-\sqrt{\eta}
.

Proof.

We have

1-\eta<\int_{B}\psi d\mu+\int_{X\setminus B}\psi d\mu\leq\mu(B)+(1-\sqrt{\eta})(1-\mu(B))=1-\sqrt{\eta}(1-\mu(B)),

which gives
\mu(B)>1-\sqrt{\eta}.

∎

11.1.2.
Bounding from below the angle function

We fix a measurable partition
\xi^{s}
subordinate to
{\mathcal{W}}^{s}
and a disintegration
\{\mu^{s}_{x}\}_{x}
of
\mu
relative to
\xi^{s}
. Below we use the condition
\mu(\mathbf{B})=0
: by (
68
) it means that for
\mu
-a.e.
x\in{\mathbb{T}}^{3}
and
\mu^{s}_{x}
-a.e.
y\in\xi^{s}(x)
,
\alpha^{s}(x,y)>0
.

Until the end of the section we fix
r_{0}>0
such that (
69
) holds for all
x\in{\mathcal{L}}
. We consider a constant
\eta=\eta(\delta)>0
which goes to
0
with
\delta
. This will be explicitly given later on.

Lemma 11.2
.

Let
B\mathop{\subset}{\mathcal{L}}
of measure
\mu(B)>1-\eta
. Then there exists a measurable set
B^{\prime}\mathop{\subset}B
as well as a number
c=c(\eta)
such that
0<c\leq r_{0}\eta
and such that the following properties hold:

(1)

\mu(B^{\prime})>1-2\sqrt{\eta}
; and for
\mu
-a.e.
x\in B^{\prime}
there exists
y\in B\cap\xi^{s}(x)
such that

(2)

\alpha^{s}(x,y)>c
.

Proof.

Let us consider the set
B_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}\{x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}:\mu_{x}^{s}[B\cap\xi^{s}(x)]>1-\sqrt{\eta}\}
. It follows from Lemma
11.1
that
\mu(B_{1})>1-\sqrt{\eta}
.

Now, given
\alpha\geq 0
, we define for
\mu
-a.e.
x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
:

\displaystyle\mathscr{A}_{\alpha}(x)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}\{y\in\xi^{s}(x):\alpha^{s}(x,y)>\alpha\},

\displaystyle\mathscr{A}_{\alpha,\eta}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}\{x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}:\mu_{x}^{s}[\mathscr{A}_{\alpha}(x)]>1-\eta\}.

Using that
\mu(\mathbf{B})=0
we find
\mu_{x}^{s}[\mathscr{A}_{0}(x)]=1
, for
\mu
-almost every
x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
. This implies that
\mu[\mathscr{A}_{0,\eta}]=1
. Then, there exists
\alpha=\alpha(\eta)>0
such that the set
B_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}\mathscr{A}_{\alpha,\eta}
has measure
\mu(B_{2})>1-\eta
.

Let us define the set

B^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}B\cap B_{1}\cap B_{2},

so

\mu(B^{\prime})>1-2\eta-\sqrt{\eta}>1-2\sqrt{\eta}

(provided
\eta<1/4
, so
2\eta<\sqrt{\eta}
). Then for any
x\in B^{\prime}
,

—

\mu_{x}^{s}(B\cap\xi^{s}(x))>1-\sqrt{\eta}
;

—

\mu_{x}^{s}\{y\in\xi^{s}(x):\alpha^{s}(x,y)>\alpha\}>1-\eta
;

Set
c=c(\eta)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}\min\left(\alpha(\eta),r_{0}\eta\right)\in\big(0,r_{0}\eta\big]
. Then, for every
x\in B^{\prime}
, we have

\mu_{x}^{s}\{y\in B\cap\xi^{s}(x):\alpha^{s}(x,y)>c\}\geq 1-\sqrt{\eta}-\eta>1-2\sqrt{\eta}>0.

So we conclude that for every
x\in B^{\prime}
, then there exists
y\in\xi^{s}(x)
such that
\alpha^{s}(x,y)>c
. This concludes the proof of the lemma.
∎

11.2.
Recurrence estimates: building long and good
Y
-configurations

In this subsection we fix a measurable set
K\mathop{\subset}{\mathbb{T}}^{3}
of measure
\mu(K)>1-\delta
for some small
\delta>0
. The main result of this paragraph is Proposition
11.6
, that builds
K
-good
Y
-configurations of length
\ell
for every integer
\ell
inside a subset of
{\mathbb{N}}
of positive density. We start by establishing some preliminary results from ergodic theory.

11.2.1.
An elementary quantitative recurrence estimate

For the sake of clarity in the presentation, we introduce the following notion.

Definition 11.3
.

Given
\gamma>0
,
n>0
and
B
a measurable set, we say that a point
x\in\mathbb{T}^{3}
is
(\gamma,n)
-recurrent
to
B
if
L>n
implies that

\#\left\{\ell\in[0,L]:f^{\ell}(x)\in B\right\}>(1-\gamma)L.

The following holds for any ergodic system
(f,{\mathcal{B}},\mu)
.

Lemma 11.4
.

For every measurable set
B
with
\mu(B)>1-\gamma
there exist
T=T(\gamma)
and a subset
B^{\circ}\subset B
with
\mu(B^{\circ})>1-\gamma
so that any
x\in B^{\circ}
is
(\gamma,T)
-recurrent to
B
.

Proof.

Let
B
be a measurable set with
\mu(B)>1-\gamma
. We consider the sequence
(\varphi_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
of
L^{1}
functions given by
\varphi_{n}(x)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\mathbf{1}_{B}(f^{k}(x))
. By Birkhoff’s Theorem, the sequence
(\varphi_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
converges almost surely to the constant function
\mu(B)
. Moreover, by Egorov’s Theorem, there exists a measurable subset
B^{\circ}\subset B
of measure
\mu(B^{\circ})>1-\gamma
such that the sequence
(\varphi_{n}|_{B^{\circ}})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
converges uniformly to
\mu(B)
. Then, there exists
T>0
such that for any
x\in B^{\circ}
and
n>T
,
\varphi_{n}(x)>1-\gamma
. Such an
x
is
(\gamma,T)
-recurrent to
B
by definition.
∎

11.2.2.
Stopping times and return times to
K
for pairs
(x,x^{u})

Recall that we fixed a measurable set
K\mathop{\subset}{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
with measure
\mu(K)>1-\delta
. Applying Lemma
11.4
to
K
yields
an
integer
T_{1}=T_{1}(\delta)
and a measurable subset
K^{\circ}\mathop{\subset}K
with measure
\mu(K^{\circ})>1-\delta
consisting of
(\delta,T_{1})
-recurrent points to
K
. Up to enlarging
T_{1}
we can assume

(70)

T_{1}>4T_{0}

where
T_{0}
is the constant given by the synchronization estimates of Lemmas
9.4
and
10.3
.

Given a pair
\omega=(x,x^{u}),
with
x^{u}\in\xi^{u}(x)
we set

(71)

E(\omega)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}\{\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}:f^{\tau(\ell)}(x^{u})\in K\>\textrm{and}\>f^{t(\ell)}(x)\in K\}.

The objects
T_{1}
and
K^{\circ}
appearing in the next statement are the ones constructed in the previous paragraph.

Lemma 11.5
.

There exists a constant
r>0
which only depends on the quasi isometric estimates so that for every
L>T_{1}
,
x\in K^{\circ}
and
x^{u}\in\xi^{u}(x)\cap K^{\circ}
and
\omega=(x,x^{u})
,

\#\left(E(\omega)\cap[0,L]\right)>(1-r\delta)L.

Proof.

Recall that the stopping times satisfy
t(\ell)\geq 0
as well as the quasi-isometric estimate

\Theta^{-1}|\ell-m|-A<|t(\ell)-t(m)|<\Theta|\ell-m|+A,

for some constants
\Theta>1
and
A>0
depending only on
f
: see Lemma
9.1
. In particular for every
L>0
we have
t(L)\in[0,L^{\prime}]
where
L^{\prime}=\Theta L+A
. By hypothesis,
x\in K^{\circ}
so whenever
L>T_{1}
(and thus
L^{\prime}>T_{1}
), we have

\#\{k\in[0,L^{\prime}]:f^{k}(x)\notin K\}\leq\delta L^{\prime}.

On the other hand, the quasi-isometric estimate also implies that an integer in
[0,L^{\prime}]
has at most
2A\Theta
preimages by
t
so we have

\#\{\ell\in[0,L]:f^{t(\ell)}(x)\notin K\}\leq 2A\Theta\#\{k\in[0,L^{\prime}]:f^{k}(x)\notin K\}\leq 2A\Theta\delta(\Theta L+A).

This proves that for every
L>T_{1}
, the set
E_{t}(x)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}\{\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}:f^{t(\ell)}(x)\in K\}
has density
>1-r_{1}\delta
inside
[0,L]
for some constant
r_{1}>0
depending only on
A
and
\Theta
. Now, since
x^{u}
also belongs to
K^{\circ}
, the same property also holds for the set
E_{\tau}(x^{u})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}\{\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}:f^{\tau(\ell)}(x^{u})\in K\}
: it has density
>1-r_{2}\delta
inside
[0,L]
for
r_{2}>0
depending only on the quasi-isometry constants of
\tau
from Lemma
9.1
.

Finally we can estimate from below the density of
E(\omega)=E(x,x^{u})=E_{\tau}(x^{u})\cap E_{t}(x)
inside
[0,L]
using the following inequality

\#(E(\omega)\cap[0,L])\geq\#(E_{\tau}(x^{u})\cap[0,L])-\#([0,L]\setminus E_{t}(x))>(1-(r_{1}+r_{2})\delta)L.

This ends the proof of the lemma.
∎

11.2.3.
Space of pairs
(x,x^{u})

It will be useful to consider a measurable structure on the space of pairs

\Omega\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}\{\omega=(x,x^{u}):x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}\>\textrm{and}\>x^{u}\in\xi^{u}(x)\}.

Note that
\Omega
is contained inside the continuous submanifold of
{\mathbb{T}^{3}}\times{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
defined as
Y=\{(x,x^{u}):x\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}\>\textrm{and}\>x^{u}\in{\mathcal{W}}^{u}_{1}(x)\}
(recall that
1
is a uniform upper bound of the diamaters of atoms of
\xi^{u}
). The topology on
Y
induced by the product topology of
{\mathbb{T}^{3}}\times{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
provides it with a Borel
\sigma
-algebra. Its restriction to
\Omega
is denoted by
{\mathcal{A}}
. Let
\pi\colon\Omega\to\mathbb{T}^{3}
be the projection on the first coordinate. So we have
B\in{\mathcal{A}}
if and only if its projection
\pi(B)
is a measurable subset of
\mathbb{T}^{3}
, and
B\cap(\{x\}\times\xi^{u}(x))
is a measurable subset of
\{x\}\times\xi^{u}(x)
. Hence we can define a measure
\nu
on
\Omega
by

\nu(B)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}\int_{\pi(B)}\mu^{u}_{x}\left[B\cap\left(\{x\}\times\xi^{u}(x)\right)\right]d\mu(x).

11.2.4.
Construction of many long and good
Y
-configurations

Assume
\ell\in E(\omega)
for
\omega=(x,x^{u})
with
x,x^{u}\in K^{\circ}
. Then
(x,x^{u},x_{-\ell},f^{\tau(\ell)}(x^{u}),f^{t(\ell)}(x))
is a
K
-good
Y
-configuration of length
\ell
. So constructing many long and good
Y
-configurations means constructing many integers
\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}
for which the set of pairs
\omega=(x,x^{u})\in K^{\circ}\times K^{\circ}
with
\ell\in E(\omega)
has large measure for
\nu
.

More precisely, let us fix a continuous function
\eta\colon[0,1]\to[0,\infty)
vanishing at
0
and write abusively
\eta=\eta(\delta)
. An explicit construction will be given in the proof of the next proposition. We will assume that
\delta
is small enough so that
\eta<1
. For
\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
x\in K^{\circ}
let us define

(72)

Q^{u}(x)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}K^{\circ}\cap\xi^{u}(x),

and

(73)

Q^{u}(x,\ell)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}\{x^{u}\in\xi^{u}(x):x^{u}\in K^{\circ},\,\textrm{and}\,\ell\in E(x,x^{u})\}\mathop{\subset}Q^{u}(x).

This yields a set of
K
-good
Y
-configurations with length
\ell
. Next we define

(74)

K(\ell)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}\{x\in K^{\circ}:\mu^{u}_{x}[Q^{u}(x,\ell)]>1-\eta\}.

We want to construct a large set of integers
\ell
with
\mu[K(\ell)]
is large enough. This is provided by the following statement, inspired by Eskin-Lindestrauss’ paper
[
14
]
and which is essentially a Fubini-like argument.

Proposition 11.6

(see Claim 6.4 in Eskin-Lindenstrauss
[
14
]
)
.

Set

(75)

{\mathcal{D}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}\{\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}:\mu[K(\ell)]>1-\eta\}.

Then for every
L>T_{1}
we have

\#({\mathcal{D}}\cap[0,L])>(1-\eta)L.

Proof.

We start the proof by considering the set

K_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}\left\{x\in K^{\circ}:\mu^{u}_{x}[Q^{u}(x)]>1-\sqrt{\delta}\right\},

so that after applying Lemma
11.1
with
\psi\colon x\mapsto\mu^{u}_{x}[Q^{u}(x)]=\mu^{u}_{x}[K^{\circ}\cap\xi^{u}(x)]
we see that
\mu(K_{1})>1-\sqrt{\delta}
. The set

B\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}\{\omega=(x,x^{u})\in\Omega:x\in K_{1},\,x^{u}\in Q^{u}(x)\}

is a measurable subspace of
\Omega
of measure

\nu(B)=\int_{K_{1}}\mu^{u}_{x}[Q^{u}(x)]d\mu(x)>(1-\sqrt{\delta})^{2}=1-\delta^{\prime},

for some
\delta^{\prime}=\delta^{\prime}(\delta)>0
tending to zero with
\delta
.

Now let
L>T_{1}
and consider the space
(I_{L},m)
, for the set
I_{L}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}[0,L]\cap{\mathbb{N}}
endowed with the counting measure that we denote by
m
. Let
F\subset\Omega\times I_{L}
be the set of pairs
(\omega,\ell)
such that
\omega\in B
and
\ell\in E(\omega)
. It follows from Lemma
11.5
that for all
\omega\in B
,
m[E(\omega)]>1-r\delta
. Hence

(\nu\times m)(F)=\int_{B}m[E(\omega)]\,d\nu(\omega)>(1-r\delta)(1-\delta^{\prime})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}1-\delta^{\prime\prime},

for some
\delta^{\prime\prime}=\delta^{\prime\prime}(\delta)
that tends to zero with
\delta
.

On the other hand set
B(\ell)=\{\omega\in B:\ell\in E(\omega)\}
so we have

F=\{(\omega,\ell)\in\Omega\times I_{L}:\,\omega\in B(\ell)\}=\bigcup_{\ell\in I_{L}}B(\ell)\times\{\ell\}.

Applying Fubini’s theorem we get

\displaystyle(\nu\times m)(F)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\int_{I_{L}}\textbf{1}_{F}(\omega,\ell)\,dm(\ell)\,d\nu(\omega)=\int_{I_{L}}\int_{\Omega}\textbf{1}_{F}(\omega,\ell)\,d\nu(\omega)\,dm(\ell)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{I_{L}}\nu(B(\ell))\,dm(\ell).

It follows from Lemma
11.1
that the set of integers
{\mathcal{D}}_{L}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}\{\ell\in I_{L}:\nu(B(\ell))>1-\sqrt{\delta^{\prime\prime}}\}
has measure
>1-\sqrt{\delta^{\prime\prime}}
for
m
. Note that

\nu(B(\ell))=\int_{K_{1}}\mu^{u}_{x}[Q^{u}(x,\ell)]\,d\mu(x),

hence, another application of Lemma
11.1
yields
\mu\{x\in K_{1}:\mu^{u}_{x}[Q^{u}(x,\ell)]>1-\eta\}>1-\eta
where
\eta\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}\sqrt[4]{\delta^{\prime\prime}}
is the function we were looking for. Now since by definition
\{x\in K_{1}:\mu^{u}_{x}[Q^{u}(x,\ell)]>1-\eta\}\mathop{\subset}K(\ell)
we conclude that
\mu[K(\ell)]>1-\eta
. This implies that
{\mathcal{D}}_{L}\mathop{\subset}{\mathcal{D}}\cap[0,L]
where
{\mathcal{D}}
is the set defined in (
75
). We deduce that
\#({\mathcal{D}}\cap[0,L])>(1-\eta)L
as claimed.
∎

11.3.
Construction of matched
Y
-configurations

We are now ready to finish the proof of Lemma
10.9
, and thus that of our main Theorem. We will build arbitrarily long pairs of good and matched
Y
-configurations.

Let
K_{0}
be a compact set included inside the Lusin set
{\mathcal{L}}
defined in §
8.1.2
of measure
\mu(K_{0})>1-\delta
.

We consider the measurable set

K=(K_{0})^{\circ},

so points of
K
are
(\delta,T)
-recurrent in
K_{0}
for some
T=T(\delta)
, and
\mu(K)>1-\delta
. We apply the results of §
11.2.1
to
K
. They yield a set
{\mathcal{D}}\mathop{\subset}{\mathbb{N}}
as defined in (
74
) and Proposition
11.6
. In particular, by definition,
{\mathcal{D}}
has density
>1-\eta
in
[0,L]
for
L
large enough and for every
\ell\in{\mathcal{D}}
,
\mu[K(\ell)]>1-\eta
(where
\eta=\eta(\delta)<1
).

11.3.1.
Constructing the tails

Recall that for
x,y\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
and
\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}
we let
x_{-\ell}
and
y_{-\ell}
denote
f^{-\ell}(x)
and
f^{-\ell}(y)
respectively.

Lemma 11.7
.

For every
\ell\in{\mathcal{D}}
, there exists
K^{\prime}(\ell)\mathop{\subset}K(\ell)
such that
\mu[K^{\prime}(\ell)]>1-2\sqrt{\eta}
and for every
x\in K^{\prime}(\ell)
, there exists
y\in K(\ell)\cap\xi^{s}(x)
such that

\alpha^{s}(x_{-\ell},y_{-\ell})>c(\eta),

where
c(\eta)
is the constant introduced in Lemma
11.2
.

Proof.

We apply Lemma
11.2
to
A=f^{-\ell}(K(\ell))
which has measure
\mu(K(\ell))>1-\eta
. Then we can define
K^{\prime}(\ell)=f^{\ell}(A^{\prime})
where
A^{\prime}
is the set provided by that lemma. It is clear that this set satisfies the desired properties (recall that
\xi^{s}
is decreasing).
∎

11.3.2.
Constructing the quadrilaterals: the matching argument

For
x\in K^{\prime}(\ell)
, so
\xi^{u}(x)
is an interval of size
r(x)
, we set

(76)

I^{u}(x)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}\left\{z\in\xi^{u}(x):\,d^{u}(x,z)>c(\eta),\,\,\,\textrm{and}\,\,\,d^{u}(z,\partial\xi^{u}(x))>c(\eta)\right\}.

Recall that
0<c(\eta)\leq r_{0}\eta\leq|\xi^{u}(x)|
, hence
|I^{u}(x)|>(1-4\eta)|\xi^{u}(x)|
. Note furthermore that if
y\in{\mathcal{L}}
is close enough to
x
then
H^{cs}_{x,y}(I^{u}(x))\mathop{\subset}\xi^{u}(y)
(we use here that
r(y)=|\xi^{u}(y)|
is uniformly continuous in
{\mathcal{L}}
and that center-stable holonomy maps converge uniformly to the identity as
x
tends to
y
).

Since
\mu^{u}_{x}
is absolutely continuous continuous with respect to the inner Lebesgue length
|.|
of
{\mathcal{W}}^{u}(x)
, with a uniform bound
\beta
on the densities (recall Lemma
2.22
) it follows that

\frac{|Q^{u}(x,\ell)|}{|\xi^{u}(x)|}>1-\beta\eta,

Proposition 11.8

(Matching argument)
.

There exists
L_{0}\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that for every
\ell\geq L_{0}
and
x\in K^{\prime}(\ell)
, if
y\in K(\ell)\cap\xi^{s}(x)
is given by Lemma
11.7
then there exist
x^{u}\in\xi^{u}(x)
and
y^{u}\in\xi^{u}(y)
such that the following properties hold

(1)

d_{u}(x,x^{u})>c(\eta)
;

(2)

y^{u}=H^{cs}_{x,y}(x^{u})
;

(3)

J(x^{u},\varepsilon,\ell)\cap Q^{u}(x,\ell)\neq\emptyset
and
J(y^{u},\varepsilon,\ell)\cap Q^{u}(y,\ell)\neq\emptyset
.

The next paragraphs are devoted to the proof of this proposition.

11.3.3.
Good matching

Let
x,y\in{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
be points verifying Lemma
11.7
. Note that the lengths of dynamical balls
|J(z)|=|J(z,\varepsilon,\ell)|
tend uniformly to
0
as
\ell\to\infty
. Hence we may suppose that
\ell
is large enough so that for every
z\in I^{u}(x)
,
J(z)\mathop{\subset}\xi^{u}(x)
and
J(H^{cs}_{x,y}(z))\mathop{\subset}\xi^{u}(y).

Let
x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}\in I^{u}(x)\mathop{\subset}\xi^{u}(x)
and
J_{1},\ldots,J_{k}\mathop{\subset}{\mathcal{W}}^{u}(x)
be the dynamical balls defined
by

J_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}J(x_{i},\varepsilon,\ell)\mathop{\subset}\xi^{u}(x).

We may assume that the following properties are satisfied

(1)

for every
i\geq 1
,
d(x_{i},x)>c
;

(2)

I^{u}(x)\mathop{\subset}\bigcup_{i=1}^{k}J_{i}
;

(3)

for every
z\in I^{u}(x)
,
\#\{i:z\in J_{i}\}\leq 2
.

Set

y_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}H^{cs}_{x,y}(x_{i})\in\xi^{u}(x),\,\,\,\,\,\,\text{and}\,\,\,\,\,\,J_{i}^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}J(y_{i},\varepsilon,\ell)\mathop{\subset}\xi^{u}(x).

The dynamical balls
J_{i}
and
J_{i}^{\prime}
are matched. In particular Corollary
10.6
implies that

\kappa_{4}^{-1}|J_{i}|\leq|J_{i}^{\prime}|\leq\kappa_{4}|J_{i}|.

Set

\tau_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}\tau(x,x_{i},\varepsilon,\ell),\,\,\,\,\,\,\text{and}\,\,\,\,\,\,\tau_{i}^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}\tau(y,y_{i},\varepsilon,\ell),

so
|\tau_{i}-\tau_{i}^{\prime}|<T_{0}
for all
i
.

Lemma 11.9

(Control of overlap)
.

There exists
m=m(f)\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that for every
z\in\xi^{u}(y)

\#\{i:z\in J_{i}^{\prime}\}\leq m.

Proof.

Let
J^{\prime}_{i_{1}},\ldots,J^{\prime}_{i_{m}}
be the dynamical balls in
{\mathcal{W}}^{u}(y)
that contain
z
. Note that for every
j
,
|\tau^{\prime}_{i_{j}}-\tau^{\prime}_{z}|\leq T_{0}<T_{1}
(see Lemma
10.3
). Let

J\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}\bigcup_{j=1}^{m}J_{i_{j}},\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,J^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}\bigcup_{j=1}^{m}J_{i_{j}}^{\prime},\,\,\,\,\,\,\text{and}\,\,\,\,\,\,\tau^{\ast}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}\min_{j=1,\ldots m}\{\tau_{i_{j}},\tau_{i_{j}}^{\prime}\}.

We claim that the oscillations inside the set
\{\tau_{i_{1}},\dots,\tau_{i_{m}},\tau^{\prime}_{i_{1}},\dots,\tau^{\prime}_{i_{m}}\}
are less than
T_{1}
.
Indeed, by Lemma
9.4
, for every
j
we have that
|\tau_{i_{j}}-\tau^{\prime}_{i_{j}}|<T_{0}
. Also, by Lemma
10.3
we have
|\tau^{\prime}_{i_{j}}-\tau(y,z,\varepsilon,\ell)|<T_{0}
and thus
|\tau^{\prime}_{i_{j}}-\tau^{\prime}_{i_{k}}|<2T_{0}
and
|\tau_{i_{j}}-\tau_{i_{k}}|<4T_{0}
. We conclude that for every
\alpha\in\{\tau_{i_{1}},\dots,\tau_{i_{m}},\tau^{\prime}_{i_{1}},\dots,\tau^{\prime}_{i_{m}}\}
it holds

(77)

|\alpha-\tau^{*}|<4T_{0},

which proves our claim due to our choice of
T_{1}
in (
70
)

On the one hand
f^{\tau_{\ast}}(z)
belongs to the intersection of intervals
f^{\tau_{\ast}}(J_{i}^{\prime})
, which have length
\leq 2
, so
|f^{\tau_{\ast}}(J^{\prime})|\leq 4
.

With this claim established we can bound from below the length
|f^{\tau^{*}}(J)|
. In fact, because at most two intervals
J_{i}
can overlap at the same time, we can estimate

\big|f^{\tau^{\ast}}(J)\big|=\left|\bigcup_{j=1}^{m}f^{\tau^{\ast}}(J_{i_{j}})\right|\geq\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{m}|f^{\tau^{\ast}}(J_{i_{j}})|.

Now, by definition of the unstable dynamical ball we have
|f^{\tau_{i_{j}}}(J_{i_{j}})|=2
and by (
77
)
|\tau^{*}-\tau_{i_{j}}|<T_{1}
for every
j=1,\dots,m
. Therefore,

|f^{\tau^{*}}(J_{i_{j}})|\geq\frac{2}{\|Df^{-T_{1}}\|}.

Combining the last two inequalities one deduces that

|f^{\tau^{\ast}}(J)|\geq\frac{m}{\|Df^{-T_{1}}\|}.

This implies in particular that there exist two points of
f^{\tau_{\ast}}(J)
that are distant of at least
\frac{m}{2\|Df^{-T_{1}}\|}
. Since such a point is at distance
\leq 1
of some
f^{\tau_{\ast}}(x_{i})
we deduce that there exists
j,l
such that

d(f^{\tau_{\ast}}(x_{i_{j}}),f^{\tau_{\ast}}(x_{i_{l}}))\geq\frac{m}{2\|Df^{-T_{1}}\|}-2.

On the one hand
d(f^{\tau^{\ast}}(y_{i_{j}}),f^{\tau^{\ast}}(y_{i_{l}}))\leq 2
(this follows from the definition of
\tau^{\ast}
and the fact that
f^{\tau^{\ast}}(z)\in{\mathcal{W}}^{u}_{1}(f^{\tau^{\ast}}(y_{i_{j}}))\cap{\mathcal{W}}^{u}_{1}(f^{\tau^{\ast}}(y_{i_{l}}))
). On the other hand if
\ell
is large enough and
\varepsilon
small enough we may ask
d(f^{\tau^{\ast}}(y_{i_{j}}),f^{\tau^{\ast}}(x_{i_{j}}))\leq\rho_{0}
(where
\rho_{0}
is the constant of Lemma
2.8
). Hence the Hölder regularity of center-stable holonomies provided by Lemma
2.8
yields

\displaystyle\frac{m}{2\|Df^{-T_{1}}\|}-2

\displaystyle\leq d(f^{\tau_{\ast}}(x_{i_{j}}),f^{\tau_{\ast}}(x_{i_{l}}))

\displaystyle\leq C^{cs}d(f^{\tau_{\ast}}(y_{i_{j}}),f^{\tau_{\ast}}(y_{i_{l}}))^{\theta^{cs}}

\displaystyle\leq C^{cs}2^{\theta^{cs}}.

We obtain

m\leq(C^{cs}2^{1+\theta^{cs}}+4)\|Df^{-T_{1}}\|.

This upper bound only depends on
f
, which concludes the proof.
∎

11.3.4.
Proof of Proposition
11.8

We are now ready to give a proof of Proposition
11.8
which is a modification of that of
[
15
, Lemma 12.8]
.

Set

\mathcal{I}^{x}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}\left\{i\in\{1,\ldots,k\}:J_{i}\cap Q^{u}(x,\ell)\neq\emptyset\right\},\,\,\,\,\,\,\text{}\,\,\,\,\,\,\mathcal{J}^{x}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}\{1,\ldots,k\}\setminus\mathcal{I}^{x},

and

\mathcal{I}^{y}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}\left\{i\in\{1,\ldots,k\}:J_{i}^{\prime}\cap Q^{u}(y,\ell)\neq\emptyset\right\},\,\,\,\,\,\,\text{and}\,\,\,\,\,\,\mathcal{J}^{y}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}\{1,\ldots,k\}\setminus\mathcal{I}^{y}.

Let

Q\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm def.}}{{=}}\{z\in Q^{u}(x,\ell):\forall\,i,(z\in J_{i}\Rightarrow i\in\mathcal{I}^{y})\}.

It suffices to show that
Q\cap I^{u}(x)\neq\emptyset
.

Note that

(Q^{u}(x,\ell)\cap I^{u}(x))\setminus Q\mathop{\subset}\bigcup_{i\in\mathcal{I}^{x}\cap\mathcal{J}^{y}}Q^{u}(x,\ell)\cap J_{i}

so (supposing that
\ell
is large enough so that
|\xi^{u}(y)|\leq 2|\xi^{u}(x)|
)

\displaystyle|(Q^{u}(x,\ell)\cap I^{u}(x))\setminus Q|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}^{x}\cap\mathcal{J}^{y}}|Q^{u}(x,\ell)\cap J_{i}|\leq\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}^{x}\cap\mathcal{J}^{y}}|J_{i}|\leq\kappa_{4}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{J}^{y}}|J_{i}^{\prime}|

\displaystyle\leq\kappa_{4}m\left|\bigcup_{i\in\mathcal{J}^{y}}J_{i}^{\prime}\right|\leq\kappa_{4}m|\xi^{u}(y)\setminus Q^{u}(y,\ell)|

\displaystyle\leq\kappa_{4}m\beta\eta|\xi^{u}(y)|\leq 2\kappa_{4}m\beta\eta|\xi^{u}(x)|.

Hence there exists
\beta^{\prime}=\beta^{\prime}(f)
such that

|Q\cap I^{u}(x)|\geq|Q^{u}(x,\ell)\cap I^{u}(x)|-|(Q^{u}(x,\ell)\cap I^{u}(x))\setminus Q|\geq(1-\beta^{\prime}\eta)|\xi^{u}(x)|>0,

as soon as
\eta<\frac{1}{\beta^{\prime}}
.

Now let
a\in Q
. There exists
i
such that
d(x,x_{i})>c
,
a\in J(x_{i},\varepsilon,\ell)
and
J(y_{i},\varepsilon,\ell)\cap Q^{u}(y,\ell)\neq\emptyset
where
y_{i}=H^{cs}_{x,y}(x_{i})
: the proof of the proposition is over.
\square

11.3.5.
The synchronization

We are now ready to finish the proof of Lemma
10.9
. Let
\ell\in{\mathcal{D}}
such that
\ell\geq L_{0}
. Let
x\in K^{\prime}(\ell)
and
y\in K(\ell)\cap\xi^{s}(x)
be given by Lemma
11.7
. Let
x^{u}\in\xi^{u}(x)
and
y^{u}\in\xi^{u}(y)
be given by Proposition
11.8
: there exist
a\in J(x^{u},\varepsilon,\ell)\cap Q^{u}(x,\ell)
and
b\in J(x^{u},\varepsilon,\ell)\cap Q^{u}(y,\ell)
.

Lemma 11.10
.

There exists
T>0
independent of
\ell
and
\tau,t>0
such that

(1)

|\tau-\tau(x,x^{u},\varepsilon,\ell)|\leq T
and
|t-t(x,x^{u},\varepsilon,\ell)|\leq T
;

(2)

f^{\tau}(a),f^{t}(x),f^{\tau}(b),f^{t}(y)\in K_{0}
.

It follows from Lemma
11.10
that for
\ell\in{\mathcal{D}}
larger than
L_{0}
, the
Y
-configurations
X=X(x,x^{u},\ell)
and
Y=Y(y,y^{u},\ell)
are
(K_{0},C,T)
-matched. This proves Lemma
10.9
.

Proof.

Set

\tau(\ell)=\tau(x,a,\varepsilon,\ell),t(\ell)=t(x,a,\varepsilon,\ell),\tau^{\prime}(\ell)=\tau(y,b,\varepsilon,\ell),t^{\prime}(\ell)=t(y,b,\varepsilon,\ell).

By construction
(x,x^{u},y,y^{u})
is a
(C,\ell)
-quadrilateral and
a\in J(x^{u})
,
b\in J(y^{u})
. Combining the results of Lemmas
9.4
and
10.3
we obtain

|\tau(\ell)-\tau(x,x^{u},\varepsilon,\ell)|<T_{0},\quad|t(\ell)-t(x,x^{u},\varepsilon,\ell)|<T_{0}

as well as

|\tau^{\prime}(\ell)-\tau(x,x^{u},\varepsilon,\ell)|<2T_{0},\quad|t^{\prime}(\ell)-t(x,x^{u},\varepsilon,\ell)|<2T_{0},

and consequently

|\tau^{\prime}(\ell)-\tau(\ell)|<3T_{0},\quad|t^{\prime}(\ell)-t(\ell)|<3T_{0},

where
T_{0}=T_{0}(\delta)
.

Proposition
11.8
implies that

f^{\tau(\ell)}(a),f^{t(\ell)}(x),f^{\tau^{\prime}(\ell)}(b),f^{t^{\prime}(\ell)}(y)\in K,

which means that these points are
(\delta,T)
-recurrent inside the compact set
K_{0}
. Now we might have
\tau(\ell)\neq\tau^{\prime}(\ell)
or
t(\ell)\neq t^{\prime}(\ell)
. Assume for example that
t(\ell)<t^{\prime}(\ell)
. Let
T^{\prime}=T^{\prime}(\delta)>\max(T,T_{0})
such that

\frac{T_{0}}{T^{\prime}}<\delta,

in such a way that
[t(\ell),t^{\prime}(\ell)]
has density
<3\delta
inside
[t(\ell),t^{\prime}(\ell)+T^{\prime}]
. Since
T^{\prime}>T
, this implies that

\left\{k\in[t(\ell),t^{\prime}(\ell)+T^{\prime}]:f^{k}(x)\in K_{0}\right\}\cap[t^{\prime}(\ell),t^{\prime}(\ell)+T^{\prime}]

has density
>1-4\delta
in
[t^{\prime}(\ell),t^{\prime}(\ell)+T^{\prime}]
. On the other hand the set

\left\{k\in[t^{\prime}(\ell),t^{\prime}(\ell)+T^{\prime}]:f^{k}(y)\in K_{0}\right\}

has density
>1-\delta
inside
[t^{\prime}(\ell),t^{\prime}(\ell)+T^{\prime}]
. Therefore these two sets must intersect (as soon as
\delta<1/5
) so there exists
t\in[t^{\prime}(\ell),t^{\prime}(\ell)+T^{\prime}]
such that
f^{t}(x),f^{t}(y)\in K_{0}
. This integer
t
satisfies

|t-t(x,x^{u},\varepsilon,\ell)|\leq|t-t^{\prime}(\ell)|+|t^{\prime}(\ell)-t(x,x^{u},\varepsilon,\ell)|<T^{\prime}+2T_{0}

so
T=T^{\prime}+2T_{0}
is the desired constant. The same argument can be reproduced to treat
a
and
b
and find the number
\tau
.
∎

11.3.6.
Postliminary: choice of
\delta_{0}
and
\delta

Let us list the requirements we made on the constant
\delta_{0}
and
\delta=3\delta_{0}
. We needed to require

\delta<\min\big(\frac{1}{5},\frac{1}{r}\big),

where
r
only depends on
f
(it is defined in Lemma
11.5
).

We defined in Proposition
11.6
a function
\eta(\delta)
(of the order of
\delta^{1/4}
) that depends only on
a
and required that

\eta<\min\big(\frac{1}{4},\frac{1}{\beta},\frac{1}{\beta^{\prime}}\big),

where
\beta
is the constant of Lemma
2.22
and
\beta^{\prime}
appears in §
11.3.4
.

12.
Remarks on Gogolev-Kolmogorov-Maimon’s perturbations

In
[
19
]
the authors consider two families of perturbations of the linear Anosov diffeomorphism
f_{0}\colon{\mathbb{T}^{3}}\to{\mathbb{T}^{3}}
induced by the matrix

A=\begin{bmatrix}2&1&0\\
1&2&1\\
0&1&1\end{bmatrix}.

The
dissipative family
is given

f_{D,\varepsilon}\begin{bmatrix}x\\
y\\
z\end{bmatrix}=A\begin{bmatrix}x\\
y\\
z\end{bmatrix}+\frac{\varepsilon}{2\pi}\begin{bmatrix}\sin(2\pi x)\\
0\\
0\end{bmatrix}\,\,\,\,\,\text{mod}(1).

The Jacobian at the fixed point of
f_{D,\varepsilon}
is given by

\text{Jac}f_{D,\varepsilon}\begin{bmatrix}0\\
0\\
0\end{bmatrix}=\begin{vmatrix}2+\varepsilon&1&0\\
1&2&1\\
0&1&1\end{vmatrix}=1+\varepsilon,

so
f_{D,\varepsilon}
is dissipative: it does not preserve any volume.

The
conservative family
is given by

f_{C,\varepsilon}\begin{bmatrix}x\\
y\\
z\end{bmatrix}=A\begin{bmatrix}x\\
y\\
z\end{bmatrix}+\frac{\varepsilon}{2\pi}\begin{bmatrix}\sin(2\pi x)\\
\sin(2\pi x)\\
0\end{bmatrix}\,\,\,\,\,\text{mod}(1).

The Jacobian at any point of
f_{C,\varepsilon}
is given by

\text{Jac}f_{C,\varepsilon}\begin{bmatrix}x\\
y\\
z\end{bmatrix}=\begin{vmatrix}2+\varepsilon\cos(2\pi x)&1&0\\
1+\varepsilon\cos(2\pi x)&2&1\\
0&1&1\end{vmatrix}=1.

We apply Gan-Shi’s criterion for joint integrability obtained in
[
17
]
in order to prove that these two families of perturbations are accessible and hence Theorem
A
applied to both of them gives the following theorem.

Theorem 12.1
.

If
|\varepsilon|>0
is small enough
f_{D,\varepsilon}
or
f_{C,\varepsilon}
are accessible. Hence any fully supported ergodic
u
-Gibbs measure for
f_{D,\varepsilon}
or
f_{C,\varepsilon}
is SRB (this is Lebesgue in the conservative case).

Proof.

In our context, Gan-Shi’s criterion for accessibility is the following.
A
C^{1+\alpha}
difeomorphism
f

C^{1}
-close to
f_{0}
is not accessible if and only if its central Lyapunov exponents at every perdiodic points coincide with those of
f_{0}
.

In order to prove the accessibility of
f_{D,\varepsilon}
and
f_{C,\varepsilon}
it is enough to prove that the central Lyapunov exponent at the fixed point differ from the one of
A
(which is approximately
1.55
). This can be done as follows. The characteristic polynomial of the differential at the fixed point for the dissipative family is

P_{D,\varepsilon}(X)=\begin{vmatrix}2-X+\varepsilon&1&0\\
1&2-X&1\\
0&1&1-X\end{vmatrix}=-X^{3}+(5+\varepsilon)X^{2}-(6+3\varepsilon)X+1+\varepsilon.

The coefficients of
P_{D,\varepsilon}(X)
vary smoothly with
\varepsilon
and when
\varepsilon=0
, this polynomial has three disctinct roots. So by the implicit function theorem, for small
|\varepsilon|
,
P_{D,\varepsilon}
has three distinct roots
\lambda_{1}(\varepsilon)<\lambda_{2}(\varepsilon)<\lambda_{3}(\varepsilon)
that depend smoothly on
\varepsilon
and satisfy the relations

\begin{cases}\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}\lambda_{3}=1+\varepsilon\\
\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{3}=5+\varepsilon\\
\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{1}\lambda_{3}+\lambda_{2}\lambda_{3}=6+3\varepsilon\end{cases}.

We must have
\lambda_{2}^{\prime}(0)\neq 0
, which yields the accessibility of
f_{D,\varepsilon}
. Indeed, if
\lambda_{2}^{\prime}(0)=0
, then derivating the relations above gives (the functions below are evaluated at
0
)

\begin{cases}\lambda_{2}(\lambda_{1}^{\prime}\lambda_{3}+\lambda_{1}\lambda_{3}^{\prime})=1\\
\lambda_{1}^{\prime}+\lambda_{3}^{\prime}=1\\
\lambda_{2}(\lambda_{1}^{\prime}+\lambda_{3}^{\prime})+\lambda_{1}^{\prime}\lambda_{3}+\lambda_{1}\lambda_{3}^{\prime}=3\end{cases}.

Combining the above one finds
\lambda_{2}+1/\lambda_{2}=3
, which contradicts
\lambda_{2}\simeq 1.55
(so
\lambda_{2}+1/\lambda_{2}\simeq 2.20
).

The same computation works for the conservative family. Indeed, the characteristic polynomial of the differential at the fixed point for this family is

P_{C,\varepsilon}(X)=\begin{vmatrix}2-X+\varepsilon&1&0\\
1+\varepsilon&2-X&1\\
0&1&1-X\end{vmatrix}=-X^{3}+(5+\varepsilon)X^{2}-(6+2\varepsilon)X+1.

So the relations between roots and coefficients give

\begin{cases}\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}\lambda_{3}=1\\
\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{3}=5+\varepsilon\\
\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{1}\lambda_{3}+\lambda_{2}\lambda_{3}=6+2\varepsilon\end{cases}.

The same argument as before provides
\lambda_{2}^{\prime}(0)\neq 0
(the calculations are left to the reader), which yields the accessibility of
f_{C,\varepsilon}
.
∎
